# ECHOES OF THE SHATTERED OCTAVE
## Book One: *The Silenced Province*

*Complete manuscript · Prologue through Echo I*

---

## PROLOGUE
### The Cracked Bell

> *Q. What is holiness?*
> *A. One note, held without end.*
> *Q. What is sin?*
> *A. The second note.*
>
> — Catechism of the Pure Note

*Sol-Luminary. The Siphon Foundry at the foot of the Dawning Spire.*
*The three hundredth sunrise of the Rite of Pure Unison. 412 SR.*

---

On the three hundredth morning of the Rite, Solenne Aubade woke already singing.

She had stopped noticing somewhere around the eightieth sunrise, when the wire took over. That was what the Tuning Men had promised the day they threaded it. *After a while, girl, you won't have to try. The copper remembers for you. You breathe; it sings.* They had said it kindly, the way a groom tells a horse the bit will stop hurting once the mouth goes numb.

The mouth had not gone numb. But she no longer had to try.

She lay on her pallet in the choir-loft with her eyes shut and listened to herself: the note leaving her on every breath, thin and exact, five hundred and twenty-eight beats to the second, the holy number, the Light's own pitch. She counted the voices around her in the dark. Thirty-nine. There were supposed to be forty. Ilse had died on the risers on the two hundred and eleventh morning, still singing, and the Inquisitor had had her carried out without the note so much as wavering, and the line had closed over the gap the way water closes over a dropped stone.

At night the loft sounded like a hive. Thirty-nine sleeping girls, humming. Solenne had lain awake through the early weeks listening to it, sick with it, and then one night she realized she could no longer remember what the loft had sounded like before, and after that she slept.

When she had tried to speak (she did not anymore, but in the first weeks she had tried) the words came out on the same pitch, every syllable pressed flat onto the one note like a flower in a book. You could say *I love you* or *I am dying* or *please*, and the wire made them all the same prayer. So the girls had built a hand-speech instead, small and quick, done under the hems of their white shifts where the Flambeaux could not see. Solenne's hands had said a great many things in three hundred days.

Her mouth had said one.

---

The Siphon Foundry had grown around the foot of the Dawning Spire the way a scab grows around a nail.

It was a cathedral of black iron, ribbed and buttressed, thrown up against the Spire's flank in a single summer by the Consortium of Bores: gantries, catwalks, cable-drums, the long channels in the floor where the glass ran, the tanker bays, the censer platform, the gallery for the nobility, and the risers where the choir stood. Its roof was open. It had to be. There was no roof in the world high enough to close over the thing it had been built against.

The Spire went up out of the mountain, out of the Foundry, out of the clouds, and past the place where the eye gave up. It was white, not the white of marble or of snow but the white of a clean bone, and it was seamless, and it was singing. It had always been singing. Solenne had grown up beneath it, in House Aubade's tall cold rooms on the Upper Tiers, and she had heard it every day of her life without hearing it, the way you do not hear your own blood.

Only on the risers, with her own voice pinned to its pitch, had she begun to hear it properly.

The Church taught that the Spire sang one note: the Pure Note, the Law of Light, five hundred and twenty-eight. The Church was wrong. Under the choir, under the grinding of the bore, under everything, the Spire sang a chord so wide you could only hear its edges, like standing inside a bell someone had struck before the world began and never stopped striking. And inside the chord, if you listened long enough, there was *grain*. Texture. Something like breath. Once, around the hundred and fortieth sunrise, she had heard something in it catch, the way a voice catches on a word it doesn't want to say, and for days afterward she could not stop thinking a thought she had no right to think.

*It sounds tired.*

The bore did not sound tired. The bore sounded like the end of the world being ground very slowly out of stone. The Carrow Bore was thirty storeys of black iron and runework, lowered down the Spire's flank on chains thicker than a man's body. It turned all day and all night like a mill-wheel, and at its tip a crown of glass teeth bit into the white stone and made it bleed.

The blood was blue. Not the blue of the sky, or of a vein, but the blue at the very bottom of a flame. Aether-Glass, running liquid out of the wound, down the cut channels in the Foundry floor and into iron tankers that men in leather aprons wheeled away every hour. The Synod said the glass was surplus: the Light's generosity, overflowing.

Solenne had watched it come out of the Spire for three hundred days. It did not overflow. It came in pulses.

Like something with a heartbeat.

---

The choir's work was to hold the note still.

That was the Consortium's theory, and the Church's blessing on the theory, and the reason forty girls of the Sky Choirs had been brought down from the heights with copper in their throats. A Spire could not simply be drilled. It rang, and a ringing thing shatters under a bit. But if its note were held perfectly steady from outside, if a unison of human voices sang its own pitch back to it without a tremor, it would stand still for the bore the way a patient stands still for a surgeon.

"You are the hand that steadies the patient," Inquisitor Ignatius had told them on the first morning, walking the line of risers with his hands folded. "Holy daughters. You hold the Light still so that it may be relieved of its excess."

He stood on the censer platform above the choir every dawn, a tall man, young for his rank, with a face like a statue someone had loved too hard, in robes the red of a banked coal, and he swung the great brass censer on its chain, back and forth, back and forth. The chain creaked at the top of each arc, and that creak was the metronome of the Rite. Solenne had heard it in her sleep for three hundred nights. She would hear it, she suspected, for the rest of her life.

He believed. That was the worst thing she could say about him, and she'd had three hundred days to find worse.

Above him, behind a rail of gilded iron, was the gallery, and in the gallery, every morning, in white, stood her sister.

Seraphina was sixteen. The Church had chosen them both on the same day, in the same hall, with their mother weeping proudly in the front pew: Seraphina for the Sun-Blade, Solenne for the wire. One daughter to carry the Light, one to hold it still. The Blade lay on a cushion of cloth-of-gold beside Seraphina's knee, waiting for the end of the Rite, a long pale sword the Church had carved from a splinter of the Spire itself. Every morning Seraphina stood beside it through the Morning Swell with her eyes lifted to the Spire and her face as clean and certain as a candle.

And every morning, under the hem of her shift, Solenne's hands said: *Look at me.*

And every morning her sister did not.

---

There was one other note in the Foundry.

It belonged to the engineer.

He came up the gantry every dawn with the Tuning Men to calibrate the bore: a young man, not yet thirty, sleeves rolled to the elbow, soot in the creases of his knuckles, a long steel tuning fork carried the way other men carry a knife. When he worked, when he thought no one could hear him under the roar, he hummed.

Low. So low it was nearly under the floor. A note like wet earth, like a root drinking. A *Reach* note, her mother would have said, with a sniff; the Reach, where they still sang to their apple trees. It was nowhere near five hundred and twenty-eight. It was, strictly speaking, a second note, and in the Foundry of the Pure Unison a second note was very nearly a sin.

Solenne had come to wait for it the way prisoners wait for the hour the sun touches their window.

He had looked at her once. On the ninth morning, when the wire was still new and she was bleeding into the collar of her shift, he had come down from the gantry to check the resonance at the risers, and his eyes had gone to her throat, and he had flinched. Not looked away. *Flinched*, the way you flinch from a stove. Then he had gone back up the gantry and never looked at the risers again.

In three hundred days, he was the only one who ever flinched.

---

"Today," said Inquisitor Ignatius, "the bore reaches the casing."

Dawn was coming up behind the Spire the way it always did in Sol-Luminary, not rising so much as *arriving*, the whole white length of the tower catching fire from the top down. The Foundry smelled of hot iron and rosin and the sweetish mineral stink of raw glass. On the gantry the Tuning Men were calling numbers to one another. The engineer stood at the calibration rail with his fork in his fist.

"Three hundred sunrises," said the Inquisitor. His voice was trained for cathedrals; it carried without seeming to try. "Three hundred sunrises you have held the Light for us, daughters, and today the Light will give us its heart. Sing as you have never sung." He lifted the censer. "One note."

The chain creaked.

"One law."

Creak.

"One hand."

And the Foundry answered him with the only word it had, which was the note.

The Morning Swell began. Thirty-nine voices rose on the one pitch until the air on the risers went thick and bright with it, until Solenne could feel it in her teeth, in the bones behind her eyes, in the copper ring of the tuning collar around her throat. On the gantry the Tuning Men called the match, *five-twenty-eight, holding, five-twenty-eight, bore to five-twenty-eight*, and the Carrow Bore dropped the last span on its chains with a sound like a mountain clearing its throat, and the crown of glass teeth went into the Spire, and the Spire bled.

And underneath them, its chord changed.

It did not grow louder. It grew *closer*. That was the only way she would ever find to say it, afterward, in the dark. The way a room changes when someone in it stops pretending to be asleep. The vast chord that had been sounding since before there was a world to hear it drew in, and in, narrowing, until it was nearly a single note, until it was nearly *their* note, and Solenne understood with her whole body that it was listening.

To thirty-nine girls holding its pitch.

Holding it *for* it.

And it asked.

Not in words. The Spire had no words; she would have sworn to that before this morning, and she would swear to it after. But you do not need words to know you have been asked a question. The whole Foundry *was* a question. The air was. The copper in her throat was. It went through her the way your own name goes through you when it is called across a crowded square, and it was so old, the question, so worn smooth with waiting, that it had calluses.

*Is it time?*

She did not know how she knew what it meant. She knew what it meant.

*Is it time? Have you come? Are you, at last, are you singing it for me?*

On the gantry, the engineer's tuning fork fell out of his hand.

She heard it hit the iron, a clean, bright, useless note, and she saw his face, and she knew that he had heard it too.

"*Stop the bore.*" His voice cracked straight across the Swell. A Reach voice, too loud. "Stop — lift it, lift it *now*, it's not — Inquisitor, it's *letting go*—"

Inquisitor Ignatius did not look at him.

"Hold the note," he said.

And they held it.

Gods help them all, they held it. Thirty-nine voices, perfect, pure, not a tremor, because three hundred days of copper had made it impossible to do anything else, and the Spire heard its own note sung back to it, steady as stone, by someone other than itself, for the first time in ten thousand years.

It made the only mistake a thing that tired could make.

It believed them.

It let go.

---

The chord stopped.

For the length of a heartbeat there was no sound in the Foundry but thirty-nine girls, and even that seemed small, because what had stopped was not a sound. It was the floor under all sound. Solenne had never heard silence before. No one in Aethelgard ever had. It was not quiet. Quiet is a sound. This was the absence of the thing that let sound exist, and she felt it in her inner ear as a falling, as if the whole Foundry had dropped an inch.

Then the light came down the Spire.

It came the way water comes when a dam goes, all at once and from everywhere, pouring off the white stone in sheets. It was the most beautiful thing she had ever seen for exactly as long as it took to reach the floor.

Where it touched, it took.

The iron tankers went first. They did not melt. They went *pale*, the black of the iron washing out to grey, to white, to the white of a dream you can't quite remember, and then they were simply less. Thinner. As if someone were erasing them from the edges in, until nothing was left where they had stood but a bright place on the floor. A Tuning Man at the foot of the gantry put up his hands against it, and Solenne watched his hands go white, and then clear, and then she could see the bones in them, and then she could not see the bones.

It did not burn. That was the thing she would never be able to make anyone understand. There was no heat, no smoke, and at first no screaming, because the people it touched were too busy being taken apart to scream. It was hungry. That was all. For ten thousand years, light had given. Now nothing was holding its law, and it did the only other thing it knew how to do.

It took everything back.

It came for the risers last, and it came for the throats first, because the copper was bright and bright things called to it, and because, she understood later, the throats were where the song had been, and it was looking for the song. It went into Hesper's mouth like a hand into a glove. It went into Aude. It went into little Coralie, fifteen, who had stood at Solenne's left shoulder for three hundred days and signed jokes to her under their hems, and Solenne felt the wire in her own throat go hot with it—

—and through the white she saw the engineer.

He had not run. He had come down the gantry ladder in something closer to a fall than a climb, and he had the apprentices, three gantry boys, soot-faced, none of them older than twelve, by their collars, and he was throwing them one by one into the drainage conduit that ran under the tanker floor: a black iron throat with a grate over it, the one place in the Foundry the light had not yet found. Above the bore, the glass reservoir burst. A wall of liquid Aether-Glass came down the channel, blue as the bottom of a flame. He got the bulkhead door over the conduit half-shut and held it, shoulder and left arm braced against a door built to be closed by a winch, while the glass came over him.

She watched it pour over the arm, and she watched the arm stop being an arm. It went blue and still and clear to the shoulder, like something stopped in amber, and he screamed. Even screaming, he screamed on his note, the low one, the Reach one, the wet-earth note, and Solenne thought, stupidly, with the light in her mouth: *I'll never hear that again.*

"*Dissonance!*"

The Inquisitor was on his knees on the censer platform with the chain wrapped around his fist. The light had taken the platform's rail and was taking the hem of his robe, and his face was not afraid. It was sure.

"The choir has broken the unison — it is the *choir* — Flambeaux! Purify them! Purify the Rite!"

Along the walls, where they had stood for three hundred days with their fire-staves grounded, the Flambeaux lifted their staves.

In the last instant before the fire reached her, Solenne looked up at the gallery.

Her sister was there.

The light had taken the gilded rail, and the carpets, and the old Canon who had stood at Seraphina's side each morning with his hands folded over his belly. It had not taken Seraphina. She stood in a small circle of untouched color in her white armor, both hands closed on the hilt of the Sun-Blade, and the light parted around her like a river around a stone. As if it knew her. As if it knew what she was holding.

She was looking at the risers now. At last. At Solenne.

Her hands did not open.

*Look at me*, said Solenne's hands, and the fire took the risers.

---

The blast threw her backward, off the top tier, over the glass channels, into the only true dark left in the Foundry.

The wound.

The seam the bore had opened in the Spire's flank was a black crack in the white, and the light would not go into it, because the light had come out of it, and nothing goes back willingly into the place it has escaped from.

She fell.

She fell for a long time. Longer than the Spire was wide, longer than the mountain was deep. She fell out of the light and out of the heat and out of the sound of the Foundry dying, down into a dark that was not the dark of a room at night, or the dark behind closed eyes, or any dark that has another side to it.

And in the dark, the wire in her throat went on singing.

Thin. Exact. Five hundred and twenty-eight beats to the second. The last note in the world.

She could not hear her heart. She could not hear her breath, or the rush of her own falling. There was nothing in the dark to carry a sound but her, and so there was nothing but the note—

—until, far below her, patiently, something opened.

She would never be able to say how she knew it was a mouth.

It took her note in, the way the Spire had. Tasted it. Held it.

And then, carefully, perfectly, pitch for pitch, it sang her note back to her, the way a child repeats the first word it has ever been taught.

---
---

## PART ONE: THE NARROW

---

## CHAPTER ONE
### The Tending Note

> *Voice at the hedge, voice at the door,*
> *answer it once and you'll answer no more.*
> *Too loud brings the hounds, too still brings the Hush,*
> *so murmur, my lamb, in the Narrow. Hush.*
>
> — Oakhaven skipping rhyme

*Oakhaven, the Resonant Reach. Autumn, 419 SR. Seven years after the Fracture.*

---

In Oakhaven, children were born into a whisper.

Lyra Ashgrove knelt at the foot of Ruth Madder's bed with a clay jar pressed to the great tight curve of Ruth's belly and her own lips pressed to the jar's neck, and she hummed.

It was a trick older than the Hedge, older than the Synod, older than the Fracture: a Cantor's trick from the orchard days, for when you wanted a sound to go *into* a body instead of into the air. The jar caught the note and gave it to the skin. Hummed into the open room, the tending note would have been a sound. Hummed into the jar, it was a warmth, a pressure, a hand laid flat where no hand could reach. One hundred and seventy-four beats to the second. The Root note. The note the whole Reach had been tuned to since before anyone thought to count, and the first note her father had taught her, before he taught her to walk.

*Low*, she told the baby through the clay. *Low and slow. Come on, now. Come down.*

The room was small and hot and stuffed with wool. Someone, Coll Madder probably, in the week before, when there had still been time to do something useful with his hands, had packed every crack in the shutters with fleece and hung felt over the door, and the hush-lamp on the chest by the bed whistled its thin reed-whistle in the corner, never loud, never stopping, so the room would not fall silent even if everyone in it did. Old Bettany crouched at Ruth's head with the bite-strap, a strip of boiled leather gone dark with other women's teeth, and whenever the pains came she put it between Ruth's jaws and put her own mouth to Ruth's ear and murmured, *in the Narrow, love, in the Narrow, you can do it in the Narrow*, over and over, like a charm.

Ruth could do it in the Narrow. Lyra had known a great many women who could not.

Outside the door, Coll paced. Lyra could hear his boots and then not hear them, and she knew he was stopping every few steps to lift his hand, palm flat, the way everyone in Oakhaven measured sound now (*a hand's width of voice, no more*), and listening to his wife through the felt to see whether she was keeping under it. There was nothing else he could do. There was nothing else anyone could do, most days, except listen and measure.

The pains came faster. Ruth bit through the strap's soft edge. Bettany said, "Here she comes," in the flat murmur of a woman who had said it three thousand times, and Lyra took her mouth from the jar and set the jar down and put out her hands.

The baby came out blue and slick and perfect, and made no sound.

Nobody breathed.

It was a strange thing, Lyra thought, not for the first time, that the most frightening moment in an Oakhaven birth was now the moment of silence. Before the Fracture, a mother had waited for the first cry as a mercy. Now she waited for it as a sentence. Too loud, and something in the forest beyond the Hedge might lift its head. Too quiet, and — well. Everyone knew about the still ones. The babies who came out without a sound and lay without a sound and were found in the morning grey and cool and *thin*, as if someone had been rubbing them out through the night like chalk from a slate. The Grafters called it infant pallor. The old women called it what it was.

*Too still brings the Hush.*

Lyra lifted the girl — it was a girl — and bent over her, and put her lips to the soft top of the skull, where the bones had not yet closed, and hummed the Root note straight into her.

*There you are. There you are. Come here to me.*

One breath. Two. On the third the baby's face creased, and her small mouth worked, and she made a sound no louder than a kettle beginning to think about boiling.

A perfect sound. A sound exactly the width of a hand.

Ruth Madder wept without making any noise at all, which was a thing Oakhaven women had learned to do, and Bettany took the baby and swaddled her with the swift disrespect of long practice and said, "There. A good Narrow cry. She'll live." Lyra sat back on her heels on the rush-matting and discovered that she was shaking.

Her voice felt scraped. It always did, after. *Every mending borrows*, her father used to say. A Cantor's voice was her body, and whatever she gave out through it came out of the same account.

"You'll stay for tea," Bettany murmured. It was not a question.

"I can't." Lyra got her feet under her. "I promised Nell Carder I'd look at Pim before the Listening Hour."

Bettany's mouth thinned. She had the look of every old woman in Oakhaven who had buried more children in seven years than in the forty before them. "How are his hands?"

"Better," Lyra lied, and went out.

---

Outside, the afternoon was going long and golden, which in Oakhaven meant it was going pale.

Everyone over thirty said the same thing, if you got them talking late enough: that colors had been brighter once. That the leaves of the iron-oaks had turned red in the autumns of their childhood, a *red* red, a red like a shout, and now they turned a kind of rust-pink, like a stain that has been scrubbed. That the sun at noon had once been a thing you could not look at, and now, on a clear day, you could look at it for a long time if you were stubborn. The Church said it was the Cascade, that all things had gone a quarter-tone flat since the Spire of Dawning broke and would come right again with prayer. People nodded, and prayed, and privately checked the reds every autumn.

Lyra walked up Cooper's Row toward the Hollow with her shawl pulled close and the tiredness humming in her ribs.

The iron-oaks were ringing.

It was a small sound: the wind moving through the high branches and the branches knocking together, and instead of the old dry creak of wood on wood, a low, sweet *chime*, like a spoon touched to a bronze bowl. It had started not long after the Consortium's bore went into the quarry and began drawing on the Root. Now the whole town rang faintly whenever the wind got up, and everyone who heard it glanced toward the Hedge, because hounds loved a chime.

The Synod had forbidden anyone to fell the oaks. They were Consortium property now, like the quarry, like the Root under the quarry, like the glass that came out of it.

On the corner by the well, a sapling had come up overnight through the cobbles, a young oak, knee-high, its roots heaving three stones out of the road like loose teeth. That made the third this week. Someone had already been at it with a hand-saw and given up; the stump was sprouting again from the cut, fine white shoots curling out of the wood like the fingers of something waking. Lyra stepped around it without slowing.

That was how you lived in Oakhaven now. You stepped around things.

You stepped around the bakery, where the loaves had started rising past their tins and splitting the ovens, so that the baker's wife punched the dough down every hour through the night and wept with exhaustion into the flour. You stepped around your own fingernails, which grew a quarter-inch between dusk and dawn, so that every household kept a knife by the washbasin. You stepped around the churchyard, where the graves had begun to heave in the spring. Not opening; only *swelling*, the turf rising in long soft mounds over the dead as if they were growing down there. The sexton had started salting every new grave by the barrow-load, and binding the jaws of the dead with linen, and setting a flat stone on each tongue, so that no corpse in Oakhaven could ring.

Lyra had never heard a corpse ring. She knew two people who had. Neither of them would talk about it.

To the north, beyond the roofs, the quarry glowed.

It glowed day and night now, green-gold, the color of new leaves with a lamp behind them. The color of Root-glass. Under Lyra's boots, under the cobbles, under the whole of Oakhaven, she could feel the bore: not a sound so much as an ache in the teeth, a slow deep grinding that never stopped. *The Root being milked*, people called it, and then looked sorry they had said anything. Once a day the tanker-wagons came up the quarry road with their loads of green glass sloshing behind iron grilles and went east toward the Organum, and on either side of every wagon walked a Carillon Legionnaire.

Lyra did not look at the Legionnaires. Nobody did.

They wore bells for helmets, full bronze bells closed at the face, with a grille where a mouth would be, and their armor was the grey of old pewter, and they did not speak. If you stood near one, which you did not, you could hear something inside the bell. A murmur. Like someone talking in their sleep in a room down the hall.

On the wall of the well-house, a new decree had gone up over the old ones, the paste still wet. BY ROYAL ASSENT, it began, as they all did, above a seal of pale green glass pressed with a queen's profile. *Maelis, by the Grace of the Pure Note, Queen of the Reach.* The Synod's decrees always came with her seal and her signature. Queen Maelis had been dead for a hundred and forty years.

Her signature was very steady. People said that was because her hand no longer shook. People said a great many things, very quietly.

---

The Hollow was the lanes behind the tannery, where the ground dipped toward the Flume and the damp came up through the floorboards. It had been a poor district before the Fracture. Now it was where the town kept its burl-sick.

Nobody had decided that. It had happened the way water finds the lowest place. The first ones had gone there because the tannery reek covered the smell of green sap. The rest had gone because the first ones were there, and because the Grafters came to the Hollow on the second Rootday of every month, and it was easier, people said, to have everyone in one place.

Easier for whom, nobody said.

Nell Carder's door was painted blue, the paint flaking, and a hush-lamp whistled in its window. Nell opened it before Lyra could knock. She was a small woman with a big jaw and eyes like bruises, and she had been widowed two winters ago, when her husband Joss saw a pack of hounds coming over the east field toward the children's lane and did the thing you never do.

He shouted.

He shouted *hounds!* and *run!*, and the children ran, and every hound in the pack turned away from the lane and toward the loudest voice in the Reach. They found his boots in the morning. Only the boots.

"He's worse," Nell said. "Since yesterday. I think. I don't know. You look."

Pim was sitting on the hearth-rug, seven years old, sturdy and freckled, in his dead father's shirt cut down to fit, with his hands resting palm-up on his knees the way Lyra had taught him. So they wouldn't touch anything.

So they wouldn't take root.

Lyra knelt in front of him. "Hello, Pim."

"'Lo, Lyra." In the Narrow, perfectly. Oakhaven children learned to murmur before they learned to read. "They're itchy."

"I know, love. Let's see."

She took his hands in hers, carefully, by the wrists, and turned them to the lamp.

At the tip of every finger, just under the nail, the skin had split in a fine white seam, and out of every seam grew a tuft of roots. Root-hairs, pale as thread and finer than hair, a thumb's length now, longer than yesterday — Nell was right — and they were moving. Very slowly, and not with any breeze. They were turning, the way roots turn in the dark, reaching toward whatever was nearest and dampest. Toward Lyra's wrists. Toward the warmth of her.

"Itchy," Pim whispered again, and his lip shook.

"I know," Lyra said. "I'm going to sing to them. Remember? You keep very still for me, and you think about something good, and you let me do the work."

"Can I think about the harvest fair?"

"You can think about anything you like."

She bent over his hands and began.

Not the Root note this time, or not only that. The Root note was for bringing things *on*: births and seedlings and wounds that needed closing, and the last thing Pim's hands needed was bringing on. She had spent a week trying to work out what they did need, lying awake over her father's old notebooks, and what she had come to was something he had written in a margin twenty years ago, beside a pencil sketch of a split apple branch. *Growth can be sung back, a little. The way a string can be let down a half-step before it complains. Try the falling fifth.*

So she sang the Root note, and then she let it fall, down the whole distance of a fifth, slow as a leaf coming off a branch, and then climbed back up and let it fall again. In the Narrow. Into the hollow of her own cupped hands, so the sound would go into his.

*Down*, she told the roots. *Go down. Go back where you came from. Go back to being a boy.*

For a long moment nothing happened.

Then the root-hairs on Pim's left forefinger curled, the way a snail's horns curl when you touch them, and drew back. Not far. A fingernail's width. The seam at the tip of his finger closed over them a little, the way lips close. Then the next finger. Then the next.

It was not a cure. She knew it was not a cure. It was a half-step. A string let down a little way, before it complained.

When she stopped, the roots were half the length they had been, and Pim was grinning, and Nell had both hands over her mouth, and Lyra's throat felt as though she had swallowed a handful of gravel.

"It'll hold a day or two," she said. "Keep his hands dry. Don't let him sleep near the wall. I'll come back on Hearthday."

"The Grafters come on Rootday," Nell said.

Lyra didn't answer.

"They took the Merrow girl's hands," Nell said. "Last week. Both of them. Here." She touched her own forearm, just below the elbow. "They said the roots had gone too deep. They said it was a kindness. She's eleven, Lyra. She's eleven, and she's got no *hands*." Her voice climbed and caught and came back down into the Narrow by force, the way you drag a dog back by its collar. "And they're saying — at the well, they're saying the Church is sending Flambeaux up from the capital. To *purify* the Hollow. Is that true?"

"I don't know."

"You'd tell me. If you knew."

"I'd tell you." That, at least, was true.

"He's all I've got left of Joss." Nell took Lyra's scraped, tired hands in both of hers. "Whatever it costs. Please."

"I'll come back on Hearthday," Lyra said, because it was the only promise she had that she could keep, and she kissed Pim on the top of his head and went out into the lane before either of them could see her face.

Halfway up the lane she felt something in her right palm, like the prick of a splinter.

She stopped under a hush-lamp and opened her hand. Out of the crease of her palm, where the life-line crossed the heart-line, grew a single root-hair. White. A thumbnail long. Turning, very slowly, toward the warmth of the lamp.

She took it between finger and thumb and pulled. It came with a small wet tug, like a hair, and a bead of blood welled up after it, and the blood was very slightly green.

*Every mending borrows.*

She wiped her palm on her skirt and walked on.

---

The Listening Hour came down on Oakhaven the way it always did: with lamplight, and the sound of people closing doors softly.

There were no bells to mark it. There had been once. The Chapel of the Pure Note had had a fine peal of six, and the town hall a great clock-bell, and the orchard-cantors had hung little wind-bells in the reed-rows. But the Synod had requisitioned every bell in the Reach in the first year of the quarry. The bore's teeth needed bronze. Nobody had argued much. By then everyone knew bells were how the hounds found you.

So instead, at dusk, the lamplighters came, walking the lanes with their poles and their tapers, lighting the hush-lamps one by one. Each lamp began its thin reed-whistle as it caught, until the whole town was full of a soft high breathing, like a field of crickets that had learned to keep time. And the night-hummers went out with their stools and their shawls, old men and old women mostly, to sit at every crossing through the dark and hum under their breath, so that no street in Oakhaven would ever fall wholly silent.

It was the most comforting sound in the world. It was also the sound of a town that knew exactly how close the edge was.

Lyra took the Hedge path home.

It was the long way, and she should not have, and she knew why she did.

The Hedge ran around Oakhaven in a great uneven ring, three men deep: blackthorn and bramble and hawthorn, planted two summers ago and grown, like everything in the Reach grew now, far faster than it had any right to. The Consortium had paid for the planting, which was the only generous thing the Consortium had ever done, and the town had done the rest. They had packed wool into it by the cartload, hung felt and sacking from its branches, stuffed its roots with straw, until it was less a hedge than a wall of wadding. Its purpose was very simple. Every small sound Oakhaven made, every dropped pan and coughing child and complaining hinge, went into the Hedge and did not come out the other side.

And anything on the other side had to listen very hard to hear what was in.

The Hedge was the second most important thing in Oakhaven. The first was not saying the wrong thing near it.

Old Tam was on his stool at the Weavers' Cross with a blanket over his knees, humming "The Miller's Daughter" through his nose and missing the high notes. He nodded to Lyra as she passed, and she nodded back.

She was ten steps past him when the voice came through the Hedge.

"*Lyra-lark.*"

She stopped walking.

It was not loud. It was never loud; it knew better. It came from the dark on the far side of the thorn, low and warm and a little hoarse, the way it had always gone hoarse at the end of a long day's singing in the orchards. Her father's voice. Tobin Ashgrove's voice, that had sung her to sleep and taught her the Root note and called her home across the reed-rows at dusk. *Lyra-lark. Lark, come in, it's getting dark.*

And after it, softly, humming: the first bars of "Apples in the Rain." The old orchard working-song. He had hummed it every autumn of her life, off-key on the fourth bar. Always the fourth bar.

Off-key on the fourth bar.

Behind her, Tam's humming stopped.

She heard him get up from his stool. She heard the tiny hiss of breath through his teeth as he made the ward-sign, two fingers to the lips and then to the ear. *Mouth shut, ears open.* She did not turn around.

"*Lark*," said her father's voice from the dark beyond the Hedge, very gently. "*I'm cold.*"

Lyra began to walk again.

She walked at exactly the pace she had been walking before. She did not hurry and she did not slow. She kept her eyes on the path and her hands in her shawl and her mouth closed, and she walked away from her father's voice as if it were no more than the wind in the thorn, the way every child in Oakhaven was taught to, the way the rhyme said. *Answer it once and you'll answer no more.* She felt Tam watching her all the way to the corner. She knew what he would tell his wife tonight — *the Ashgrove girl, walked right past it, cool as milk, didn't so much as turn her head, and it her own da* — and she knew the tone he would use, which was the tone people used about Cantors. Admiring. A little afraid.

Behind her, off-key on the fourth bar, the humming followed her along the Hedge for a while, and then stopped.

---

Her father had been taken two years ago, in the spring, at the Pellow farm, out past where the Hedge runs now.

The Pellows' youngest had gone down with a marsh-fever, and the fever had gone the wrong way. Not burning but *cooling*, the child growing quieter and quieter in her cot, paler, stiller, slipping toward the kind of silence that does not come back. Her father had sung to her all night in the Narrow, and she had kept slipping. Somewhere toward dawn, Mistress Pellow said afterward, Tobin Ashgrove had looked at the child and then at the window and said, very calmly, *Well. There's nothing for it*, and had opened his mouth and sung.

Full voice. The Root note, and then the old calling-song the Cantors used for bringing lost lambs home, loud enough to go through walls, loud enough to go through the fever and find the child wherever she had wandered and drag her back by the hand.

It worked. The Pellow girl opened her eyes and began to cry: a good loud outraged cry, the first loud sound in that house in a year.

And every hound between the quarry and the Flume heard him.

He had known they would. That was the part Lyra could never get past. He had known, and he had gone out the kitchen door so that they would find him in the yard and not in the house, and he had kept singing all the way across the yard so that they would follow the song and not the child. They found his lyre in the morning, by the stile. Nothing else.

A week later, the voice began. At the edge of the fields, at first. And at the Hedge, once there was a Hedge.

*Lyra-lark.*

---

Where the Hedge path forked, one track went on toward Cooper's Row and home, and the other went out through a gap in the thorn with a gate across it, a real gate, oak and iron, always locked, and on through the dark toward the charcoal clearing.

There was a light out there. There always was.

Lyra stopped at the fork, as she did most nights, and looked at it: a single lamp in the window of a burner's hut half a mile off among the stumps, the only lit window in the world outside the Hedge. And if the wind was right, and the town was quiet enough, you could hear the other thing, too. A high, thin, endless tone, like a wet finger drawn around the rim of a glass and never lifted.

Kaelen's arm.

They had been children together, she and Kaelen Vance: the Governor's boy and the orchard-cantor's girl, which had made them an odd pair in the eyes of everyone but themselves. He taught her to climb the iron-oaks. She taught him the Root note, which he was hopeless at, and which he practiced anyway, stubbornly, for years, until one summer he was not hopeless anymore. At seventeen he went to the Organum, to the Synod's great Conservatory of Bores, on a scholarship the whole town had been proud of. He wrote to her for a while. Then less. Then not at all.

Seven years ago he came home with a Synod discharge and no letter of explanation, and his left arm turned to glass from the fingertips to the shoulder. Glass the color of a winter sky, pale and blue and clear, with the shadows of his own bones visible inside it when the light was right. He did not go to his father's house, or his father did not let him in; the town told it both ways. He went out to the burner's hut in the clearing, before there was a Hedge to go out past, and he had lived there ever since, alone.

The hounds did not touch him. Everyone knew that. They came down to the clearing sometimes; on bad nights you could see their jitter in the air among the stumps, like heat shimmering off a summer road. But they would not go near the hut. The Grafters said it was the glass. *Spire-blood*, the old women said, and made the ward-sign.

People said he had done something at the Spire of Dawning, the year it broke. People said that was why the sky had gone pale. People said a great many things.

He had told her once, the only time she asked. They were sitting on the stile outside his hut, that first winter he was back, and she asked him straight out, *What happened, Kael?* He looked at his arm for a long time and said, *I was supposed to hold something together. And I didn't.*

She had not asked again.

She did not go to him tonight. She told herself it was because she was tired, and because her voice was scraped raw, and because it was the Listening Hour and nobody walked outside the Hedge in the Listening Hour. All of which was true.

She took the other path home.

---

Her father's house on Cooper's Row was two rooms and a loft, with a lean-to kitchen and a hush-lamp in every window. She had lived in it alone for two years and had not moved a single thing.

His chair still stood by the hearth, the cushion still dented. His books were still on the shelf over the settle, the Cantor's notebooks, twenty years of them, full of intervals and orchard-lore and small pencil sketches of trees. And on the wall by the door, on their pegs, hung his wind-reed pipes, a set of seven, cut from the orchard reeds when she was small.

The orchards themselves were gone. The wind-reeds had been the Reach's glory once: whole valleys of them, planted in rows by the Cantors over three hundred years and tuned by pruning, so that when the wind came down off the hills the orchards *sang*, great slow chords you could hear from Greenroot. On the day the Spire of Dawning broke, every reed in the Reach turned in an hour to something like spun sugar, brittle and glassy and clear, and the next wind blew them into glittering dust. Lyra had been standing in the rows when it happened. She watched her father open his mouth to call her name and heard nothing come out but a kind of hiss, like static. It lasted a moment. Then his voice came back, and he was calling her, and the orchard was dust.

These seven pipes were the last reeds in the Reach that had not turned. She oiled them every month, because he always had. They had not been played in two years.

She made tea she didn't drink. She sat in her own chair, not his, and watched the hush-lamp and listened to it whistle. She went up to the loft and lay down in her clothes and closed her eyes and did not sleep, and at some point she heard the night-hummers change at the crossings, the soft shuffle of stools, the murmur of *all's well, all's quiet, keep to the Narrow*, and knew that it was the dead hour.

She got up. She put on her shawl. She went downstairs and out the back door, soft as a thief, into her own town.

There was a stretch of the Hedge behind the old cooperage where the thorn was thinner. A wet spring had rotted some of the wadding, and nobody had got round to replacing it. There was no crossing near it and no night-hummer. There was a flat stone at its foot, worn smooth, where a person could sit with her back to the thorn.

For two years she had walked past the voice, the way the rhyme said. Then, one night at the end of summer, after she had sung the Tanner twins into the ground side by side, she had come out to the cooperage wall and sat down on this stone, and when the voice spoke, she had answered.

Lyra sat on the stone.

She pulled her shawl tight and drew up her knees and listened to the dark on the other side of the Hedge. The oaks chimed once, far off, in some small wind. Somewhere in the town, very faintly, a hush-lamp whistled. Under everything, in her teeth, the bore ground on and on at the Root.

She did not have to wait long. She never did.

"*There's my lark.*"

So close. Just the other side of the thorn, a hand's width away, a hand's width of voice, the way he would have wanted. Warm, and a little hoarse. She shut her eyes.

"Hello, Da," Lyra whispered.

"*You're out late.*"

"I couldn't sleep."

"*You never could, after a birth. Your mother was the same.*" A soft sound, like a laugh through the nose. "*Boy or girl?*"

"Girl. Ruth Madder's. She came out quiet, but I brought her round."

"*Course you did.*" There was pride in it. Real pride; she could hear it. "*Did you remember to oil the reed-pipes? They crack in the dry, this time of year.*"

"I oiled them on Memoryday."

"*Good girl.*" A pause. Something in the dark shifted its weight; she heard the thorn creak, very slightly, as if something had leaned against it from the other side. "*And the Carder boy? His hands? You said you'd try the falling fifth.*"

Lyra opened her eyes.

She looked at the dark, and at the thorn, and at her own hands in her lap, where a bead of green-tinged blood had dried in the crease of her right palm.

She had told it that. Last night. She had sat on this stone and told it about Pim Carder's hands, and the notebooks, and the note in the margin, and the falling fifth. Because it had asked about her day. Because it always asked about her day. Because her father had always been the one she told everything to, every patient and every fear and every small stupid thing, and he was dead, and his voice was on the other side of the Hedge, and it asked.

Her father had never heard of Pim Carder's hands. Her father had been dead for two years.

This was the forty-first night.

"It worked," she whispered. "A little. The roots went back a half-step."

"*That's my girl*," said her father's voice, warm as a hand laid on her hair, from a place in the dark where there was nothing warm at all. "*Tell me everything.*"

And because she had never in her life been able to refuse him, Lyra did.

---

---
---

## CHAPTER TWO
### Charcoal and Glass

> *Every structure has a note at which it wishes to fail. The engineer's task is to find that note before the structure does, and to hold the bore a hair's breadth beneath it.*
>
> — K. Vance, *On the Calibration of Resonant Bores*, Conservatory of Bores, 409 SR

*The charcoal clearing, outside the Hedge. Rootday.*

---

Kaelen Vance slept with his arm in a trough of sand.

He had built the trough the first winter, out of four planks and a tarred bottom, and set it on trestles beside the cot so that he could lie on his right side with his left arm buried to the shoulder. Sand was the best thing he had found. Wool muffled the glass, but it muffled it the way a hand over a mouth muffles a scream: you could still feel the scream underneath. Sand took the sound and spread it through ten thousand grains until each grain carried almost nothing, and on a good night, almost nothing was what he heard.

This had not been a good night.

He lay in the grey before dawn with his eyes open and listened to the arm listening.

It heard everything. That was what the glass did. It heard the clamps ticking as they cooled, and a fox moving in the bracken at the edge of the clearing, and, half a mile off through the thorn and wadding of the Hedge, the whole field of Oakhaven's hush-lamps whistling their thin reed-whistles, like crickets in a jar. It heard the bore at the quarry, a mile north and five hundred feet down, grinding at the Root, a sound he felt in the glass the way you feel a cart go by in the boards of a floor.

And under the bore, it heard the thing he did not name.

It was not a sound, exactly. It was the shape of one: something vast, and slow, and very far down, drawing in and letting out. Like breathing heard through a wall. Like someone in the next room who has been ill for a very long time. It had been there, faintly, for as long as the bore had been in the quarry, and in the last month it had grown. Not louder. *Nearer*, the way a sound grows nearer when you stop pretending you can't hear it.

He had heard something like it once before.

He did not think about where.

He drew the arm up out of the sand in a long hiss of grains running off glass, and sat up, and the world came in through it bright and sharp and much too loud. He waited for it to settle. It never did, quite. Then he reached for the tailor's tape on the shelf above the cot and measured.

The glass began at his fingertips and ran unbroken to the shoulder, and at the shoulder it did not stop cleanly. It gave way in a ragged frost-line, where clear glass clouded to a milky sheen and the sheen faded into skin. He called it the tideline. Every morning he measured from the point of his shoulder to the tideline, along the line of the collarbone, the way other men checked the weather. This morning it was two fingers and a half. At midsummer it had been one.

He wrote the number on the wall with a nub of charcoal, under all the other numbers, and did not look at the column as a whole. He knew what a column like that did. He had spent his youth drawing them.

"Morning," he said to the hut.

He had got into the habit, living alone, of saying things out loud. Nobody heard. That was the point.

---

There were four clamps in the clearing, and he walked them in the half-light the way a shepherd walks a fold.

A clamp was only a heap of cordwood built into a careful dome, packed round with turf and earth and lit from the center, so that it burned for days without ever catching fire. That was the whole art: to keep a thing burning without letting it burn. Too much air and it flared, and you had a heap of ash and a lost week. Too little and it died. You managed it by listening. A good clamp breathed, a low steady hiss through the vent-holes. A clamp about to flare went quiet first, and then popped — a small hollow crack, deep in the stack, like a knuckle.

He was very good at it. He had been told at the Conservatory that he had the finest ear for failure of any student in forty years. It had been meant as praise.

The second clamp was running hot. He knelt and packed turf over two of its vents with his right hand and listened until the breathing slowed. The glass hand he kept against his chest. It was no good for fine work anymore. It could still hold, and it could still hit, but it had no feeling except the one, and that one never switched off.

Halfway to the woodpile, he stopped.

There was a patch of ground beside the stacked cordwood, a rough oval a little longer than a big dog, where the dew had not formed. The grass all around it was grey with wet. Inside it the grass was dry, and flattened, and when he dropped a pebble into the middle of it, the pebble struck the earth and made no sound at all.

A hound's bed.

They came down into the clearing some nights. Everyone knew that. They did not come near the hut. They lay out among the stumps at the clearing's edge, where the forest began, and in the mornings their beds were there: dead oval patches where no bird would land and no sound would carry for a day, until whatever they had left behind wore off. He had never found one closer than the stumps.

This one was twenty paces from his door.

He stood and looked at it for a while. Then he looked at the Hedge, a long dark wall of thorn a quarter mile off, and the lamplit town behind it.

"You're getting bold," he said, to no one, and went to fetch the barrow.

---

The Legionnaire was waiting at the Hedge gate when he brought the week's first barrow of charcoal down the track.

Old Harrow the gate-warden stood beside it with his keys in his fist and the look of a man who had been asked to stand next to a lit furnace. The Legionnaire did not look at anything. It stood very straight in its pewter-grey plate, hands at its sides, its bell-helm tilted slightly toward the sky, as if it were listening for something only it could hear. Up close, the bell was beautifully made. Kaelen had not stood this near to one since the Organum. Someone had cast it with care: the bronze was thin, the lip finely turned, the grille at the mouth worked into a pattern of interlaced reeds.

"Got a letter for you," Harrow muttered, and would come no nearer to hand it over, so the Legionnaire did.

It lifted its arm with a small sound of oiled joints, a folded sheet between its gauntleted fingers. Green wax. The Oakhaven oak.

*The Governor requires the attendance of K. Vance at the Greenhall. Immediately.*

His father's hand. His father had signed his first letters to the Conservatory *your loving father, A.*, and then, after the second year, only *A.*, and then he had stopped writing altogether, and Kaelen had not noticed for a month, which he had never forgiven himself for either.

*Requires.* And sent with a Synod soldier, as though he might need to be carried.

"I'll need to wash," Kaelen said.

The Legionnaire did not answer. Somewhere inside the bell, very softly, someone was talking.

---

He washed at the rain-barrel, and put on the one coat he owned that had never carried charcoal, and last of all he drew the felt sleeve up over the glass arm and laced it tight at the shoulder.

The town had asked him to wear it, that first winter. Not for the look of the arm. For the sound. On still days, in the lanes, the ringing got into people's teeth, and the children cried, and the old women said it was drawing the hounds, which it was not. He had worn the sleeve ever since. It did not stop the ringing. It turned it down to something you could pretend was a noise in your own ears.

They walked into Oakhaven together, he and the Legionnaire, down the track from the North Gate and along Cooper's Row toward the square.

Oakhaven watched him go by the way it always watched him: sideways. The baker's wife, floured to the elbow, stopped in her doorway with a tray of loaves on her hip and stared until her husband pulled her inside. Two girls at the well made the ward-sign, fingers to lips and fingers to ear, and then, because he was walking beside a Synod soldier, made it again, faster. Nobody spoke. Nobody ever spoke to him in the lanes. They bought his charcoal through Harrow and left the coin on the gatepost, and on Chapel-days, when the chapel-reader's sermons came round to the Consecrated and the blessing of the glass, some of them looked toward the North Gate with a kind of nervous piety. Most days they simply stepped around him, the way they stepped around the saplings in the road.

The Legionnaire walked on his left. Its bell was level with his shoulder, and his shoulder was glass, and so he heard what was inside the bell much more clearly than he wanted to.

A woman's voice. Young. Patient, the way you are patient with a child who is not listening.

*—the blue jug, Mam. Not the white. The blue one. The white one's cracked, it leaks all over the table—*

A pause, as though someone had answered.

*—the blue jug, Mam. Not the white—*

He had known. Of course he had known. At the Conservatory the Catalogue's cores had been *resonant engrams* and *harmonic templates*, a line in a supply ledger, and the engineers of the Consortium concerned themselves with the Catalogue's methods no more than a butcher concerns himself with the farmer's. He had been very good at not concerning himself. He had stood in the same yard as Carillon Legionnaires a hundred times and never once put his head close enough to a bell to hear what it was saying.

*—the blue one, Mam—*

He changed sides and walked on the Legionnaire's right, and under the felt his arm rang a little higher, the way it did when it recognized something.

---

The square was the same as ever — the well-house with its layers of decrees and the dead queen's glass seal, the Chapel of the Pure Note with its empty belfry, the Greenhall's long timbered front — except that above the roofs to the north, where the Consortium's mooring mast stood over the quarry like a gallows, a skyship was riding at anchor.

Kaelen stopped to look at it before he could stop himself. It was small for a skyship, sixty feet perhaps, brass-ribbed and oak-planked, with a patched grey envelope and four lift-tanks slung beneath her keel glowing the faint cobalt of drift-glass. Her name had been painted over on the bow, and then painted over again. She flew no colors. Consortium freighters flew green and black, and the Synod's cutters flew iron-grey, and a ship that flew nothing at all from a Consortium mast was either a Consortium secret or a smuggler with a Consortium contract. In Kaelen's experience those came to the same thing.

A smuggler would take passengers. A smuggler would not ask why.

He filed the thought where he filed everything, and followed the Legionnaire into the Greenhall.

---

The orchard-cantors had built the Greenhall three hundred years ago as a guildhall for the reed-growers of the Reach, and every beam in it was carved: reeds and apple boughs and small singing birds, climbing the posts and running the length of the tie-beams, polished dark by three centuries of lamp smoke. When Kaelen was a boy, his father had held the Governor's audiences here with the carvings in full view. There had been a Cantor's chair at his right hand, and on court days Tobin Ashgrove had sat in it and hummed under his breath whenever the petitions grew tedious.

The carvings were hung over now with banners: the Synod's iron-grey and bronze, the Consortium's green and black with its drill-and-wheel, and behind the Governor's chair, where the Cantor's chair used to stand, the pale green glass of the Queen's seal, a yard across, set into a frame of oak.

His father sat beneath it.

Aldric Vance had been a big man, handsome in a red-faced, shouting way — a man who filled doorways and argued with weather. He was still big. But the size had gone soft and grey at the edges, like a loaf left out, and the shouting had gone out of him altogether. He wore the Governor's chain of bronze oak-leaves over a coat that had fit him ten years ago. His hair, which he had always kept short, he now wore long on the left side, combed down over the temple.

"Kaelen," his father said.

"Governor."

"Sit."

He did not sit. His father's jaw moved once and did not move again.

There was a woman at the end of the table. She rose as he came in, which nobody in Oakhaven had done for him in seven years, and inclined her head.

"Engineer Vance," she said.

Nobody had called him that in seven years either.

She was perhaps forty-five, tall and spare and very neat, in black with green piping at the collar and cuffs: Consortium colors, worn by someone who had never needed telling to wear them. Her grey hair was cropped close to the skull. On her nose sat a pair of round spectacles whose lenses were not glass but Aether-Glass, pale green and faintly luminous, and through them her eyes looked slightly too large and slightly too bright.

Resonance lenses. He had helped to calibrate the first pair.

"Adela Vosk," she said. "Resident Factor for the Oakhaven works. Thank you for coming."

"I was required."

"Yes. I'm sorry about that. The Governor felt you might not come for a request." Her gaze went to his left arm, to the felt sleeve, and stayed there, and he knew what she was seeing. Through those lenses, pitch was color, and the arm would be burning like a lamp. "Dawning glass," she said softly. "Third stage. I had heard, of course. But to see it." She seemed genuinely moved. "May I—"

"No."

"Of course." She sat. After a moment, so did he — not because anyone had asked him again, but because she had spread a sheaf of drawings across the table, and he had seen the title block on the top sheet, and his knees had made the decision for him.

---

It was a bore plan: the Oakhaven works, drawn in the Consortium's standard hand. The quarry in section; the shaft; the bore-head descending through strata of stone and clay and a great twisting mass drawn in green ink and labelled simply ROOT. At the bottom of the ROOT, a tight dark shape: the casing. Numbers everywhere, in neat columns. Pitch tables. Torque curves. The resonance envelope of the bore-head, plotted against depth.

And in the title block, in the lower right-hand corner, in a draughtsman's capitals:

**CALIBRATION: VANCE SEQUENCE, REV. 7 (OAKHAVEN ROOT)**

He had written the Vance Sequence at twenty-four, in six weeks, in a rented room over a chandler's shop in the Organum's Brass Quarter, living on bread and cheap wine and the certainty that he was the cleverest person in the city. It was a method for walking a bore down through a resonating structure by holding the bit always a hair's breadth beneath the structure's failure-pitch: listening for the note at which the thing wished to break, and staying just under it, all the way down. It had made his name. It had won him his badge and his rank and his patron. It had been used on every major bore the Consortium had sunk since 409.

He had written three revisions of it. The third had been used at the Dawning Spire.

This was the seventh.

"Nine days," Factor Vosk was saying. "At the present rate of descent, the bore-head reaches the casing in nine days — Hearthday next. And we have been experiencing what my engineers insist on calling harmonic slippage." A thin smile. "The Root does not hold still the way stone does. It moves. We have shattered three bore-heads this month. We have lost two shift-crews." She said it the way she might have said *two wagons*. "The Organum has sent us the revised Sequence you see here, and my people can implement it. What they cannot do is *hear* it. There is only one man in the Reach who has walked a bore through a living casing, Engineer Vance."

"I didn't walk it through," Kaelen said. "It went through me."

The Factor inclined her head, as if conceding a clever point in a debate.

He turned the pages. He made himself read them properly, as an engineer, because the only alternative was to read them as something else. Revisions four through six were refinements, and good ones. Whoever had made them understood the mathematics better than he had at twenty-four; he could see a dozen places where they had caught his errors and quietly mended them. The hand was not a Consortium hand. It was small and exact and very beautiful, and in the margin beside each revision, where a draughtsman would put his initials, there was only a mark: a tiny pair of scales.

The Arbiterium's sigil.

He had never known the Arbiterium to care about bore-work. The Arbiterium cared about law, and taxes, and its Edicts. It left the drills to the Consortium the way a lord leaves the drains to his steward.

He turned to the seventh revision, and his arm rang.

Not the usual ringing. A sharp, high, rising note, straight up through the felt, so sudden and loud that the glass chimney of the lamp at his elbow sang back in sympathy and his father flinched in his chair.

The seventh revision added a stage. It sat at the foot of the pitch tables, at the moment the bore-head met the casing, in the same small beautiful hand:

**STAGE 9 — UNISON HOLD**
*Pitch: 174. Duration: 4 hrs, or until casing yields.*
*Source: Cantor, local (1).*
*Collar: standard. Wire: standard.*

He knew those words. He had seen them once before, on a procurement order pinned to a board in the Siphon Foundry at the foot of the Dawning Spire, in a column headed SKY CHOIR (40). He had signed for the tuning collars himself. It had been one line among two hundred, and he had not read it.

*Collar: standard. Wire: standard.*

There was one Cantor left in the Reach.

"Is something wrong, Engineer?" said Factor Vosk.

"No," said Kaelen, and his voice came out perfectly level, because he had spent seven years learning how to make it do that. "The lamp. The glass resonates with certain chimneys. I should have warned you."

She looked at him through the green lenses a moment longer than was comfortable. Then she smiled again.

---

"Let me be plain," she said. "The Consortium would like you to consult on the final calibration and to supervise the hold. In return, we offer three things."

She counted them off on long fingers.

"First, your arm. Our physicians in the Organum have had real success in arresting third-stage calcification."

That was a lie. There was no arresting the third stage. There were people who died slowly and people who died quickly, and the Consortium's physicians had studied a great many of both. He had read their reports. He let the lie lie on the table between them and did not touch it.

"Second, your badge. Four-oh-eight-A, reinstated, with seniority. The Chancellor-Magnate asked me to tell you herself that the door has never been closed." She let that settle. Ysolde Carrow, who had found him in a chandler's attic and taken him to dinner and told him he would change the world. *The door has never been closed.* "And third," the Factor went on, more gently, "your Hollow."

"It isn't mine."

"Nevertheless. The Church has taken an interest in Oakhaven's burl-sick. An Inquisitor is on the road from the capital with a warrant of purification; I'm told he is four days out." She folded her hands. "The Consortium would much prefer Oakhaven's workforce unburned. The dead don't dig. If the calibration is secured, I believe I could make a persuasive case to the Church that the Hollow is a Consortium matter, and that the Consortium has it well in hand."

"And if it isn't secured?"

"Then I expect I shall be very busy with other things for the next nine days," said Factor Vosk, "and quite unable to spare a thought for the Inquisitor's warrant."

"It's a chance, Kaelen," said his father.

It was the first thing Aldric had said since *sit*. He said it low and rough, looking at the table, and there was something in it Kaelen had not heard in his father's voice since he was a boy, and it took him a moment to name it. Hope. His father thought he was helping. His father had written to the Consortium, and sent a Synod soldier to fetch his son across the town in front of everyone, because it was the only way he knew to open a door.

"And the Cantor?" said Kaelen.

"Will be very well compensated," said the Factor. "Four hours. The collar is removed afterward."

The collar was removed afterward. The wire was not. The wire had been threaded through the vocal folds on a silver needle and knotted into the tuning collar on either side, and when the Rite ended — for those it had not ended — the Tuning Men had cut the knots and drawn the wire back out through the folds, and it had not come out. It had torn. He had heard it described. He had not been there. He had been in an infirmary on the Umbral Tiers of Sol-Luminary with his arm in a tub of ice-water, screaming at a note he could not make stop.

"I'll need to see the bore," Kaelen said.

The Factor smiled a real smile. She reached into her coat and laid two things on the table between them: a small purse, heavy with Consortium scrip, and a disc of green Root-glass the width of his palm, stamped with the drill-and-wheel.

"Show that at the quarry gate," she said. "Any hour, day or night. The purse is an advance against your fee. For your trouble this morning."

He took them both. It was the easiest thing he had done in seven years, and he hated how easy it was.

---

When the Factor had gone, taking her lenses and her drawings and her Legionnaire with her, his father rose heavily, went to the sideboard, poured two cups of apple-brandy, came back, set one in front of Kaelen, and sat.

Kaelen did not touch it.

"Thank you," said Aldric.

"Don't."

His father drank. He drank the way he had always done everything, like a man with a job to get through. Then he set the cup down and looked at his son properly for the first time.

"You'll do it, then."

"Who's the Cantor, Da?"

A long silence.

"You know who," his father said.

"Say it."

"Lyra Ashgrove." Aldric's hands lay flat on the table. "She'll be paid. Paid enough to leave the Reach if she wants — go south, go somewhere the sky's still blue. It's four hours. The Factor gave me her word."

"Do you know what the wire is?"

"Then tell me." Aldric leaned forward. "You're the engineer. You were at the Organum ten years. You were there, when the Spire went. Tell me what the wire does, Kaelen, and I'll go to the Factor myself and tear up the paper in front of her. Tell me."

He opened his mouth.

And the explanation was the Foundry. It was forty girls on the risers in white shifts, and the one on the top tier bleeding into her collar on the ninth morning, and the censer-chain creaking, and the question coming up out of the stone like a hand out of deep water, and his own fork falling from his own fingers, and three hundred days of looking away. It was a procurement order he had signed without reading. It was his name in a title block.

He closed his mouth.

His father watched him do it. Something in Aldric's face, which had been open for the length of a breath, closed too.

"No," his father said quietly. "I didn't think you would."

"You sold them," said Kaelen.

"I fed them."

Aldric did not raise his voice. He had not raised it in years. But his big hands had closed into fists on the table, and his voice had the old iron in it, the Governor's voice, the voice that argued with weather.

"You were in the Organum the winter the Spire broke. You were in a hospital bed with the best physicians the Consortium could buy. I was here. The orchards went in an hour — you've seen the dust. The wheat came up wrong. By midwinter we'd eaten the seed-corn. By Candlefast we were boiling harness leather. I buried the Wetherby twins, and the Hale girl, and the miller's three, and eleven more that winter, Kaelen — *eleven* — in ground so hard the sexton had to burn fires on it to get the spade in. And in the spring the Consortium came up the Greenroot road with forty wagons of grain and a paper." He sat back. "And I signed it. And I'd sign it again tomorrow. Don't you look at me like that. Don't you *dare*, with that on your arm. You drew the pictures, boy. I only sold them the ground."

He was right. That was the thing. He was right about all of it except the part he did not know.

"Da," Kaelen said. "Your hair."

His father's hand went to his left temple before he could stop it — the reflex of a man who had been hiding something so long he had stopped noticing he was doing it. The long grey hair had slipped. Beneath it, above the ear, was a square of clean linen, and beneath the linen's edge, pale as a grub, a root-tip. A thumbnail long. Turning very slowly toward the lamp.

Aldric smoothed his hair back over it.

"The Grafters are discreet," he said, "with the Governor."

"How often?"

A pause. "Twice a month, now."

The Governor who had sold the Root, putting out roots. Kaelen looked at his father and could not think of one thing to say, and his father looked back, and neither of them said it.

"You never came to the door," Aldric said at last. "When you came home. You stood at the gate."

"You never came to the gate," said Kaelen. "You stood on the step."

Both of which were true. The town told it both ways because it had happened both ways: a young man with his arm in a sling of glass standing at the gate of the Governor's house in the snow, and an old man standing on the step with the door open behind him and the warm light spilling out around him, and the two of them looking at each other across the yard for as long as it took the snow to cover the young man's shoulders. Then the young man turning and walking away up the North Gate road, and the old man going back inside and closing the door.

Kaelen stood up.

"Your room's as you left it," his father said, to the table. "I had them air it every spring."

Kaelen stood with his hand on the back of the chair and his arm ringing, and there was nothing in him anywhere that knew how to answer that.

He went out.

---

The square was full of pale noon light. The skyship rode at the mast above the roofs, turning slowly on its line. Under his boots the bore ground on at the Root, and under the bore, very far down, the vast slow breathing that was not a sound went in, and out, and in.

He stood by the well-house with the Consortium's purse in one pocket and the Consortium's glass in the other, and made himself think about it like an engineer. That was what he was for.

Nine days. A bore he had designed, walking down to a casing he could hear, on a Sequence someone in the Arbiterium had improved into something he did not yet understand and did not like. A unison hold on a single local source, which could only be one throat. An Inquisitor four days out with a warrant for the Hollow. A smuggler at the mast who would not ask questions.

Every structure had a note at which it wished to fail. The bore was a structure. He had written the book on finding that note.

Nine days to break a machine he had designed. Nine days to get Lyra Ashgrove out of the Reach without telling her how he knew what the wire would do to her.

He was fairly sure he could manage the first.

---
---

## CHAPTER THREE
### Burl

> *The patient must not be permitted to cry out. Administer the mute-drops a quarter hour before the first incision, and remind the family that silence is a mercy extended to the whole street.*
>
> — *Handbook of the Grafting Office*, Oakhaven Works

*The Hollow. Rootday.*

---

Lyra went to the Carders' before dawn.

She had told Nell Hearthday. She had lain awake after the Hedge, after the forty-first night, after she had told the voice everything, and listened to the hush-lamp and counted the hours to Rootday. An hour before first light she got up and put on her shawl and went out, because Hearthday had been a lie, and she had known it when she said it.

Nell opened the blue door at the first soft knock. She had not been asleep either.

Pim had. He lay curled on the pallet by the hearth with his hands held out stiff in front of him even in sleep, the way he'd been taught, and his roots had grown in the night. Not back to what they had been. But longer. Reaching, very slowly, for the damp of the hearthstones.

Lyra knelt beside him, cupped his small hands in hers, and began.

The falling fifth. The Root note, and down, slow as a leaf; and up; and down. In the Narrow, into the hollow of her own palms. She did not wake him. A sleeping child's body did not fight you. She sang for most of an hour, while the window went from black to grey, and the roots came down. A half-step. Then — she had not known it could — a whole step. The seams at his fingertips drew closed over the white hair-roots like mouths closing over secrets, until all that showed was a faint pale line beneath each nail, like the mark a ring leaves.

By the time she stopped, the sky was pink and Nell was crying in the corner without a sound, and when Lyra tried to say *there*, nothing came out of her throat at all.

Not a whisper. Not a rasp. Air.

She put her hand to her throat. It was hot, and it hurt the way a torn muscle hurts, all the way down.

*Every mending borrows.*

She had borrowed everything.

"Lyra—" Nell whispered.

Lyra shook her head. She found a stub of chalk on the mantel and wrote on the hearthstone, in a hand that shook more than she liked: KEEP HIS HANDS WARM. DRY. WHEN THEY LOOK, HE HOLDS STILL.

Nell read it and nodded, and nodded, and could not stop nodding.

---

The Grafters came up the quarry road at the second hour.

They came in a long white wagon drawn by two grey horses whose harness had been wrapped in felt so that nothing on them jingled, and whose hooves were booted in leather. On the wagon-bed rode a brazier of banked coals in an iron cage, a rack of instruments under oilcloth, and three Grafters in white. A Carillon Legionnaire walked at the tailboard with its bell lifted to the sky.

Lyra watched them come from the mouth of the Hollow, where the lane dipped toward the tannery. She had watched them come every second Rootday for three years, and she had never got used to how quiet they were.

The Grafters wore long aprons of white leather over white smocks, white gloves to the elbow, and masks: a shell of stiffened leather over the whole face, with round glass eyepieces and, over the mouth, a lattice of fine wire, so that their breathing came through as a soft continuous hiss, exactly in the Narrow. They trained in the Organum to work in the Narrow. It was said they could take a leg off at the hip without making a sound louder than a kettle.

It was true. Lyra had seen it.

The lead Grafter climbed down from the wagon and, as he always did when he saw her, unbuckled his mask and took it off.

Surgeon-Grafter Anselm Rooke was fifty or near it, with a long clever face creased like old paper, ink on his cuffs, and eyes that had been tired for a very long time. He had come to the Reach from the Organum four years ago. In those four years he had cut more limbs off more people than any butcher in the Reach had cut off pigs, and he had done every one of them with enormous care.

"Cantor," he said.

She nodded.

He looked at her face a moment. Then at her throat. His mouth thinned.

"Ah," he said softly. "You went ahead of me."

She nodded again.

It was an arrangement they had, she and Rooke, which neither of them had ever said aloud. She went ahead of him. He went slowly.

"Well," he said. "Let's see what you've left me."

---

He went house by house down the Hollow with his sawboys behind him and his instruments under his arm, and she went with him, because she always did, because somebody had to hold their heads.

Hob Wendle first: a field-hand of forty, big-shouldered and sheepish, whose roots had come out through the soles of his feet in the summer, so that for weeks he had walked on them without knowing it and left little white threads in the dust of every lane. Three of his toes had gone to wood. Rooke studied them through a brass lens for a long time, and pressed them with a gloved thumb, and listened to the pressing — Lyra had never known what he listened for — and then nodded to a sawboy, who brought the brown bottle.

The mute-drops. Three on the tongue, and a quarter hour. Hob's eyes went glassy and calm, and then his throat worked, and worked, and nothing came out of it, not even the sound of breath. The drops did not take the pain. They took the voice. The Grafting Office was very clear about that.

Lyra held his head against her shoulder while they did it. The hush-saw was a fine-toothed thing with a felted grip and a blade so thin it flexed, and it went through the wooden toes with a sound like someone whispering *shh* into a pillow. The sap-iron came after, from the brazier, and that was the only true sound of the morning — a small sharp hiss, and the smell, green and scorched — and Hob's whole body went rigid against her, his mouth wide open, perfectly silent, his fingers digging into her back hard enough to leave marks she would find that night.
