She could not hum to him. She had nothing to hum with. She pressed her lips to his hair and breathed the Root note, soundless, only air in the shape of the note, and she did not know whether he could feel it.

Afterward he wept with his mouth open and no sound came out, and his wife held him.

"Good," Rooke said quietly, peeling off his gloves. "Clean. He'll walk."

---

Then the Carders.

Nell had Pim sitting on the hearth-rug with his hands resting on a hot brick wrapped in flannel. When the Grafters came in, he looked at the masks, and the glass eyepieces, and the hissing wire mouths, and his lip began to shake, and he did not move.

Rooke knelt in front of him. He had not put his mask back on; he never wore it for the children. He took Pim's hands very gently by the wrists and turned them to the window and bent over them with his brass lens, one finger at a time.

It took a long time.

Lyra stood by the door with her fists in her shawl and did not breathe.

"Early," Rooke said at last. He was looking at the faint pale lines beneath Pim's nails. "Very early." He turned the small left hand over, and back. "And receding, I'd have said. If I didn't know better." He glanced over his shoulder at Lyra by the door.

She looked back at him.

"Unusual," said Rooke, after a moment. He let go of the boy's wrists and made a mark in his book. "Recheck next month. Keep him out of the damp." He stood, his knees cracking, and patted Pim on the head in passing — a dry, awkward, fatherly pat — and went out.

Nell made a sound behind her hand that was very nearly above the Narrow, and caught it, and folded over it. Pim looked from his mother to Lyra and back again without understanding, and Lyra went to him and knelt and put her arms around him and held on.

---

Then Orla Hale.

Mother Hale was seventy-three. She had delivered half the Hollow before Bettany took over, and she had sung the harvest home every autumn for fifty years in a voice like a rusty gate that everyone in Oakhaven loved. Her roots had started in the spring, in her fingertips, the way Pim's had. She had been too proud to let anyone see and too poor to stop working, and by the time her son Davin found her sitting very still in her chair by the window one evening at midsummer, unable to lift her hands, it was far too late.

She was in the chair still.

She had grown into it. Her hands lay along its arms, and her fingers had split and put out roots, and the roots had gone into the oak of the arms and through them and down the legs and into the floorboards, a pale web of them, fine as lace, pinning her where she sat. And the chair, which had been dead wood for forty years, had answered. Along its arms and up its back, small green shoots had broken through the varnish, and a few had put out leaves, tender and new, the color of spring.

It was, Lyra thought every time she saw it, the most beautiful and the most terrible thing in the Hollow.

"Here's my girl," said Orla Hale, and smiled with half her mouth. "And the sawbones. Come in, come in. I'd get up, but." She lifted her eyebrows at her hands, which could not lift. "I'm afraid I've put down roots."

She said it every time. Every time, Rooke smiled at it.

Not this time. He knelt by her chair and took a brass ear-trumpet from his bag and set its bell to her chest, below the collarbone, and listened. Lyra watched his face. She watched it go still.

She could hear it herself from the doorway, now that she knew to listen. Under Orla's breathing, which was slow and even and peaceful, there was another sound. A dry sound. A faint whispering rustle, in and out with every breath, like wind moving through a field of barley.

Roots. In the lungs.

Rooke took the trumpet from her chest and sat back on his heels and said nothing. He did not need to. He reached into his apron and took out a stick of chalk.

Green chalk.

*Let me try.*

Lyra had crossed the room before she knew it. She was kneeling at the other side of the chair with her hands on Orla's rooted hands and her face close to Orla's chest, and she opened her mouth and pulled up everything she had from the very bottom of herself for the falling fifth—

And nothing came.

A rasp. A dry, cracked scrape of air, like a bow drawn across a slack string. She tried again, and her throat seized, and she tasted blood.

She had spent it. Before dawn, on a hearth-rug, in the dark. She had spent every note she had on Pim Carder's fingers without once thinking there might be somebody else, and here was somebody else, and she had nothing left to give her.

*Every mending borrows.*

She had not known, until this moment, that it could borrow from other people.

"Don't, lovey," said Orla Hale.

She could not lift her hands. So she leaned her head sideways instead, until her grey cheek rested on the top of Lyra's head, and stayed there.

"It doesn't hurt," she said, very softly. "I thought it would. It doesn't. It's like—" She searched for it. "It's like being held. From underneath. Like the whole of the ground's got its arms round me." A soft rustling breath. "I'm not frightened. I was, in the spring. I'm not now."

Lyra shut her eyes.

"But you'll sing me down," Orla said. "When it's time. Won't you? Proper, mind. Not in a jar. Not in the Narrow. Proper, the way I sang the harvest home." A breath. "You promise me."

Lyra could not speak. She nodded against the old woman's cheek.

"Good girl," said Orla Hale.

Behind them, quietly, the Surgeon-Grafter got to his feet, and went to the door, and drew a green circle on the outside of it with a line struck through.

---

In the lane afterward, Lyra caught his sleeve.

She could not ask. She pointed at the door behind them, at the green mark, and looked at him.

Rooke looked at the mark too. He put his gloves in his apron pocket and did not put his mask back on.

"The sap-mark," he said. "It means *past cutting*. That's all it has ever meant. It meant I would come back each month and keep her comfortable, and at the end, when it was time, I'd give her the long drops and you would sing, and her son would bury her." He spoke very low, even for the Narrow. "The Inquisitor has asked for the list."

She stared at him.

"He's four days out," Rooke said. "He carries a warrant from the High Patriarch for the purification of the Hollow, and the warrant names the marked houses. My marks." He looked down at his hands, clean and white and very steady. "I have been told," he said, "that if I stop making them, someone will be sent to make them for me. Someone less careful."

She let go of his sleeve.

"My saws leave people alive," said Surgeon-Grafter Rooke. "At least there's that. Most days there's that."

He buckled his mask back on over his tired face, and the wire lattice took his breath and made it a hiss, and he walked on down the lane to the next house, and his sawboys followed with the brazier.

At the end of the lane, on the step of the house with the red shutters, Lissa Merrow sat in the sun. She was eleven. Her forearms ended in neat white bandages a hand's width below the elbow, and she was holding a tin cup of water between them, pressed carefully from both sides, and drinking from it, slowly, without spilling a drop. She watched the Grafters go by with an expression of perfect, terrible calm.

Lyra made herself look at her until the Grafters had passed. Then she walked home.

---

It was a strange thing, to be a Cantor with no voice.

People came to the door all afternoon. A carter's wife with a feverish baby. The sexton, about a burial on Massday. One of the Tanner cousins, pale and shaking, because her mother had begun to hear a ringing in her ears, and wasn't that the first sign of glassing, didn't Lyra think, and couldn't she come and listen? Lyra stood in the doorway and opened her mouth and shook her head, and pointed at her throat, and wrote on a slate, and watched each of them understand, and watched each of them go away with the look of people who have come to a well and found it dry.

In the evening, washing at the basin, she found two root-hairs growing from the crease of her right palm. She pulled them one after the other. The second bled green for a long time.

She was sitting at the table with the hand wrapped in a rag and a cup of cold tea in front of her when someone knocked on the door.

Not the soft Oakhaven knock, two knuckles, one tap. Three raps, firm, the way people knocked in towns where knocking was not a risk.

She knew who it was before she opened it. She could hear the other sound under the knock. High, thin, and fast. Much faster than usual.

---

He stood on her step in his good coat, with his arm in its felt sleeve, and behind him the lane was going blue with dusk, and the lamplighters had not yet come.

"The gate-warden let me in," he said. "Before you ask. The gate opens for Consortium glass now." He held up a disc of green glass stamped with the drill-and-wheel, then put it back in his pocket. "Which tells you everything you need to know about who owns the gate."

She looked at him.

He had not been inside the Hedge after dark since there was a Hedge. He had not been to this door in seven years. She had gone to him — to the stile, to the clearing, a dozen times, a hundred — and he had never once come to her, and she had told herself it was because of the town, and the gate, and the hounds, and she had known it was not.

He was looking past her, at her father's chair.

She stepped back and let him in.

He stood in the middle of the room as though he did not know where to put himself. He looked at the chair, and the notebooks on the shelf, and the seven wind-reed pipes on their pegs by the door, and she saw him recognize them, and saw it go into him like a splinter. Under the felt, the arm was ringing so fast and so high that the reed in the hush-lamp began to buzz in sympathy.

His jaw was set so hard she could see the muscle jump.

She came and stood in front of him.

"You're doing it again," she whispered. It was all she had, a whisper, torn at the edges. The afternoon had given a little of it back. "Clenching your jaw until your molars splinter. You think if you don't make a sound, the pain doesn't exist."

"Save your voice."

"Haven't got one." She held out her hand. "Give me your wrist."

"Lyra—"

"Give. Me. Your. Wrist."

He gave it to her.

She unlaced the felt sleeve at the shoulder and drew it down, and the room filled with the sound of the arm: a high, clear, unending note, like a finger on the rim of a wine glass, so pure it made her back teeth ache. In the lamplight the glass was the color of the sky on a winter morning, pale and blue and perfectly clear, and inside it she could see the long shadows of his bones, the two bones of the forearm and the little bones of the hand strung like beads. It was beautiful. It was always beautiful. That was one of the worst things about it.

At the shoulder, where the glass ended, the frost of the tideline had crept inward past the joint, along the collarbone, onto his chest.

She had seen it at midsummer. It had barely cleared the joint then.

She looked up. He had seen her see it.

Neither of them said anything.

She had no voice to hum with. So she did the only other thing she could think of. She lifted his glass hand in both of hers, and bent her head, and set her lips to the inside of the glass wrist, where a pulse would have been, and breathed the Root note into it. Not sound. Only breath, shaped the way the note was shaped. One hundred and seventy-four. The first note her father had taught her, and the note she had taught Kaelen on a stile when he was nine and she was eight and he had been so hopeless at it.

The glass took it.

She felt it go in: a warmth under her mouth, and then a color, a faint green warmth rising through the pale blue like dye through water, up the wrist and the forearm and into the elbow, and wherever it went the ringing slowed. Softened. Came down and down, from a scream to a keening to a low clear hum, the way a struck bell comes down when you lay a hand on it.

Kaelen let out a breath that went on for a long time. His shoulders dropped an inch.

"I can still hear it," he said, very low. "The day it broke. Every time it's bad, I hear it again." He was looking at his own arm, green-shot in her hands. "It didn't sound like thunder. Everyone thinks it must have. It sounded like a wine glass snapping inside your ear."

She waited.

He said nothing else.

She had never asked. Not since the stile, that first winter. She had told herself it was kindness, and some of it was. She did not ask now. She drew the felt back up over the quieter glass and laced it at the shoulder, and pushed him down into her own chair — not her father's — and poured him the cold tea, and sat down across the table, and waited.

---

He told her about the Greenhall.

He told it like an engineer, in order, without adjectives: the Legionnaire at the gate, the Factor with the green lenses, the bore plan, the nine days. Hearthday next. The harmonic slippage, the three shattered bore-heads, the two lost shifts — *attrition*, the Factor had called them. The Consortium's offer. The Inquisitor four days out with a warrant for the Hollow.

Then he told her about Stage Nine.

"*Source: Cantor, local*," he said. "*One*." He looked at her across the table. "There's one Cantor left in the Reach, Lyra."

She had known where it was going since he said *unison*. It still went into her like cold water.

"For four hours," she whispered.

"They'll put a collar on your throat," Kaelen said, "and a silver needle through your vocal folds, with a copper wire behind it. They'll tune the wire to the Root note, and after that you won't be able to make any other sound. Not a word. Not a scream. Every breath you take will come out as one hundred and seventy-four. And after four hours, or when the casing yields, which could be much longer, they'll cut the collar away and draw the wire back out." He stopped. "It doesn't come out, Lyra. It tears."

The hush-lamp whistled in the silence.

"How do you know?" she said.

"I've seen it."

"Where?"

He did not answer at once. She watched him decide how much to say.

"Sol-Luminary," he said.

*The year it broke.* The words hung unsaid between them. She could have said them. She could have leaned across the table and asked him, at last, what he had been doing at the Dawning Spire when the sky went pale, and what he had been supposed to hold together, and why he had never once come to her door. She felt the question in her mouth, whole and ready.

She saw him see it there. She saw him brace.

"Who did you see it done to?" she whispered instead.

Something in his face gave way, very slightly.

"A girl," he said. "In the choir. She stood on the top tier, at the end. She'd have been nineteen, maybe twenty. On the ninth morning her collar was bleeding. Down the front of her shift. Not a lot. Just — steadily. I came down to check the resonance at the risers, and I saw it, and I—" He stopped.

"And you?"

"I went back up the gantry," said Kaelen.

He said nothing more about her, and Lyra did not ask.

"I'm going to break the bore," he said after a while, in his engineer's voice again. "Before Stage Nine. At the casing, where the Sequence runs narrowest. I know where it's weak. I wrote the thing. I know where it's weak better than anyone alive. There's a dampener coil in the mechanic's depot at the quarry — a reverse-phase coil, they use them to bleed off slippage — and if I can get it into the bore-head's resonance chamber at the right moment, it'll throw the lock. The bit seizes. The Sequence fails. They can't restart without a new bore-head from the Organum, and that's a season at the least."

"And then?"

"And then they've no reason to wire anyone." He laid his right hand flat on the table. "There's a skyship at the quarry mast. A smuggler with a Consortium contract. I'm going to buy passage south. Three berths. You, and Nell Carder, and the boy."

She stared at him.

"The Inquisitor will be here in four days," Kaelen said. "With a list. You know whose names are on it. Pim's hands—"

"Pim's clean," she whispered. "Rooke passed him. This morning."

"For this month. He'll be marked in one month or two, and you know it, and so does Rooke. Get them out. Get yourself out. Go south, where the sky's still—"

"And you?"

"Somebody has to be in the shaft when it breaks."

"No."

"Lyra."

"*No*." It came out torn, and too loud, louder than the Narrow, a cracked scrape of a word. They both froze, and listened. Outside the window the hush-lamps whistled and the oaks were still and nothing beyond the Hedge lifted its head. She brought her voice back down to nothing. "No. I'm not getting on a pirate's bucket and flying south while Orla Hale burns in her chair. I sat with her this morning. She's rooted into it, Kael, right down through the floor. They can't move her. They'll have to burn the house around her." Her whisper shook. "And I'm not leaving you in a hole in the ground with a machine you built, so you can die underneath it and call it paying."

"I'm not—"

"You are. You've been doing it for seven years. You're only doing it faster now." She leaned across the table. "You don't get to carry this one alone. You don't get to carry any more of them alone."

He looked at her for a long moment.

"The coil might not be enough," he said at last, slowly. "If the Sequence is running on a unison hold, even a partial one, the lock will be too strong for a coil alone. It would need a counter-note. Something pulling against the bore from the other side. Something live."

"A Cantor's voice."

"Yes." He said it as though it cost him. "If you had one."

"I'll have one," said Lyra. "It comes back. It always comes back."

"And the berths?"

"Buy them," she said.

Which was not the same as saying she would use one, and they both knew it. She watched him hear the gap. She watched him decide not to push. It was, she thought, the first time in their lives they had lied to each other in the same moment, both knowing it, and let it stand.

---

He rose to go before the Listening Hour. At the door he paused, hand on the latch, and looked back at the room — the chair, the notebooks, the pipes — and she saw his jaw begin to set again, and saw him catch it.

And under his breath, the way he always did when something hurt and he thought no one was listening, he hummed.

Low. So low it was nearly under the floor. The Root note, the one she had taught him on the stile. He had it perfectly now. And as he hummed it the glass arm answered, the way glass answers a voice, and a thin high ring went up inside the low note and threaded through it like a silver wire drawn through wool. A low warm drone, with a bright cold ring at the heart of it.

Lyra went still.

She had heard that sound before.

Not from him. Not in this room, or on the stile, or in the clearing. She had heard it in the dark, at the Hedge, behind the cooperage — on some of the forty-one nights, not all of them. Under her father's voice. A low drone running beneath his words the way a Cantor's drone runs beneath a song, with a thin cold ring threaded through its heart. She had thought it was the thorn creaking. She had thought it was the oaks, chiming somewhere far off.

It was not the oaks.

"What?" said Kaelen.

She had to tell him. She opened her mouth to tell him, and understood, in the same instant, everything she would have to say after it. *I've heard your note at the Hedge. I've heard it because I go there. I go there at the dead hour and sit on a stone with my back to the thorn and talk to it, and it talks back in Da's voice, and I tell it everything. I've told it about you.*

She closed her mouth.

"Nothing," she whispered. "Go home, Kael. Before the Hour."

He looked at her a moment longer, the way the Factor with the green lenses must have looked at him, as if he could see something through her that was a different color from her face. Then he nodded, and went.

---

She did not go to the Hedge that night.

It was the first night in forty-two that she did not. She lay in the loft in her clothes with her wrapped hand on her chest and listened to the hush-lamp whistle, and the night-hummers change at the crossings, and the bore grinding on and on beneath everything, and she did not get up.

At the dead hour, it began.

Very faint. From the direction of the cooperage wall, a street and a half away, under the whistling of every lamp in Oakhaven. She would not have heard it if she had not been listening for it. She would not have known what it was if she had not heard it, three hours earlier, in her own doorway.

A low hum. The Root note, perfectly held. And threaded through the heart of it, thin and bright and cold, the ring of glass.

It did not call her name. It did not use her father's voice. It did not need to. It only hummed, low and patient, the way you would hum to a child who was pretending to be asleep, over and over, all through the dead hour and on toward dawn.

Lyra lay very still in the dark and let it, and did not go, and understood for the first time what she had been talking to for forty-one nights.

It had not been waiting for her.

It had been practicing.

---
---

## CHAPTER FOUR
### The Boar & Dulcimer

> *VOX. Genuine Last Words, Sealed at the Deathbed.*
> *Do Not Unstop Within a Mile of Open Country.*
> *No Refunds.*
>
> — Label on a vox-bottle, Port Cantabile

*Oakhaven. Hearthday.*

---

There were twelve crates in the hold of the *Drift-Kestrel*, and all twelve of them talked in their sleep.

Captain Vane had carried worse. He told himself so at regular intervals, generally with a drink in his hand, and it had the great virtue of being true. He had carried powder for rebels and saints' bones for the Church, and forty barrels of something from the Blighted Caldera that ate through its own staves in a week and had to be poured into fresh barrels every three days by men in gloves. He had once carried a live Terrasque calf for a collector in Fortis, and it had eaten Pennick's predecessor and most of the starboard rail. In twenty-five years of carrying things for money, he had carried almost everything.

He had never carried anything that talked in its sleep.

"Morning," he said to the hold, from the top of the ladder.

The hold, being full of stencilled crates — OFFICE OF THE CATALOGUE • RESONANT ENGRAMS • HANDLE IN SILENCE — did not say good morning back. It said what it always said: a low overlapping murmur, like a crowded room heard through a floor. A man counting. A woman giving directions to somewhere. Somebody laughing at a joke that had been told a long time ago. It never stopped, it never grew louder, and it never quite resolved into anything, and if you stood at the top of the ladder long enough, you started leaning down toward it to make out the words.

Vane had stopped standing at the top of the ladder long enough.

He shut the hatch and went up on deck, where it was merely cold.

---

The *Drift-Kestrel* rode at the Consortium's mooring mast a hundred feet above the Oakhaven quarry, turning slowly on her line in a wind that smelled of wet stone and cut timber and, faintly, of the green-gold glass the quarry had been bleeding for two years and more. She was sixty feet of brass rib and oak plank under a grey sailcloth envelope that had been patched so often it looked like a quilt, and four lift-tanks slung beneath her keel glowed the faint cobalt of drift-glass. Drift-glass wanted, above all things, to *move*; the whole art of skyship engineering lay in persuading it that the direction it most wanted to move in was up. When the persuasion was going well, the tanks hummed a low, contented chord. At present they were humming a chord with a sour note in it, which meant Pennick had been up all night with his tuning-hammer again and would be impossible until noon.

She was not a beautiful ship. She was not, strictly speaking, entirely his ship. But she was the only thing Vane owned that did not float on water, and he loved her with the uncomplicated devotion of a man who has decided not to think about how she was paid for.

Mags was at the rail, splicing line.

Margarethe Tennant was six feet and some inches of Rust Coast bosun, with forearms like hawsers and a face like a closed door, and she had been deaf since the day she was born. She read lips in three languages and spoke Rust Coast hand-cant faster than Vane could follow when she was angry, which was often. When the tonal trenches had started opening off the Coast, and the first vox-bottles had turned up in the markets at Cantabile, and it had become generally understood that the dark was now full of things that could call to you in your mother's voice, the Free Captains had begun paying double for deaf bosuns. Vane had hired Mags before all that. He liked to think it showed foresight. It had in fact shown that she was the only bosun on the Driftwood Cay docks who had not already heard about him.

*Ship's sick*, Mags signed, without looking up from the splice. *Tanks.*

*Pennick?*

*Asleep on the tanks. Tuning-hammer in his fist. Like a baby with a rattle.*

*Let him sleep.* Vane leaned on the rail beside her and looked down into the quarry. *Paymaster?*

Mags spat over the side, which was answer enough.

Two days. Two days they had ridden at this mast with twelve crates of the Catalogue's property in the hold, waiting for a Consortium paymaster to sign a Consortium chit so that a Consortium garrison could take delivery of its replacement cores. The paymaster would not sign until the Factor approved the invoice, and the Factor was — the paymaster had said it with a perfectly straight face — *occupied*. So the crates stayed aboard. So the crates kept talking. So Captain Vane had spent the last two evenings at the only public house in Oakhaven, which had the singular distinction of being the one place in the Reach quieter than his own hold.

*Going to the tavern*, he signed.

Mags looked at him. She had a way of looking at him that he had not been looked at since he was a boy, by his grandmother, when he came home with his knuckles skinned.

*It's afternoon*, she signed.

*It's a very quiet tavern.*

*You'll come back drunk.*

*I'll come back*, Vane signed, *which is the main thing*, and swung himself onto the mast-ladder before she could answer.

---

He did not think about the rumor on the way down.

He was good at not thinking about it. He had had since spring to practice. A rumor in the waterfront taverns of Driftwood Cay: a woman, pale as a drowned thing, with eyes the color of the sky after sunset, going from tavern to tavern along the Rust Coast and asking, very politely, whether anyone knew where a certain captain was berthed these days. The captain who used to have the *Wave-Cutter*. The one who cut his tow over the trench off Cape Dissonance, five years back.

Vane had signed on for the Catalogue's northern runs the next morning. The northern runs went inland. Nobody had ever been drowned inland.

In his coat, against his ribs, a small stoppered bottle knocked with every rung of the ladder.

He did not think about that either.

---

The Boar & Dulcimer stood on the corner of the square across from the Greenhall, and it was, Vane had decided on his first night in Oakhaven, the saddest public house on the continent of Aethelgard. He had drunk in the taverns of Redoubt Zero, where the dead don't rot.

It was a good room in its bones. Low black beams, a deep hearth, settles worn smooth by generations of Reach backsides. Over the hearth, on two iron hooks, hung the dulcimer that gave the house its name: a fine old thing of polished cherrywood, with its strings cut. All of them, snipped short at the pegs, so that the bright ends curled like a dead man's hair.

The tankards were leather. The tables were covered in felt. Hush-lamps whistled from every beam. And by the door, on a black-painted board, in the landlady's square white capitals, were the rules of the house:

NO SINGING.
NO TOASTS.
NO DICE.
NO WEEPING ABOVE A HAND.

He had read that board every night for two nights, and every night it had got him in exactly the same place, somewhere under the breastbone, and he had had to go and sit down.

The landlady was Hetty Bram, who was nearly as big as Mags and considerably more frightening, and who communicated almost entirely with her eyebrows. She raised one at Vane as he came in, and he raised one back, and she drew him a leather mug of the dark Reach ale without being asked and put it on the bar and took his coin, and that was the whole of their conversation, as it had been the whole of their conversation for two nights. He was beginning to think it was the most honest relationship he had ever had.

He took the corner settle. It was late afternoon, and the room was a third full: carters and quarrymen mostly, a few old men by the hearth, a table of women from the dye-works. They drank the way people drank in Oakhaven, which was the way people prayed elsewhere: quietly, steadily, heads close together. The conversation was one low steady murmur, a hand's width of voice across the whole room.

It was, Vane thought, exactly like his hold. Except that everyone in here still had a body.

He drank to that. Silently. No toasts.

---

He was halfway down his second mug when the door opened, and the whole room turned its head without turning its head, the way a flock turns.

A man and a woman.

He knew the man before the door was shut. He had heard about him from the quarry-hands at the mast — *the glass man, the Governor's boy, the one the hounds won't touch* — and the moment the man stepped into the lamplight Vane could hear him too: a thin, high, sweet ringing, faint through the felt of his left sleeve, like a finger going round the rim of a wine glass in another room. Tall. Thirties. Charcoal under his nails and in the creases of his knuckles, and a face that had been handsome before it was made to do something it did not want to do for a very long time.

The woman, he didn't know. But the room did. Three people touched her sleeve as she crossed it — lightly, quickly, the way sailors touch a figurehead for luck — and murmured the same word: *Cantor*. She was small and dark and plainly dressed, with a rag wrapped round her right hand, and she crossed the room the way certain captains cross a deck. She didn't look like she thought she owned it. She looked like she thought she was responsible for it, which was worse, and much rarer.

They came straight to his corner.

"You're the *Kestrel*," said the glass man.

"I'm her captain," Vane said. "She's a ship. People do get that confused. She's much prettier." He pushed a stool out with his boot. "Sit. You're making the room nervous. Well — more nervous."

The man sat. The Cantor did not. She stood at his shoulder with her wrapped hand resting on the back of his stool and looked at Vane with a steady, measuring, not unkind look, as if she were counting his ribs.

"Kaelen Vance," said the glass man.

"I'd heard. Vane." He didn't offer a hand, and the glass man didn't seem to expect one. "And the lady?"

"Lyra Ashgrove." A whisper — torn and hoarse, barely a whisper at all. She touched her throat, and he understood. A Cantor with her voice gone. Like a gunner with wet powder.

"I need passage," said Kaelen Vance. "South. Three berths."

"South's a big word."

"Out of the Reach. Greenroot, then the Salt-Chime Route to the coast—"

"Not the coast."

It came out faster than he'd meant it to. The glass man looked at him. Vane smiled, broad and easy, the smile that had got him out of more harbor-masters' offices than he could count.

"The *Kestrel*'s a sky-girl," he said. "Salt gets in her tanks and makes her moody. Anywhere south of the Reach and north of the water, I'll take you. For a price."

"Name it."

Vane named it. He named, in fact, about three times what it was worth, on the principle that a man who asked a smuggler for passage in a public house in broad daylight was a man with nowhere else to go.

Kaelen Vance reached into his good coat and set a purse on the felt between them and loosened its string.

Consortium scrip. Green and black, stamped with the drill-and-wheel. A good deal of it.

Vane looked at the scrip. Then at the glass man. Then at the Cantor. Then, because he could not help it, he laughed — silently, into his mug, shoulders shaking.

"Consortium scrip," he said, when he could. "You're paying the Consortium's courier with the Consortium's money to carry something out from under the Consortium's nose." He wiped his eyes. "I don't know what it is yet, and I love it already."

"It's not a something," said Kaelen.

"It never is." Vane looked up at the Cantor. "It's you, isn't it?"

"I'm not going," Lyra Ashgrove whispered.

"She's going," said Kaelen.

Vane looked from one to the other. They were not looking at each other. They were so carefully not looking at each other that the air between them practically hummed. He had seen it before, in old married couples and in mutineers: two people who had been not-saying the same thing to each other for about twenty years and had got very, very good at it.

"Marvelous," he said. "I'll want that in writing. From both of you."

---

"How many can you carry?" the Cantor whispered.

"Three. With my cargo aboard."

"Put the cargo off."

"Can't. It isn't mine."

"What is it?"

"Glass," said Vane.

Kaelen Vance was looking at him. It was not a hostile look. It was the look of one man reading another man's manifest.

"Engrams," the glass man said quietly.

The word sat on the felt between them, next to the purse.

"Glass," Vane said again, pleasantly, and held his eye.

After a moment the glass man nodded, very slightly, the way you nod to another man in a place neither of you should be. He said nothing more about it. Vane found, somewhat to his own surprise, that he was grateful, and then found that he disliked being grateful, and drank.

The Cantor had missed none of it. He could see her not missing it.

"We survived the Fracture," she whispered — to Kaelen, not to him, as if it were an old argument between them. "We'll survive this."

"Survived?" Vane set his mug down. "Gods above, Cantor, don't romanticize it. Surviving just means you were lucky enough to be standing five paces to the left when the sky dropped its guts." He tipped his chair back against the wall. "Last year the tide came into Cantabile harbor singing a high C-flat, and my frigate — a good ship, the best ship I ever had, laid up at her moorings minding her own business — dissolved into blue jelly under the harbor-master's nose. Hull, masts, figurehead. There was a man asleep on her deck. We never found him." He shrugged. "You can't sail on water that forgets its own surface tension. So now I fly. And I carry glass for whoever pays. And if you three-chord saints ever do hammer those Spire-pins back into the bedrock, honest enterprise might resume, and I'll go back to smuggling brandy like an honest thief."

"When?" said Kaelen Vance.

"When do I leave?" Vane considered it. "When the Consortium pays me for the cargo, or when the Consortium tries to kill me. Whichever comes first. Could be tomorrow. Could be a week."

"Eight days. Hold the berths eight days."

"Eight days is a long time to hold a berth."

The glass man pushed the purse an inch further across the felt.

"Eight days," Vane agreed, and put his hand on it.

---

He didn't notice the man at the hearth until the man stood up.

Afterward he would think he should have. He'd been in enough taverns. He knew the look of a man who has been drinking with purpose since noon — the particular stillness of him, the way the landlady watered his mug without being asked and kept one eye on him while she did it. Vane had seen it and filed it — *trouble, hearth, not mine* — and gone back to his berths and his scrip.

A big man. Thirties. A carter's jerkin and hands scarred by rope. Red-eyed, and perfectly steady on his feet, which was the worst kind.

He stood up from the settle by the hearth, and the whole room stopped.

It was astonishing how completely it stopped. Vane had seen a crew go still on deck when a waterspout came up off the bow, and it was nothing to this. Every head in the Boar & Dulcimer came up and every mouth closed and every hand went flat on the felt, and in the silence the hush-lamps whistled from the beams like a field of frightened birds.

The Cantor's hand had closed hard on the back of Kaelen Vance's stool.

"Davin," she whispered. "Oh, no. Davin, no."

The big man lifted his chin and opened his mouth and sang.

*Oh, the last sheaf's in and the barley's high—*

Full voice. Not the Narrow. Not a hand's width. A big rough carter's baritone, cracked and wet with drink and grief, filling the low room from the flags to the black beams and going up the chimney and out through the shutters into the square, where it rang off the front of the Greenhall and came back.

*—and the swallows are low in the harvest sky—*

Hetty Bram was out from behind the bar. She went at him like a ship coming about, both big hands up, and he put out an arm without looking and pushed her away, and she stumbled into a table, and a leather tankard went over, and nobody moved to right it.

"She sang it," the big man said, between lines. He was weeping openly now. Above a hand. Well above. "Every harvest home. Every year of my life. Fifty years. And they've put the mark on her door, and they're going to burn her in her chair, and nobody's going to sing it for her. Nobody's allowed. So I'm going to sing it." He wiped his face with his sleeve. "She should hear it once more. She should hear it from somewhere."

And he went on.

*—so fill up your cup and fill up your can, for the reaper's the last and the best of man—*

Vane found that he was counting.

He didn't mean to. It was simply what he did when something was coming and he couldn't stop it. *One.* The Cantor was moving, slipping round the tables toward the hearth, her ruined voice making no sound at all. *Two.* Kaelen Vance was on his feet. *Three.* The old men at the hearth had their hands over their ears, as if that would do anything. *Four.* Someone should hit him. Someone should put a fist into the side of that big grieving head and lay him out on the flags and put a hand over his mouth, and everyone in the room knew it, and nobody moved, the way nobody moves when there's a snake on the floor.

*Five.* The Cantor reached him. She laid her wrapped hand flat on his chest, over his heart, and looked up into his face, and he looked down at her and kept singing, and the tears ran into his beard.

*Six.*

Outside, in the square, the iron-oaks began to ring.

It came from everywhere at once: every oak in Oakhaven together, a long rising shimmer of bronze, as though something enormous had drawn a hand across the whole town. The hush-lamps fluttered in their beams. Then the ringing stopped, all at once.

And in the silence after it, every bird in the square stopped too.

*Seven.*

Vane had his hands inside his coat.

*Eight.*

Faintly, from outside — from the square, from the lanes, from the Hedge, from several directions at once — someone began to sing *The Last Sheaf* back.

In Davin Hale's voice. His exact voice, cracked and wet and grieving. Only wrong. A half-beat behind. A quarter-tone flat. As if someone who had never heard a song before had listened to this one very carefully, just once, and was trying to sing along.

*Nine*, thought Vane, and knew exactly which number came next, and had known since the big man stood up, and laughed.

---

They came in through everything.

Not the door. The door was barred and felted and packed round with wool, and they didn't bother with it. They came in through the gaps — under the sill, down the chimney, through the chinks in the shutters — the way heat comes up off a summer road: a shimmer first, a wrongness in the air, a patch of room that looked as if it had been drawn by someone who did not quite understand rooms. Then the shimmer thickened and had a shape, and the shape was a dog. More or less. A long low hound-shape made of air that jittered like a bad reflection. It had no eyes. It had mouths instead, along the length of its back like the gill-slits of a shark, a row of small wet mouths that opened and closed. Out of every mouth came a different sound.

A baby laughing. A cartwheel creaking. A church bell, bright and far off, a bell nobody in the Reach had rung in three years. A man screaming *run*.

And from the mouth nearest its head, a quarter-tone flat: *the last sheaf's in and the barley's high*.

Five of them. Six.

Vane had his pistols out.

They were a matched pair of Rust Coast work, long-barrelled and silver-chased, and he had named them *Last Rites* and *Second Opinion* in a waterfront bar a very long time ago, for reasons that had seemed extremely funny at the time. They were loaded, as they were always loaded now, with glass shot: little pellets of Aether-Glass cast by a woman in Cantabile who charged a sovereign a pellet and was worth every copper. The Cacophony did not like glass. Nobody knew why. Some said it was the ring; some said glass was the Spires' blood and the Cacophony were the Spires' enemies. Vane had never much cared why, so long as it worked.

He was laughing. He couldn't stop.

He knew exactly how he looked. He had seen himself do it in a mirror once, in a brothel in Port Cantabile during the Screaming Year, when the first hounds came in off the harbor and took the girl in the next room, and he had laughed so hard he couldn't breathe. It wasn't bravery. It wasn't madness either, not quite. It was just what his body did when it was more frightened than it could hold. The laughter came up out of him like water through a split hull, and there was nothing to do but keep bailing.

*Last Rites* went off like the end of the world.

In that hushed room the shot was a physical blow. Vane felt it in his teeth and saw the old men at the hearth fold over with their hands on their ears. The glass shot took the nearest hound through its shimmering flank, and the hound came apart like a dropped tray of glasses played backward, a spray of bright falling notes and a shriek that was a dozen stolen voices shrieking at once. The mouths along its back went on singing for a moment after the rest of it was gone.

Every other hound in the room turned toward the gunshot.

And toward the laughter.

Which was — Vane understood, with a clarity that was almost peaceful — the loudest remaining sound in the Boar & Dulcimer.

Davin Hale was still singing. The hounds had reached him first. They were around him now like dogs around a fallen stag, and he stood in the middle of them with the Cantor's hand still flat on his chest, and he was singing. But the song was changing. It was rising in pitch, and thinning, and breaking into grains, the way a voice breaks up at the far end of a speaking-tube. And Davin Hale was changing with it. The edges of him were going soft. Grainy. Like a charcoal drawing someone was smudging with a thumb. Vane could see the hearth through his shoulder.

The Cantor had not let go.

Kaelen Vance reached her in two strides and took her round the waist with his right arm and pulled her bodily away, and she fought him — silently, furiously — and the hound at Davin's left side turned its eyeless head toward the movement.

The glass man tore the felt sleeve off his left arm with his teeth.

The sound of the glass filled the room like a struck bell. It was so pure and so loud and so high that Vane's laughter caught in his throat. Every hound in the tavern flinched. Every one of them — it was like watching a pack of real dogs cringe from a raised whip — flattened itself and shrank from that clean terrible ringing, and the mouths along their backs clamped shut all together.

Kaelen Vance put Lyra Ashgrove behind him and walked into them.

He did not fight like a soldier. He fought like a man breaking up a wall. His glass fist went into the nearest hound's flank and through it, and where it went through, the hound's jittering substance fell apart into a spray of dying sound — a laugh, a creak, a scrap of hymn — and the glass arm drank it. Vane saw it drink. The ringing of the arm dipped for an instant, as if swallowing, and then came back stronger. Fuller. As if the thing had fed. The glass man hit another one, and another. His face was perfectly blank. He looked like a man chopping wood.

*Second Opinion* went off. Another hound came apart in a shower of glass-bright notes.

Vane realized he was still laughing, and that two of the hounds were coming for him across the felt tables, low and fast. And behind the bar, where the Cantor had got to — somehow, in the middle of all this, she had got the dye-works women and two small children behind the bar — there was a knot of people making no sound at all. The Cantor was kneeling in the middle of them with two children's faces pressed into her chest and her wrapped hand held gently, gently, over a third child's mouth, and her eyes were closed.

She couldn't sing. She was doing something else. She was being the quietest thing in the room.

And he, Vane, was the loudest.

He could have stopped. He thought about it afterward, a great deal. It would have been easy. He had emptied both pistols and there was no time to reload. He could have gone quiet and still, and let the two hounds cast about for the next loudest thing, which would have been — he glanced — a child behind the bar, who was beginning to whimper.

He threw back his head and laughed.

Loud. Deliberately. The biggest, ugliest, most carrying laugh he had, a quarterdeck laugh, a laugh for rounding Cape Dissonance in a full gale. It hurt his chest. It filled the room. Both hounds turned from the bar and came for him, and he laughed right into their mouths as they came, and he was very frightened, and none of it was funny at all.

The first one reached his boots. He felt it the way you feel the moment before you fall asleep: a lurch, a loosening, the sound of his own laughter going grainy in his ears. Then a pale blue fist came down through the middle of it, and it burst into bright falling noise, and the second one broke and fled up the chimney, singing *The Last Sheaf* in Davin Hale's voice all the way up and out over the square and away across the rooftops toward the Hedge.

Then there was nothing in the Boar & Dulcimer but people breathing.

---

Nobody spoke for a long time.

Davin Hale's boots stood on the flags by the hearth, side by side, still laced. Nothing else. Not his jerkin. Not his hat. The boots, and a leather mug on its side, and a patch of hearthstone where the ash had gone dead and flat, and would not, Vane knew from long experience, make a sound when you stepped on it for a day or two.

Someone was weeping. Above a hand. Nobody stopped them.

Kaelen Vance stood in the middle of the room with his left arm hanging bare at his side, blazing and ringing, and the ringing was different now. Deeper. It had a fullness in it that it had not had when he came in. He was looking down at the arm as though it belonged to someone else, someone he did not like very much.

Vane's hands were shaking so badly he could not get his powder-horn open. He put the pistols down on the felt and sat on them.

The Legionnaires came eventually: four of them in a row, bell-helms lifted, clanking softly across the square. They were too late, as Legionnaires always were, and a Synod clerk followed them with a ledger, looked at the boots, and wrote for some time. Vane read it upside down as the man passed: *Hale, D. Carter. Dissonant: self-inflicted. No further action.*

Then Hetty Bram, who had not said a word all night, went to the black board by the door. She took a rag and wiped the rules off it, slowly, line by line, until the board was clean. Then she picked up the chalk.

NO SINGING.
NO TOASTS.
NO DICE.
NO WEEPING ABOVE A HAND.

Her hand did not shake at all.

---

The Cantor came and sat down across from him at last, the glass man beside her. She looked at him for a long time with that counting look.

"You drew them off the children," she whispered.

"I laugh when I'm frightened, Cantor. It's a medical condition. I've had it looked at."

"You did it on purpose. After the first one."

He picked up his mug, found it empty, and set it down again.

"Eight days," he said to the glass man. "Three berths. And you come aboard for the loading. Your — that." He nodded at the arm. "They didn't like that. I'd sleep better with it on deck. Call it ballast."

"I'm not coming," said Kaelen Vance.

Vane looked at him. At the blank face, and the arm, and the way he stood between the Cantor and the rest of the world without seeming to know he was doing it.

"No," he said. "You wouldn't be."

He knew the look. He had seen it once in a shaving-mirror, a long time ago, on a morning off the Rust Coast before a tow went wrong.

---

It was full dark when he climbed back up the mast-ladder. Mags met him at the top with a lantern and read the whole evening off his face in one look, and signed a single word — *singing?* — and he signed *yes*, and she asked nothing else. Pennick was still asleep on the lift-tanks with the tuning-hammer in his fist. The tanks had stopped humming sour. Somewhere in the night the boy had found the note.

Vane went down into the hold.

He always did, at night. He didn't know why. He took the lantern and climbed down the ladder and stood among the twelve crates in the smell of pine and straw and cold glass, and he listened, the way a man might go and stand at a ship's rail on the anniversary of something.

The murmur rose around him. The man counting. The woman giving directions. The laughter. Tonight he leaned in, the way he'd sworn he wouldn't, and put his ear to the rough pine of the nearest crate.

A child. A little girl, by the sound of her. Very close.

*—it's your turn, it's your turn, you have to count, you can't peek—*

And then, in a different voice, a smaller one, carefully:

*—one. Two. Three. Four—*

He took his ear away from the crate before it got to ten.

He stood in the hold for a while with the lantern. Then he sat down on the bottom rung of the ladder, and reached into his coat, and took out the bottle.

It was a small thing of blue glass, sealed with red wax and a paper label in a Cantabile scribe's hand. A dealer on the Salt Stair had sold it to him in the spring, the week before the rumor started, for an absurd amount of money. *Genuine last words*, the dealer had said. *Recovered from the trench off Cape Dissonance. First mate of the* Wave-Cutter*, Captain. I thought you'd want it.* The label said, in small careful letters: VESPER.

It was a fake. It was obviously a fake. There were no last words in the trench; nobody had gone down with a bottle; the dealer had seen a drunk captain with money and made up a name. Vane knew that. He had known it when he paid.

He had never unstopped it. If it was a fake, he would lose the one thing it had actually sold him, which was not a voice. And if it wasn't—

He turned it over in his hands, in the lantern-light, among the murmuring crates.

"Forty-eight," he told the dark, the way he did most nights.

The dark, as usual, had nothing to say to that. But all around him, in the straw and the pine and the cold glass, the crates went on talking quietly among themselves, the way they always did, as if he were not there at all.

---

---
---

## CHAPTER FIVE
### The Quarry

> *An engram is a harmonic template and possesses no identity. Speech emitted by a damaged engram is to be disregarded and is not to be recorded.*
>
> — Catalogue Circular 12, *On the Maintenance of Carillon Engrams*

*The Oakhaven quarry. Hearthday, the middle watch.*

---

The quarry gate opened for Consortium glass the same way the Hedge gate did.

Kaelen held the disc up to the grille, and the clerk in the gatehouse looked at it, and at him, and for a long moment at the felt sleeve, and pulled the lever. Two Legionnaires stood on either side of the gate with their bells tilted to the sky, and he did not so much walk between them as through the murmur they made, like walking through a room where two people are talking in their sleep.

He had come straight from the Boar & Dulcimer. There was still a smear of something on the felt of his sleeve that he did not look at. The glass beneath it was ringing deeper than it had in years: a full round note with a new warmth at the bottom of it, like a bell recast heavier, and when he flexed the fingers he could feel the fullness in them, like the ache of a limb after a meal. He had not measured the tideline. He would measure it at dawn. He already knew.

The Oakhaven quarry had been a hole in the ground for two hundred years before the Consortium came: a stepped bowl of grey stone a quarter-mile across, from which the Reach had cut its millstones and its chapel steps and the lintels of half the houses in Oakhaven. Kaelen had swum in its flooded bottom as a boy, in the summers, with Lyra, and the water had been so cold it made your teeth sing.

The water was gone. In its place, at the bottom of the bowl, stood the headframe.

It was smaller than the Siphon Foundry. That was the first thing he noticed, and it did not help. It was iron, and it was ribbed, and it was lit from below — a green-gold light coming up out of the shaft the way light comes up out of a well that someone has dropped a lamp into — and around it, on the stepped terraces of the old quarry, stood everything he remembered. The cable-drums. The tanker bays. The glass-channels cut into the stone. The gantries, and the long low sheds of the depots, and a censer-platform, empty, draped in white. They were expecting a Church officiant. Above it all, black against the stars, rose the mooring mast, and at the mast rode the little smuggler's ship with no colors, a single lantern burning low in her hold.

He did not stop to look at it. He walked down the terraces at an engineer's pace, looking at the things an engineer would look at, while the arm under the felt listened to the shaft and to the thing breathing at the bottom of it.

---

"Engineer Vance?"

The young man came up the last flight of steps from the headframe at a run and stopped two steps below him, and stood there with his mouth open as if Kaelen were something out of a book. Twenty-four, perhaps. Consortium drab, with a junior's single green bar on the collar. A face full of freckles and sleeplessness, and ink on his thumb.

"Tobiah Marsh, sir. Junior engineer, night shift. The Factor said you might come, any hour, she said — I didn't think—" He stopped. He swallowed. Then, visibly unable to help himself, he dug into the satchel at his hip and brought out a small battered book with a broken spine and held it out in both hands. "I'm sorry. I know it's — I studied it. At the Conservatory. We all did. It's why I—" He went red to the ears. "Would you sign it, sir?"

*On the Calibration of Resonant Bores*, said the spine, in gilt worn nearly away. *K. Vance.*

Kaelen looked at it for a long moment.

He took it in his right hand. The pages were soft with thumbing. Whole paragraphs had been underlined in two colors of ink, and the margins were full of a small eager hand: *yes!!* — *but see ch. 4* — *hold beneath the failure-pitch — ALWAYS BENEATH*. On the flyleaf the boy had written his own name, and beneath it the year: *409 SR*. The year the book was printed. He would have been fourteen.

Kaelen took the pencil the boy held out and signed his name under the boy's. His hand was quite steady. It was always steady. That was what he had instead.

"Thank you, sir," said Tobiah Marsh. "Thank you. You don't know—" He put the book away as though it were made of glass. "Will you want to go down? To the face? The Factor said you'd want the readings."

"I want the readings," said Kaelen.

---

The night-shift office was a plank shed against the foot of the headframe, full of the tick of instruments and the smell of lamp oil and wet slate. Tobiah laid the readings out on the drawing-table with the eagerness of a boy showing his father his schoolwork. Pitch against depth. Torque against time. The resonance envelope of the bore-head, plotted every quarter-hour for a month in the same small careful hand, and running through all of it like a crack through a plate, the slippage.

"It's worst near the face," Tobiah said. "The Sequence assumes a static failure-pitch, sir — you know that better than anyone — but the Root's isn't static. It shifts. Like it's—" He hesitated. "It'll sound stupid."

"Say it."

"Like it's moving in its sleep." He did not look up from the charts. "The last time a head shattered, I was at the rail. The pitch went up a whole tone in about four heartbeats, like when you touch someone who's sleeping and they flinch. And then the head went. And the crew on the platform—" He stopped. "Marten, and Old Coyle, and the Tanner twins. I'd been drinking with the Tanner boys the night before."

Kaelen said nothing. There was nothing to say that the boy would believe.

"The Organum's revisions are brilliant, though," Tobiah said, rallying. "Revision Seven especially. The unison hold will pin the Root's pitch exactly. It won't be able to shift. I've never seen anything like it." He brightened. "And the Factor runs the tightest works in the Reach. She walks the Legion every morning before the day shift, with her lenses, and listens to every one of them. They keep everything, you know. Everything they've seen and heard. She can hear it all back like reading a ledger. Nobody gets away with anything on her terraces." He said it with real pride. "Oh — and the kit came up yesterday. For Stage Nine. Would you like to see it?"

"Yes," said Kaelen.

It lay in a locked cabinet at the back of the shed, in a case of black leather lined with white velvet, with the Church's gold sunburst tooled on the lid and, beneath it, very small, the Arbiterium's scales. Tobiah unlocked it with a key on a chain around his neck, lifted the lid, and turned the case to the lamp.

A tuning collar. Brass, finely made, a ring the width of a woman's throat, with four small tuning pegs at the quarters and two eyelets at the front. A coil of copper wire, very fine, wound on an ivory spool. A long silver needle, curved like a new moon, lying in its own fitted groove in the velvet.

Kaelen did not touch it. He did not need to. He had seen forty.

"My mother used to take me to hear the Sky Choir," Tobiah said softly, looking at the collar. "When I was small. Before the Fracture. We'd stand on the Pilgrims' Stair at dawn, and you could hear them all the way down the mountain. I thought it was the most beautiful thing in the world." He touched the edge of the case with one ink-stained finger. "They say it's a great honor, for the Cantor. To be chosen for a Rite. Do you think she knows yet, sir?"

"She knows," said Kaelen.

---

The cage went down on its cable in the dark — a hundred feet, two hundred — the stone walls of the shaft sliding past close enough to touch, lit by the single lamp Tobiah held and by the green glow coming up from below. At three hundred feet the stone gave way to clay, packed grey and sweating. At four hundred, the clay gave way to something else.

Root.

He had seen it on the drawing, a twisting mass in green ink, and the drawing had not prepared him. The shaft opened out below the clay into a chamber the size of the Chapel of the Pure Note, and the chamber had not been dug. It had grown. Its walls were root — a single root, or the flank of one, so vast that its curve was the curve of the chamber, rising out of the dark below and away into the dark above. Grey-brown, ridged like bark, but warm, and wet, and veined: every ridge threaded with glowing lines of green-gold glass, like the veins on the back of a hand held up to a lamp. The veins pulsed. Slowly. In, and out.

The air was thick with the smell of sap, green and sweet and overwhelming, and of hot iron, and it was full of the grinding.

The Carrow Bore hung in the middle of it on its chains. Its head was buried in the Root to the collar, turning, turning, and where it went in, the Root bled. Green-gold glass welled out of the wound in a slow thick stream and ran down into the channels cut in the platform floor, and along the channels to the pumps.

There were men at the pumps. A dozen of them, in grey smocks, working the long iron handles in pairs, up and down, with a rhythm so perfect it looked like clockwork. None of them looked up when the cage arrived. None of them looked at anything.

"Catalogue labor," said Tobiah, following his eyes. His voice had gone a little flat. "They don't need rest. They don't need — much. The Factor says it's a kindness, giving them work. Otherwise they only wander."

Hollows. Kaelen had heard the word. He had never seen them. The man at the nearest pump had a wedding ring on his hand and a scar through one eyebrow and a face as smooth and empty as a pond with nothing in it, and he was working the handle up and down, and his lips were moving. Kaelen could not hear what he was saying. He did not try.

---

He stepped out of the cage onto the platform, and the glass heard the Root.

It came up through his boots and into the arm like cold water climbing a wick. Under the grinding of the bore, under the creak of the pumps, under everything: the breathing. It was not faint anymore. It filled the chamber. In, slow as a tide coming in over flat sand. Out. And inside the breathing — he had not been able to hear this from the clearing, and he had told himself there was nothing to hear — there was something else.

Humming.

Very low, very slow, and very tired. Not a song. The shape of a song, worn down to its bones by a very long time. Like someone humming in their sleep. Like his mother had hummed the winter he was twelve, in the last week of the fever, lying in the big bed in the Governor's house with her eyes closed, humming the same five notes over and over because she could no longer remember the rest.

He had put his hand on the Root before he knew he meant to.

His left hand. The glass.

The humming stopped.

Nothing else in the chamber changed. The bore went on grinding, and the pumps went on pumping, and the glass went on bleeding into the channels. But the humming stopped, the way his mother's had stopped when he came into the room and she opened her eyes — and in the silence where it had been, he felt something turn.

Not toward the bore. Toward him.

He had felt it once before. He had felt it in the Siphon Foundry at the foot of the Dawning Spire, with a tuning fork in his fist and forty girls singing, when a chord ten thousand years wide drew in, and in, and in, until it was the size of a question.

He took his hand off the Root.

"Sir?" Tobiah was staring at the gauge on the platform rail. The needle was swinging, slowly, back down toward its line. "Sir, it just jumped. A whole tone. Just now. Did you see?"

"Slippage," said Kaelen.

His voice was perfectly level. It always was.

---

He made himself do what he had come for.

He walked the platform with Tobiah and let the boy talk, and asked the questions an engineer would ask, and looked where an engineer would look, and the whole time he was reading the bore the way he had once read a hundred bores, listening for the note at which it wished to fail. It did not take long. He had built its grandfather. The Carrow Bore's harmonic lock was housed in a drum of brass and glass on the bore's collar, where the head met the shaft, and in the drum's flank, sealed by a plate and eight bolts, was the phase port: the place where the Sequence's timing entered the machine, and the place where, if you knew how — if you had written the book — it could be made to fail.

A reverse-phase coil, seated in that port at the wrong moment, would throw the lock. The head would seize in the Root. The Sequence would die.

"How many coils have you got?" he asked, looking at the drum. "Reverse-phase. For the slippage."

"Two left, sir. They burn out fast down here. They're in Depot Three, by the tailings." Tobiah hesitated. "Do you think we'll need them, for the casing?"

"I think," said Kaelen, "you'll need everything you have."

---

He left the boy at the headframe with the readings and a promise to come back, and walked up the terraces in the dark toward the tailings.

Depot Three was the last shed on the lowest terrace, a long low building of tarred planks with its back to the slag-slope, where the waste stone of two centuries tumbled away to a pit of grey rubble and old green water. There was no one at the door. The Consortium did not waste guards on spare parts. It had not wasted a lock, either. He went in.

The coils lay at the back on a rack, in straw: two squat drums of copper and glass, each the size of a hatbox, stencilled RPC-II, their glass windows dark. He knelt and lifted one out of the straw with his right hand to feel its weight. Heavy. But a man could carry it. It could be carried in a toolbag. It could be carried down a shaft in a cage, among an engineer's instruments, by an engineer the Consortium had begged to come and supervise.

Behind him, the door creaked.

He did not turn at once. He set the coil back into its straw, carefully, and stood, and then he turned.

The Legionnaire stood in the doorway with the night behind it. Its bell was tilted downward now, toward him, and the grille at its mouth was worked in a pattern of interlaced reeds.

He knew that grille. He had walked across Oakhaven beside it three days ago.

*—the blue jug, Mam. Not the white—*

It had followed him down from the headframe. He had not heard it come. He had been listening to something else.

It did not move. It did not need to. In the morning, before the day shift, the Factor would walk her line of Legionnaires with her green lenses and listen to each one, and this one would tell her that the Engineer had gone alone to Depot Three in the middle watch, and knelt, and lifted a reverse-phase coil out of its straw, and weighed it in his hand.

"I'm sorry," Kaelen said.

He said it to the grille. To whoever was behind the grille. It did not seem to hear. It raised its hands — long pewter gauntlets, slowly, with that small sound of oiled joints — and came toward him to take him by the arms.

He took it by the bell instead.

He laid his glass hand flat against the side of the bronze, the way you lay a hand on a struck bell to stop it ringing, and felt the bell try to sound — felt the whole of its thin, fine bronze gather itself for the one enormous note that would have woken every soul in the quarry — and felt the glass drink it. The note went into his hand and up his arm and was gone, swallowed, and afterward the arm rang low and full, like a mouth closing on something.

The bell made no sound at all.

Then he drew back his fist and put it through the grille.

The bronze reeds broke. His knuckles went through into the dark inside the bell and struck something smooth and round and cold, the size of an apple, and it cracked beneath them like ice under a boot.

And the voice came out.

Not murmuring now. Clear. Close. A young woman's voice, very frightened, right there inside the bronze, as if she had been shut in a cupboard in the dark and someone had just opened the door.

"—*Mam?* Mam, where — I can't find it, I can't find the blue jug, I've been looking and looking — where *is* everyone—" A breath that was not a breath. "I'm Tamsin. I'm Tamsin. I'm *Tamsin Reed*. Tell her. Tell Mam I'm Tamsin Reed, I'm—"

And stopped.

The Legionnaire folded at the knees and went down onto the plank floor, slowly, the way a tent comes down when the pole is pulled, and lay there. Inside the broken bell, something small and cold and cracked rolled once against the bronze and was still.

He stood over it for a long time.

Then he reached into the bell with his right hand and found the core in the dark and took it out: a sphere of pale lilac glass the size of an apple, cracked clean through, with a milky fog inside it that was already going thin. It was cold. It was very light. He put it into the inside pocket of his coat, over his heart, and buttoned the pocket.

Then he dragged the Legionnaire out of the back door of Depot Three by its pewter feet, forty yards along the lowest terrace in the dark, to the head of the slag-slope, and set his boot against its breastplate, and pushed. It went over the edge and tumbled end over end down two hundred feet of grey waste stone, bell and gauntlets and pewter plate, and came to rest at the bottom in the old green water of the pit, and sank.

It made no sound. The glass had drunk that too.

---

He walked home through the Hedge gate in the grey before dawn. Old Harrow let him through without a word and did not look at his sleeve, where the felt was torn across the knuckles.

The hound's bed was five paces from his door.

He stood and looked at it in the growing light: a dead dry oval in the wet grass, pressed flat, the size of something long and low. Five paces. He could have stepped from his threshold into it in five strides. Beyond it the clearing lay grey and still, and the stumps at the forest's edge stood in the mist like mourners, and nothing moved.

He went inside and sat on the edge of the cot and took the tailor's tape from the shelf and measured.

Three fingers, from the point of the shoulder along the collarbone.

He wrote it on the wall beneath the others.

Then he took the cracked core out of his coat and held it in his right hand and looked at it. The fog inside was almost gone. When he held it to his ear there was nothing — not a murmur, not a word. Whatever had been in there had said the last thing it had, and it had said it to him.

*Speech emitted by a damaged engram is to be disregarded and is not to be recorded.*

He had read that circular once, years ago, at a desk in the Organum, and turned the page.

"Tamsin Reed," he said aloud, to the hut. So that he would not forget it. He had got into the habit, living alone, of saying things out loud. Nobody heard. That was the point. "Tamsin Reed. I'm sorry."

He set the core on the shelf beside the tape.

And from outside — from the clearing, from the grey mist among the stumps, from somewhere much nearer than the stumps — a voice said, quietly, perfectly:

"*Tamsin Reed.*"

His voice.

His exact voice. The Reach in the vowels, the rasp at the bottom of it, the particular way his own tongue came down on the *R*. Not an echo. It came a full breath after he had finished speaking, the way an answer comes. Then, softer, almost tenderly, as if trying the shape of the words:

"*I'm sorry.*"

Kaelen sat very still on the edge of the cot, with his glass arm ringing low and full in the dim room, and did not move, and did not breathe.

For seven years he had said things out loud in this hut because nobody heard.

He sat in the grey light with the broken glass of Tamsin Reed on the shelf in front of him and understood that, for at least some of those years, somebody had.

---
---

## CHAPTER SIX
### The Listening Hour

> *For lost lambs, the calling-song. Sing it full, or not at all. A lamb in the dark will not follow a whisper.*
>
> — T. Ashgrove, Cantor's notebook, 401 SR

*Oakhaven. Massday.*

---

Her voice came back the way the tide comes back into a harbor: not all at once, but by degrees, filling the low places first.

Lyra tested it at dawn, sitting on the edge of the loft bed with a clay jar pressed to the hollow of her own throat, the way Cantors had tested themselves for three hundred years. She hummed into the jar. What came out was thin, and rough at the edges, like a string tuned too far and let down again — but it was there. The Root note. Hoarse, and true.

She sat and held the jar against her throat for a long time afterward, feeling the note go out of her into the clay and back into her, and did not know whether she was relieved or afraid. Both. It was always both.

*Every mending borrows.*

She had asked her father once, when she was small, *borrows from what?* And he had laughed and said, *From the Cantor, lark. What else is there?* and ruffled her hair, and she had not asked again. She thought now that he had not answered the question. She thought now that he had known the answer and had not wanted her to have it yet.

---

Hetty Bram came to the door at the first hour with Davin Hale's boots.

She said nothing. She held them out, tied together by their laces, and Lyra took them, and Hetty looked at her for a moment with her big square face working, and then turned and went back up Cooper's Row toward the square. The Cantor sang the burials. The Cantor took the boots. That was how it had been in Oakhaven for two years.

There would be no burial. There was nothing to bury. There would be the boots, and the song, and a stone in the churchyard with a name on it and nothing underneath.

She carried them down to the Hollow.

Orla Hale sat in her chair by the window, where she always sat, where she would always sit. The shoots along the chair-arms had grown in the three days since Rootday; some of them had put out a second leaf. The window was open a crack, and the morning came in pale and cool and lay across the old woman's rooted hands.

Lyra did not have to say anything. She set the boots on the floorboards at Orla's feet, side by side, the way she had found them, and knelt down beside them.

Orla looked at them for a long time.

"He sang it," she said at last. "Didn't he."

"He sang it."

"I heard him." A rustling breath. "Clear across the square. I was sitting here with the window open, and I heard my boy sing the harvest home." Her eyes were wet and quite steady. "Was he any good?"

Lyra thought about it. She owed her the truth.

"He couldn't hold the fourth line," she said.

Orla laughed. It was a terrible sound — a laugh with the dry whisper of roots moving beneath it, like wind in barley — and it went on too long and turned into something else, and Lyra put her arms around her as well as she could, around the chair and the shoots and the pale web of roots, and held on.

"Never could," Orla said into her hair. "His da couldn't either. Forty years I tried to teach the pair of them." A breath. "I heard it again, after. Did you know that? Out past the Hedge. Somebody singing it back." She was quiet a moment. "It had his voice. It didn't have *him*. You can tell, can't you, when you've known a voice all its life. It had the voice, and there was nobody inside it."

Lyra said nothing.

"You'll still sing me down," said Orla. "Proper."

"Proper."

"Before they come with their fire, Lyra." The old woman's voice was very calm. "Not after. Promise me that. I'll not have them singing their one note over me while I burn in my chair. I want the harvest home. All the lines." A pause. "Even the fourth."

Lyra promised.

---

Old Tam was on the bench outside the cooperage in the midday sun, weaving a basket with his long knobbed fingers, and he looked up as she passed and patted the bench beside him, and she sat, because you did not refuse Tam.

He was eighty-one. He had been a night-hummer since the first autumn of the Hedge, and a basket-maker for sixty years before that, and he had outlived his wife and both his sons and most of his friends. He had a face like a walnut and a voice, when he hummed at the crossings, that could not reach any note above the middle of the stave.

"Saw you Nullday," he said, in the Narrow, weaving. "At the Weavers' Cross. Walked right past it." He nodded to himself. "Your da'd be proud. Cool as milk. Never turned your head."

Lyra looked at her hands.

"I hear 'em too," Tam said, after a while. "Nights. I'm on the Flume crossing most nights now, down by the tannery culvert, where the Hedge is thin. You hear 'em clearer there." He pulled a withy tight. "Voices I know."

"Whose?"

"The missus, mostly." He smiled with half his mouth. "Forty years dead, near enough. The pox took her the year the old king went into the glass. She used to call me *Tammy*. Nobody else ever did, before or since. And now something out past the thorn calls me *Tammy*, three or four nights in the eight." He wove. "I don't answer. Never have. Never will."

The withies creaked in his fingers.

"But I listen," he said. "Some nights. I'll be honest with you, girl, since you're a Cantor and a Cantor ought to know these things. Some nights I sit on my stool and I stop humming, just for a bit, and I listen to her say it." He did not look up from the basket. "Is that wicked, do you think?"

Lyra could not answer him for a long moment.

"No," she said at last, in her new hoarse voice. "I don't think it's wicked."

"Nor do I," said Tam. "But I stop humming, and that's wicked enough for a night-hummer." He tied off the rim. "Flume crossing tonight. Come by, and I'll give you a basket for your da's reeds. Keep the damp off 'em."

---

She had taught Pim the whistle the day before, on the Carders' step, in the hour before she went to the Boar & Dulcimer with Kaelen.

She had had no voice then. Only breath. So she had sat with him in the sun and put her lips together and whistled — very softly, in the Narrow — the falling fifth. The Root note, and down. Two notes, like a bird that knows only one thing to say.

"If you're ever lost," she had whispered, with what was left of her whisper, "or frightened, or you can't find your mam, you whistle that. Only that. And I'll whistle it back. Then you'll know it's me."

Pim had tried. He was seven; his whistle came out mostly air and spit, and they had both laughed, silently, the way Oakhaven children laughed. Then he got it. Two notes. Down.

"Only you and me know it," she had told him. "It's ours."

She had not told the stone behind the cooperage. She had not been to the stone since the forty-first night. That was the point of it. She had thought about that, sitting on the step in the sun, with a cold sick feeling in her stomach that she had not let herself look at.

*Only you and me know it.*

---

The Listening Hour came down on Oakhaven with lamplight and the soft sound of doors, and Lyra walked to the Hollow to look at Pim's hands.

She was halfway down the lane behind the tannery when she saw the blue door standing open and Nell Carder in it, with a lamp in one hand and the other hand over her mouth.

"He went for water." Nell's whisper was so tight it barely came through. "The pump by the Flume, before the Hour, like always. Fifty steps. It's *fifty steps*, Lyra. I watched him go. And he didn't come back, and I went, and the bucket's there, and he's—" She seized Lyra's arm with a grip like a trap. "He said he heard you."

Lyra stood very still.

"Before he went. He said, *Lyra's singing, Mam. She's down by the Flume. She says she's got something for my hands.*" Nell's eyes were enormous. "I told him you'd come by the house later. And he said you were singing the one — the falling one — the one you sing for his hands — and he wanted—" She shook Lyra's arm. "You weren't there. Were you. You weren't at the Flume."

"I was at home," Lyra said.

She heard her own voice say it, hoarse and small and torn, and understood, in the same moment, everything.

Forty-one nights. Forty-one nights on a stone behind the cooperage with her back to the thorn, talking. Telling it about her day. The patients. The Carder boy's hands, and the falling fifth, and the notebooks, and the note in the margin. Talking and talking, in her own voice, a hand's width from its mouth, while it listened.

It had never needed to learn her voice. She had given it her voice. Every night. For forty-one nights.
