She was already running.

---

The Flume crossing was where the tannery lane met the bank of the green aqueduct-river at the lowest corner of the town, and there the Hedge came right down to the water. The tannery's runoff went out beneath it through a stone culvert with an iron grate, into the water-meadow beyond. A child could not lift the grate. A child did not need to. Its bars had rusted through at the bottom two winters ago, and nobody had got round to it.

Tam was on his stool.

He was humming *The Miller's Daughter* through his nose, missing the high notes, with his blanket over his knees and his eyes shut, and when Lyra came pelting down the bank he opened his eyes and stopped, and looked at her face, and then, slowly, at the culvert.

The grate was pushed aside. A small bucket lay on its side in the mud beside it, a trickle of water running out of it into the reeds.

"I didn't see," Tam said. His face had gone grey. "I didn't — Lyra, I was listening. I'd stopped. Just for a bit. I was only—"

From the far side of the Hedge, very clearly, a woman's voice began to sing.

Lyra's voice.

Her voice — her *old* voice, her whole voice, the voice she had had before she broke it on a hearth-rug before dawn on Rootday. Clear and warm and perfectly in tune, singing low and sweet and patient, in the Narrow: the Root note, and down. The falling fifth. Over and over. And between the notes, gently, the way she spoke to the children in the Hollow, it murmured:

"*Come on, love. Come here. Let me sing to your hands.*"

And a small voice, further off than it should have been, uncertain, answered:

"*Lyra? I can't see you. Where are you?*"

"*Here, sweetheart. Just a little further. Hold your hands out stiff, the way I showed you.*"

She had said that. On the stone. *I've shown him how to hold his hands out stiff, Da, so they won't touch anything.*

Tam's hand closed on her wrist.

"Don't," he said. "Lyra. It's the Hour. You can't. Don't."

She drew her wrist out of his hand, very gently, and went down on her knees in the tannery mud, and crawled into the culvert.

---

It was eight feet long, and low, and thick with the stink of lime and rotten hide, and when she came out of the far end on her hands and knees into the water-meadow, she was on the wrong side of the Hedge for the first time in two years.

The world outside was very quiet.

That was the first thing. Inside the Hedge there was always a sound: hush-lamps whistling, night-hummers humming, somebody's cradle creaking two houses down. Out here there was nothing. The meadow lay in a long grey slope of wet grass and mist, down to the tree-line of iron-oaks at the forest's edge, and the Flume ran alongside it, green and slow and silent, and there was no lamp anywhere, and no voice but the one.

*Come on, love. Just a little further.*

Pim was halfway across the meadow.

She could see him in the dusk: a small figure in his dead father's cut-down shirt, walking slowly through the wet grass toward the trees with his hands held out stiff in front of him, the way she had taught him. He was not running. He was walking the careful, uncertain walk of a child going toward someone he trusts in the dark.

And at the edge of the trees, waiting for him, stood her father.

---

It was his height. That was the first thing. It was his height, and his stoop — the long-backed forward stoop Tobin Ashgrove had carried from forty years of bending over reed-rows — and it stood the way he had stood when he listened to the orchard: weight on one leg, head a little to one side. She would have known that stance across a valley. She had known it all her life.

It was made of the air.

She could see the iron-oaks through it. Its outline wavered the way air wavers over a road in high summer, restless, never quite settling on an edge, and where its face should have been there was no face — only a place where the dusk rippled like water with a wind on it. It had no eyes. It did not need them. It had its head tilted toward the boy coming across the meadow, listening, the way her father had listened, and out of the rippling place where its face was not came her voice.

Her own voice. Singing her own song. Out of her father's shape.

She had thought, for two years, that she knew what the worst thing in the world was. She had not known.

*Come here, sweetheart.*

Lyra put her fingers to her lips and whistled.

The falling fifth. Two notes, sharp and high and far louder than the Narrow — the Root, and down — cutting across the meadow like a thrown stone.

Pim stopped.

He turned. She saw his small white face turn toward her in the dusk, and his hands, still held out stiff. She saw him look back toward the trees. Toward her. Toward the trees. Two Lyras: one whistling behind him in the mud by the Hedge, one singing ahead of him at the edge of the dark.

She whistled it again. *Only you and me know it. It's ours.*

The thing at the tree-line stopped singing.

It tilted its rippling head the other way. Listening. And then, out of the place where its face was not, came a whistle.

Wrong. The first note was right. The second fell too far — a sixth instead of a fifth — and wavered, and slid, the way a child's whistle slides when it has heard a tune once and is trying to catch it. It tried again. Closer. Nearly.

It was learning. There, in front of her, in the space of two breaths, it was learning.

Pim was crying now. She could hear him: thin, and frightened, and much too loud, standing in the middle of the wet meadow halfway between them with his hands held out before him, turning his head from one to the other.

And the thing at the tree-line gave up the whistle and began a different song.

She knew it before the second note. Everyone in the Reach knew it. It was the calling-song — the old song the Cantors had used for three hundred years to bring lost lambs home off the hills at dusk, the song her father had sung at full voice across the Pellows' yard on the last morning of his life, until the hounds came and there was nothing left of him but a lyre by a stile.

It had eaten him while he sang it.

It sang it now in her voice. Softly. Beautifully. In the Narrow. *Come home, come home, the light's going down, come home to the fold—*

And Pim, crying, turned back toward the trees.

*For lost lambs, the calling-song. Sing it full, or not at all.*

Lyra stood up in the mud of the water-meadow, and opened her mouth, and sang.

---

It was the first time in two years that she had sung above the Narrow.

It came out of her like something tearing. Her throat had not healed — it would not heal for days — and the sound that came out of it was not the voice she had had on the stone behind the cooperage, clear and warm and whole. It was hoarse, and cracked, and ragged at the edges like torn cloth, and it was *loud*. It was the loudest sound she had ever made in her life. It went out across the meadow and struck the tree-line and came back off the iron-oaks, and every one of the great trees rang with it, a long low shimmer of bronze from one end of the forest's edge to the other.

*Come home, come home, the light's going down—*

It hurt. It hurt like swallowing glass. She did not stop.

The thing at the tree-line sang too, louder now, matching her, in her own voice, perfect and sweet and unbroken, and for three long notes the two Lyras sang the calling-song at each other across the meadow in the falling dark, one whole and one torn, and a seven-year-old boy stood between them in the wet grass and listened.

Then he ran.

Toward the torn one.

She understood why as he came, stumbling, arms out, face wet. The voice at the trees was the voice she had had *before*. Before Rootday. Before a hearth-rug in the dark, and a whole step instead of a half, and his hands coming down like mouths closing over secrets. It had learned her whole. It did not know what she had spent.

Pim knew. He had been asleep when she spent it. But every word she had said to him since had come out of her in this voice: hoarse, and torn, and his.

He hit her at a run, and she caught him up in her arms, and he wrapped his arms and legs around her and his stiff small root-hands around her neck, and she turned and ran for the culvert.

Behind her, the forest's edge was moving.

---

She did not look back. She did not need to. She could hear them: the jitter coming out of the tree-line and across the meadow, low and fast, a sound like rain beginning on a slate roof, and inside it, from a dozen small wet mouths, every sound they had ever eaten. A baby's laugh. A cart's creak. A man screaming *run*. Davin Hale, a quarter-tone flat: *the last sheaf's in and the barley's high*. And beneath all of them, close and getting closer, her own voice, singing the calling-song, perfectly.

The culvert was thirty yards away. Twenty. She was not going to reach it. She knew it with perfect calm: she was carrying a boy, and her legs were heavy with mud, and her throat was full of blood, and the things behind her were faster than anything in the world.

And then, from the Hedge, from the gap beside the culvert, somebody began to sing *The Miller's Daughter*.

Loud. Very loud. Badly. An old man's cracked tenor, far above the Narrow, going for the high notes and missing every one of them.

Tam came out of the culvert on his hands and knees and stood up in the meadow with his blanket still around his shoulders, and walked away from her. Along the outside of the Hedge, away from the culvert, out into the open grass, singing at the top of his lungs.

*Oh, the miller's daughter, she was fair, and she combed the flour from her yellow hair—*

The jitter behind her swerved.

She felt it go past her — not at her; *past* her — like a gust of wind that has changed its mind, the whole pack of them veering away across the meadow toward the louder voice, the nearer voice, the old man's voice going up and up for a note it had never once in its life been able to reach.

She reached the culvert. She pushed Pim into it ahead of her and heard Nell's voice at the other end, a sob, and small hands being taken, and she went in after him on her knees. At the mouth of it she turned, because she could not help it, and looked back.

Tam stood in the middle of the meadow with the hounds all around him. He was still singing. He had his face turned up to the dark sky and his eyes shut and his blanket round his shoulders, and he was singing *The Miller's Daughter*, and the song was going grainy, and he was going grainy with it, his edges softening and blurring like a drawing someone is rubbing out with a thumb, and still he sang. He came to the high note at the end of the verse. The one he had never, in eighty-one years, been able to reach.

He missed it.

And then there was nothing in the meadow but the jitter, and the mist, and the song — still going, in his voice, a quarter-tone flat — moving away across the grass toward the trees.

---

They found his stool at the Flume crossing, and his blanket gone, and on the stool the basket he had made for her that afternoon, finished, its rim tied off neat.

Nell sat on the bank in the lamplight with Pim in her lap, rocking, both of them silent. Pim's hands were held out stiff behind his mother's back, and he was looking at Lyra over his mother's shoulder with an expression she would carry for the rest of her life. Not fear. Something older than fear. The look of a child who has just learned that there are two of everyone.

People came down the lane with lamps. They stood at a distance. Somebody had seen the Cantor go through the culvert at the Listening Hour; somebody had heard the calling-song at full voice over the whole of the Hollow; somebody had heard Tam. They did not come close. They stood and looked at her, sitting in the tannery mud with blood at the corner of her mouth, and she knew what they were seeing, and she could not blame them for it.

After a while, one of the old women from the dye-works came down the bank without a word, and picked up Tam's stool, and set it straight at the crossing, and sat down on it. She pulled her shawl around her. She began to hum.

The Listening Hour went on. The hush-lamps whistled. Somewhere in the town, a door closed softly.

---

She did not go to the Hedge that night.

She did not have to. At the dead hour, lying in the loft with the basket on the floor beside the bed and her father's seven reed-pipes on their pegs below, she heard it begin. It came from the direction of the cooperage wall, and from the Flume, and from the stretch of Hedge behind Cooper's Row, all at once — as though the thing at the Hedge had discovered, somewhere in the last three days, that it did not need to stand in one place.

It sang to her in four voices.

Her father's, humming *Apples in the Rain*, off-key on the fourth bar.

Kaelen's: the Root note, low, with the thin cold ring of glass threaded through its heart.

Tam's, singing *The Miller's Daughter*, going up and up for the high note, and missing it.

And her own. Not the voice it had used in the meadow — the perfect one, the whole one, the voice she'd had before. The new one. Hoarse, and cracked, and ragged at the edges like torn cloth. The voice she had found in the water-meadow at dusk, singing the calling-song at full volume across the grass to a boy who chose it because it was broken.

It had learned that, too.

And at the very end, just before dawn, softly, in the Narrow, it whistled. Two notes. The Root, and down.

It got the interval exactly right.

---
---

## CHAPTER SEVEN
### Flambeau

> *Where the second note has taken root, let the Pure Flame be the only song.*
>
> — Warrant of Purification, under the seal of His Resonance the High Patriarch

*Oakhaven. Dawnday.*

---

He heard them before dawn, through the sand.

He had been lying with the arm buried to the shoulder, not sleeping, listening to the breathing at the bottom of the world, when the arm began, very quietly, to sing along with something.

Not ring. *Sing.* A single note, rising up through the sand in a pure clear line — the glass answering, the way glass answers a voice — and for a moment he did not understand what it was answering, because it was a note he had not heard sung by human throats in seven years.

Five hundred and twenty-eight.

He pulled the arm out of the trough and sat up, and the note filled the hut, bright and high and holy, pouring out of his own flesh. Dawning glass. Light-glass, the blood of the Spire of Dawning, tuned to the Pure Note in the moment it set around his bones. For seven years it had rung at whatever pitch it pleased. Now, for the first time since the Foundry, it had heard its own note sung by others, far off, many voices together, and it was singing back to them like a dog that hears its master's whistle across a valley.

He buried it again, to the shoulder, and packed the sand hard over it with his right hand, and it went on singing, muffled, under the sand. He could feel it in his teeth. He lay there and could not make it stop.

---

They came up the Greenroot road at sunrise, singing.

Kaelen watched from the North Gate, with Harrow and half of Oakhaven's north side packed into the gatehouse and along the inner bank of the Hedge with their hands over their mouths. The whole town, he thought afterward, must have been at a window or a wall. Nobody had heard singing on the Greenroot road in two years. Nobody had heard singing anywhere — aloud, in the open, in daylight — and lived.

There were thirty in the choir, young men and young women in white, walking four abreast with gorgets of gold at their throats, and they were singing the Pure Note in unison. One note. Five hundred and twenty-eight. Held without end, as the catechism said, the breath passed down the line from voice to voice so that the note never broke. It rose off the road and over the fields and filled the whole bowl of the sky above the Reach, bright and loud and unbearably pure.

On either side of them walked the Flambeaux.

Kaelen had not seen Flambeaux since the Foundry either. They had not changed. Tall men and women in robes the red of banked coals, hooded, their faces in shadow, each carrying a fire-stave: a staff of black iron as tall as a man, topped with an iron cage, and in every cage a fist-sized lump of amber Aether-Glass, burning. Glass-fire. It burned white-gold and smokeless and without fuel, and it burned, the Church said, with the Pure Flame of the Light itself.

At the edges of the fields, along the tree-line on both sides of the road, the hounds were watching.

He could see them from the gate: a jitter in the air along the forest's edge like heat above a summer road, pacing, keeping pace. Dozens of them. More than he had ever seen at once. They had come to the singing the way they always came to singing. And they would not close. They paced along the edge of the fields with the procession, a hundred yards off, and whenever one drifted nearer, a Flambeau on that side raised his stave, and the white-gold fire in its cage flared, and the jitter flinched back into the trees.

The Pure Note walked where it liked. That was the lesson, and everyone on the Hedge understood it perfectly.

And at the head of the procession, alone, in red, swinging a great brass censer on a chain, walked the Inquisitor.

The chain creaked at the top of each arc.

Kaelen heard it from the gate. He heard it from three hundred yards away, over thirty voices and the crowd's breathing and his own arm singing under its sleeve. He would have heard it, he thought, from the far side of the world.

Creak.

He was on the gantry. The fork was in his fist. Forty girls in white shifts on the risers below him, and the one on the top tier with blood in her collar, and the chain creaking at the top of each arc, and—

Creak.

He took hold of the gatepost with his right hand and held on until it passed.

---

They sang all the way to the square and stopped, and the silence afterward was so enormous that people put their hands over their ears as though it hurt.

His father was on the Greenhall steps. Aldric Vance stood in his Governor's chain with his long grey hair combed down over his temple, and Factor Vosk stood at his shoulder in her green lenses, and a line of Carillon Legionnaires stood below them with their bells lifted. His father's face was grey and set and very still: the face of a man attending an execution he has signed for.

The Inquisitor climbed the steps and gave the Governor his hand to kiss.

He was thirty-eight now, Kaelen reckoned. He had not aged the way men age. He had aged the way statues weather — the lines cut cleaner and deeper, the face still beautiful. He wore his red robes as though he had been born in them. On his right hand was the Inquisitor's ring. His left he kept gloved in white silk, and when he raised it to bless the Governor, the silk slid back at the wrist, and Kaelen saw what was underneath.

The skin was gone. Not burned. Not scarred. *Gone* — bleached out, the way the light had bleached the iron tankers in the Foundry, the color washed out of it until it was the translucent white of a skinned almond. You could see the blue veins inside it, and the pale shadows of the tendons, and in bright sun, he thought, you would see the bones.

The light had taken the hem of the Inquisitor's robe that morning. Then it had taken his hand. Then it had stopped. Nobody had ever explained why it stopped.

A herald in white and gold stepped forward, unrolled a warrant with the High Patriarch's great sunburst seal hanging from it on a ribbon, and read it aloud.

Aloud. In the square. In a voice trained for cathedrals, going up off the stones and over the rooftops, and nobody on the Hedge breathed, and out along the tree-line the jitter paced and paced.

*Whereas the second note has taken root in the town of Oakhaven, in the Resonant Reach, among the dwellings of the district called the Hollow* — and on, through the whereases — *and whereas the ground must be made clean before the Rite—*

Kaelen went cold.

*—it is ordered that the Pure Note be held over the town of Oakhaven without end, by day and by night, until the Rite; and that the Hollow be given to the Pure Flame at the hour the Rite of Unison is begun, on Hearthday next, that the ground beneath the Rite be sanctified. And it is further ordered that the Cantor of Oakhaven, Lyra Ashgrove, having been chosen by the Consortium of Bores and by the Arbiterium for the honor of the Unison Hold, be kept in the Church's protection until the Rite, that her voice be preserved unto the Light.*

Across the square, three hundred heads turned.

She was there. Of course she was there. She stood at the mouth of Cooper's Row with her shawl around her shoulders and her hands in its folds, with a bruise on her cheekbone that had not been there on Hearthday and a split at the corner of her mouth. He had heard about the meadow from Harrow on Stasisday morning, who had heard it from everyone: the Cantor had gone out through the tannery culvert at the Listening Hour and sung the calling-song at full voice and brought the Carder boy back, and old Tam had walked out after her singing *The Miller's Daughter*, and they had found his stool. Kaelen had gone to her door that afternoon. She had not opened it. He had stood in the lane for an hour, and he had not blamed her.

She stood very straight now under three hundred stares and did not look at anyone. Two Legionnaires detached themselves from the line below the Greenhall steps and crossed the square toward her, clanking softly, and took up positions on either side of her, bells lifted, and stood there.

*In the Church's protection.*

His father did not move. He stood on the Greenhall steps with his hands clasped behind his back and did not look at Cooper's Row, and did not look at his son.

The choir filed into the Chapel of the Pure Note, still singing, and did not stop. It would not stop now until Hearthday. The note went up out of the empty belfry and settled over Oakhaven like a lid.

---

The Inquisitor found him afterward, while the crowd was breaking up.

Kaelen had not moved from the corner of the well-house. The Flambeaux had grounded their staves at the four corners of the square, so that the glass-fire burned in its cages like four small suns, and people were going home along the edges of the square, quickly, heads down, not looking at the fire. He felt the Inquisitor coming before he saw him. He felt it in the arm. The glass knew the censer.

Creak.

"Engineer," said Ignatius Crane.

He stopped a pace away, the censer hanging quiet at his side on its chain, and looked Kaelen up and down with an expression of courteous and absolute interest, the way a scholar looks at a text he has only ever seen quoted.

"Engineer Vance," he said. "The prodigal technician returns. I see you survived the Foundry." His eyes went to the felt sleeve. "More or less."

"You too," said Kaelen. He looked at the white silk glove. "More or less."

The Inquisitor smiled, and lifted the gloved hand a little, as if showing it to a friend. "The Light took what it wished of me and returned the rest. I have never questioned its choice." He tilted his head toward the sleeve. "And you. Dawning glass, from the fingertips nearly to the heart — I can hear it from here. The Light's own blood, set in your bones. Do you know what the Church calls men like you, Engineer?"

"Consecrated."

"Consecrated." Ignatius savored the word. "The High Patriarch would give a great deal to have you kneel in the Chapel of the Organum. A living relic of the Dawning. You would be a sermon in yourself." A pause. "I told him you would never come. I told him that in three hundred days you never once sang the Pure Note on the gantry. I heard you, you know. Every dawn. Humming your little Reach note under your breath, while forty holy daughters held the Light." He said it without heat, as though remarking on the weather. "I have prayed about that humming for seven years."

"Have you."

"I have." The Inquisitor stepped closer. His voice dropped — not into the Narrow; he did not appear to know the Narrow existed — but into something private. "Shall I tell you what I have come to believe, in seven years of prayer? The unison did not fail. It was *broken*. Thirty-nine voices held the Light perfectly, Engineer. Perfectly. And the Light was pleased. And then one voice shouted. A Reach voice. Too loud." He looked into Kaelen's face. "The first dissonance was yours. The Light heard a second note, and it turned its face from us."

*It did not turn its face because I shouted. It let go because they held. Because they held it perfectly. Because it had waited ten thousand years for somebody to hold it, and I built the bore that let them.*

He said none of that. He said:

"It let go because they held."

The Inquisitor went very still.

"It asked a question," Kaelen said. "Just before. You heard it. I saw your face. It asked, and they answered, and it believed them. That is why it let go. Not because anybody shouted." He was surprised at how steady his voice was. "You have been praying about the wrong sound for seven years, Inquisitor."

For a moment — only a moment — something moved behind the statue's face. A crack of some kind. A fault-line running deep under the surface. Kaelen had spent his life listening for fault-lines. He heard this one.

Then it closed.

"Do not presume to teach me the Light, Engineer," said Ignatius Crane softly. He raised the bleached hand between them, and the white silk slid back, and in the pale morning Kaelen could see the shadows of the bones. "I have *held* it."

He let the glove fall back into place.

"I am to officiate the Hold," he said, pleasantly again. "As I did at the Dawning. The Arbiterium asked for me by name. It is a great mercy, you know, this new Sequence. One voice, not forty. Four hours, not three hundred days. The Supreme Arbiter's mathematicians have made it so much kinder." He inclined his head. "I shall see you at the Rite, Engineer. I am told you will be at the wire."

Creak, went the chain as he walked away. Creak.

And under its sleeve, very softly, the arm kept time.

---

"I'm told I'll be at the wire," Kaelen said.

The Factor looked up from her desk.

Her office was a room at the back of the quarry's counting-house: whitewashed, spare, with one window onto the terraces and the headframe, and a lamp with a plain tin shade and no chimney — which he noticed, and which she saw him notice. On the wall behind her hung a framed drawing of the Carrow Bore in section, very fine, signed in the corner in a hand he knew: *Y. Carrow*.

"Sit down, Engineer," said Adela Vosk.

He did not sit.

"The Church," she said, "has asked that the wire be threaded by a Consecrated hand. As at the Dawning — though I understand you did not thread it yourself, then." The green lenses rested on his sleeve. "The Inquisitor was very particular. He wants you at the collar. He says the Light will know the hand."

"And the Hollow."

"Ah." She folded her hands on the desk. "You heard the warrant. I did warn you, on Rootday. The Church has its own reasons for everything, and its reasons for Oakhaven's Hollow are Rite reasons — the ground must be clean — and the Church's warrants rank above my invoices." She said it with the faintest distaste, as though the Church were a supplier who had overcharged her. "However. If the calibration is secured — if the supervising engineer of Stage Nine is a Consecrated hand, and the Inquisitor has what he wants at the collar — then I believe I can persuade him that purification may wait until after the Rite. After the Rite, the Root will be open, and the Consortium will have a great deal of work for the Hollow's hands. Living hands." She let that settle. "Which is to say: sign as supervising engineer, and I will petition to defer the fire. I believe the petition will be granted. The Inquisitor wants you very much."

She took a paper from a drawer, laid it on the desk between them, and turned it to face him. A pen lay beside it.

*STAGE NINE — UNISON HOLD. Supervising Engineer: ____________. Collar & Wire: ____________.*

"Thirty-one households," said the Factor. "Eighty-four souls, by Surgeon-Grafter Rooke's census. Nineteen of them children. Against four hours." She did not say *against one throat*. She did not need to.

Kaelen looked at the paper.

He had spent his youth doing this arithmetic. That was what the Vance Sequence was, underneath the pitch tables: arithmetic about which things you could afford to let break. He looked at the paper, and the empty line, and the number the Factor had said, and he felt the arithmetic do itself in the back of his head, cold and fast and neat, the way it always had, and arrive at its answer.

"When do you need it?" he said.

"By dusk on Driftday." She paused. "Oh — Engineer. A small thing. One of my Legionnaires walked into the slag-pit in the middle watch on Hearthday. They do that occasionally, when an engram degrades. They wander." The green lenses did not leave his face. "You were on the terraces that night, I think. Did you see anything?"

"No," said Kaelen.

"No," the Factor agreed. "It was very dark." She picked up her own pen. "Driftday, Engineer."

---

The two Legionnaires at Lyra's door let him in because of the green glass disc. They lifted their bells a little as he showed it — the way a dog lifts its head at a familiar step — and stood aside.

He thought about that on the doorstep. The Consortium's consultant. The Rite's engineer. The hand that would be at the wire. The door opened for him because of what he had agreed to become.

She was at the table, with a cup of something steaming between her hands, and a clay jar beside it, and a slate, and the basket from the Flume crossing on the settle with her father's seven pipes laid in it. The bruise on her cheekbone was purple going yellow. When she saw him, she did not get up.

He put a finger to his lips, and pointed at the door, and at the bells beyond it. Then he sat down across from her, pulled the slate toward him, and wrote: *THEY KEEP EVERYTHING. THE FACTOR LISTENS TO THEM EVERY MORNING.*

She read it. She looked at the door. Then she took the chalk out of his fingers and wrote underneath: *THEN WE WHISPER, AND WE WRITE.*

"I heard," he said, barely a breath.

"The whole town heard." Her voice was a ruin — worse than Rootday, hoarse and cracked right through, the voice of somebody who has screamed at a funeral. "Tam walked out into the meadow singing, so I could get Pim back through the culvert. He never reached a note above the middle of the stave in his life. He didn't reach that one either."

He did not know what to say to that, so he said nothing.

"It has my voice," she whispered. "The thing at the Hedge. It called Pim out in my voice, Kael. My old voice. It sang him the falling fifth." She looked into the cup. "I didn't tell you something, on Rootday. I should have." She drew a breath, and he watched her decide, and watched her decide not to. "It knows my voice," she said. "That's all. It knows mine, and it knows yours."

"I know," said Kaelen. "It said a name back to me. Massday, at dawn. In my voice." He looked at his hands. "I killed a Legionnaire in the quarry on Hearthday night. After the tavern."

She went still.

He told her, in the lowest whisper he had. Depot Three. The coil in the straw. The reed-pattern grille. The bell he drank, and the core he broke, and the young woman inside it who had been looking for a blue jug, and who had said her own name three times over as if she were afraid of losing it. He took the cracked lilac sphere out of his coat and set it on the table between them. The fog in it was gone. It was only broken glass now.

Lyra looked at it for a long moment. Then she reached out and laid one finger on it, very gently, the way she had touched Pim's roots.

"Tamsin Reed," she whispered.

"Tamsin Reed."

Then he wrote the Factor's offer on the slate, line by line, and turned it around to face her.

---

She read it twice. Then she wiped the slate with her sleeve and wrote two words and turned it back.

*SIGN IT.*

"No," he breathed.

"Eighty-four people, Kael." She said it very evenly, in the ruin of her voice. "Nineteen children. Orla. Nell and Pim. The Merrow girl. Hob Wendle, who can't run on those feet. They'll burn them in their houses on Hearthday while the choir sings its one note, so that nobody hears them scream. I heard the warrant." She laid her hands flat on the table. "Four hours."

"It isn't four hours. I told you what it is."

"I heard you."

"Then you know it isn't—" He stopped. He looked at her across the table — the bruise, the split lip, the torn voice, the terrible calm — and understood what he was looking at, because he had seen it in a shaving-mirror every morning for seven years.

"You told me once," he said, "that a martyr is just a musician who gave up before the coda."

She stared at him.

"We were twelve," he said. "On the stile. I'd broken my arm falling off the chapel roof trying to get a look at the bells, and I said I didn't care if it hurt, because I was the only one who had to have it. And you said that. *A martyr is just a musician who gave up before the coda.* I didn't know what a coda was. I had to go and ask your da."

"I was a very annoying child," Lyra whispered.

"You were right."

She looked away. He watched her jaw work, and watched her not cry, which was a thing he had seen her do since she was eight years old.

"There's another way," he said. He took the slate and wrote as he whispered. "I go down tonight, before the Legion posts a guard on the shaft. The coil in my toolbag. I throw the lock *now*, five days early, while the head is still above the casing. No bore, no Rite. No Rite, no warrant — the fire is for the Rite's ground. They'll have nothing left to burn it for."

"And the Root?"

He did not answer at once.

"You don't know what it will do," Lyra said. "If you break the bore five days early with the Root half opened. You don't know. I can see it on your face." She leaned forward. "And they'll know it was you. They'll know by morning. The Factor already knows you were on the terraces on Hearthday. They'll hang you off the headframe and burn the Hollow anyway, out of spite, and then they'll wire me anyway when the new head comes up from the Organum in the spring."

He said nothing. She was right. He had known she was right before he said it. He had said it anyway, because it was the plan in which only he went into the dark.

"No," Lyra said, and took the chalk. "Listen. This is what we do."

She wrote it out, and whispered it, slowly, in the ruin of her voice, with her other hand resting on the broken core of Tamsin Reed. As she did, he understood that she had been working it out since the warrant was read — standing at the mouth of Cooper's Row, with three hundred people staring at her and two Legionnaires taking up their places at her shoulders.

*1. YOU SIGN.* "You're the supervising engineer. The Consecrated hand. The Inquisitor gets what he wants, the Factor gets her petition, and the fire waits until after the Rite. That buys the Hollow past Hearthday."

*2. THE CARDERS GO ON THE SHIP. AND ANYONE ELSE HE'LL CARRY.* "I'll make him take more than three. I don't care what it costs. There's the purse, and there's me, and I'll sing at his funeral for free."

*3. THE COIL GOES DOWN IN YOUR TOOLBAG.* "With your instruments. On Hearthday. Nobody searches the supervising engineer's toolbag."

*4. THEY BRING ME DOWN TO THE CASING THEMSELVES.* "With the collar. Because I'm the voice."

She looked up at him, and wrote the last line slowly.

*5. YOU'RE THE HAND AT THE WIRE. SO DON'T THREAD IT.*

The hush-lamp whistled. Over the rooftops, in the Chapel, the Pure Note went on and on and on.

"The Inquisitor will be standing over me," Kaelen breathed. "With a censer. He has done this before. He will be watching the needle."

"Then don't let him see it. You've the finest ear for failure in forty years. You wrote me so yourself, when we were eighteen and you were insufferable." Her whisper cracked straight across, and she went on through the crack. "Put the collar on me. Make it look right. Leave the wire out, or thread it wrong, or whatever you have to do so that I can still sing my own note. And when the bore comes to the casing, you seat your coil in its port, and I sing against it. Both of us. From the inside."

"And if I get it wrong?"

"Then you'll have got it wrong," said Lyra, "and I'll have a wire in my throat, and at least it will have been your hand that put it there, and not somebody who doesn't know how to be sorry." She put her hand over his, on the table, over the broken glass. "You don't get to carry this one alone. We said so. Rootday."

He looked at her hand.

There were three things the engineer in the back of his head wanted to say. That the plan had nine points of failure he could count without trying, and probably thirty he could not. That the Inquisitor had heard him hum under his breath on a gantry, through forty voices, for three hundred days, and would not miss a needle. That on the ninth morning at the Dawning he had come down from that gantry and seen blood in a girl's collar and flinched, and gone back up, and never looked again — and that if he set a collar around Lyra Ashgrove's throat in front of the Inquisitor, he did not know whether his hands would be steady, or whether, for the first time in his life, they would not.

He said none of them.

"The Carders go first," he whispered instead. "Before anything. Before I sign. I want to see them on that ship."

"The Carders go first," Lyra agreed.

"And the pirate—"

"Leave the pirate to me."

She wiped the slate clean with her sleeve, slowly, line by line, until there was nothing written on it at all.

---

On Driftday afternoon, from the North Gate, he watched a small figure in a dead man's cut-down shirt climb the mast-ladder of the *Drift-Kestrel* behind a woman built like a ship's hawser, his stiff little hands locked around her neck, and go aboard. A while later a thinner figure followed, climbing slowly, looking down at every rung. Nell Carder did not like heights. She climbed anyway.

He signed the Factor's paper at dusk that day, in the Greenhall, under the pale green seal of the dead Queen.

His father was there. The Inquisitor was there, in red, with the censer hanging at his side. The Factor was there with her lenses, watching his pen. He wrote *K. Vance* on the line beside *Supervising Engineer*, and on the line beside *Collar & Wire* he wrote it again, and his hand was perfectly steady, because that was what he had instead.

Across the table, his father let out a breath that Kaelen had never heard him let out before. It sounded like relief. It sounded like a man who has been holding a door shut against a storm for seven years and has just heard the wind drop.

Aldric looked at his son, and his eyes were wet, and he nodded once, as if to say: *There. There, now. You've come home.*

Kaelen could not look at him.

The Inquisitor lifted the censer and swung it once over the signed paper in blessing, and the chain creaked at the top of its arc.

Creak.

Outside, in the square, the four cages of glass-fire burned white-gold on their staves in the falling dark, and over the rooftops the Pure Note went on without end. Along the tree-line beyond the Hedge, the jitter paced. In the Hollow, eighty-four souls had been given until after the Rite. On Cooper's Row, two Legionnaires stood at a door with their bells lifted to the sky, murmuring, and behind the door a woman with a ruined voice sat at her father's table with a wiped slate in front of her, planning.

Four days.

Creak.

And under the felt of his sleeve, very softly, his arm began to keep time.

---
---

---
---

## PART TWO: THE ROOT

---

## CHAPTER EIGHT
### Cargo

> *Item: engram cores, standard, in straw. Twelve crates. Consignee: Oakhaven Works, Carillon garrison. The consignment is not to be opened in transit. The Office is aware that carriers sometimes hear the consignment. Carriers who report hearing the consignment will not be engaged again.*
>
> — Bill of lading, Office of the Catalogue

*The* Drift-Kestrel*, at the Oakhaven mast. Driftday night.*

---

There were twelve crates in the hold of the *Drift-Kestrel*, and now there was also a woman and a boy.

Mags had brought them up the mast-ladder at three in the afternoon in full view of the quarry, which was, Vane had explained at some length in hand-cant, the single stupidest thing anyone had ever done aboard his ship. Mags had watched his hands to the end, with the patience of a woman letting weather happen, and then signed back: *Nobody looks up.*

She was right, of course. That was the infuriating part. In twenty-five years of carrying things he should not have been carrying, Vane had learned that nine-tenths of smuggling is knowing that people only ever search at the level of their own eyes. Nobody in the Oakhaven works had looked up at a mooring mast all week. They had a Rite to build and a Church to feed and a bore four days from the casing, and a small boy going up a ladder a hundred feet above their heads was about as visible as a bird.

He went down into the hold at dusk to lay out the rules.

Nell Carder sat on a coil of cable with her back against a crate, and her son lay asleep with his head in her lap and his hands held out stiff in front of him, even sleeping. In the lantern-light she looked like a woman who had not stopped moving in four days and had just realized she could.

"Three rules," said Vane. "One. You don't go on deck. Not for air, not for the view, not if the ship's on fire. Two. If anybody comes aboard — anybody — you go in there." He pointed at the sail-locker, a triangular cupboard in the bow stuffed with spare canvas. "Both of you, fast and quiet, and you stay until Mags comes for you. Not me. Mags. If I come for you it means somebody's watching me."

Nell nodded. She was looking at the crates.

"Three," said Vane. "Don't let the boy listen to the cargo."

Her eyes came up. "What's in them?"

"Glass," said Vane.

It was the third time he had said it that week, and it went down worse every time, like a drink you keep ordering out of spite.

---

The paymaster's clerk came up the ladder an hour later.

He was a narrow young man in Consortium drab with ink on his cuffs and a tally-book under his arm, and he climbed the hundred feet without once looking down, which impressed Vane against his will, and arrived on deck already talking.

"Captain. The Factor's compliments. There's been a delay in the invoice — no, don't, I've heard it, I agree with you, I've said as much to the Factor herself — but I'm to tell you the garrison will take delivery on Hearthday. After the Rite." He consulted the tally-book. "The Legion's quartermaster wants the crates counted before he signs. I'm to count them now so there's no argument later."

"Now," said Vane.

"Now."

Mags was at the hatch. Vane looked at her for one long second, and she looked back, and then she went down the ladder ahead of the clerk at a pace that could plausibly be called helpfulness and was in fact the fastest Vane had ever seen her move.

He gave her a count of twenty. He gave it to her by talking: about the wind, about the state of the Reach, about a tariff dispute in Port Cantabile that he invented as he went, about the criminal price of drift-glass. The clerk waited it out with the polite glaze of a man used to being talked at by captains, and then went down the ladder into the hold.

The sail-locker door was shut. The coil of cable was empty. There was, Vane noticed with a lurch, a child's boot lying on the deck-boards by the third crate, and he put his own boot over it and left it there.

The clerk counted. Twelve. He wrote twelve in his tally-book.

And in the quiet, as he wrote, the hold murmured.

It was doing what it always did: the man counting, the woman giving directions to somewhere, the laughter at a joke told long ago. In the lantern-light the clerk's pen did not so much as pause.

"They do carry on, don't they," he said, pleasantly, and shut the book.

Vane said nothing at all.

"My first posting was the harvest-house at Whisper-Grove," the clerk said, tucking the book under his arm. "Three years. You get used to it. My mother won't have a glass in the house now, not even a drinking glass, but that's mothers." He smiled, and it was a perfectly ordinary smile, the smile of a young man who has come up a ladder and done his job. "Hearthday, then, Captain. After the Rite. Oh — and the mail-cart brought this up from the Greenroot road. It's been chasing you a fortnight."

He handed over a letter, and climbed back down the ladder, and did not look down that time either.

---

Vane took the letter to the stern rail and read it by the glow of the lift-tanks.

It was in Oona Salt's own hand, which meant she had not wanted a scribe to see it, which meant nothing good. The Harbor-Queen of Port Cantabile wrote the way she spoke, which is to say as though every sentence cost her money.

*Vane.*

*Four hundred and eighty sovereigns against the Kestrel, and you have paid me sixty since the spring. I have been patient because patience is cheaper than lawyers. I am not patient now. Bring me two hundred by the turn of the season or bring me the ship.*

*Also: a woman came to the Salt Stair asking after you. Pale. Came in off the water in the evening, which nobody does. Eyes gone violet, the way they go on people who have been down where the trenches open. She was very polite. She asked which captain it was who cut his tow over the trench off Cape Dissonance in the year of the Roaring Tide, and where he might be berthed these days.*

*She paid in old coin. She paid a great deal.*

*I told her you were up in the Reach on the Catalogue's runs.*

*Business is business, Vane. You'd have done the same, and you have.*

*— O. Salt*

He stood at the rail for a while with the letter in his hand and the wind coming up off the quarry, smelling of wet stone and cut wood and, underneath, the sweetish mineral stink of the Root.

*She paid in old coin.*

Dead men's money, they called old coin on the Rust Coast. Bottle-money. What you got when you sold something to somebody who had been in the water too long.

The sensible thing, obviously, was to cut the mast-line.

He worked it out standing there, the way he had worked out every escape of his adult life: she could not be more than three or four days behind the letter, and the *Kestrel* had lift for two days' hard running north-east if he dumped the water, and the Iron Tramway valleys would hide a small ship from a coastal woman who did not know the inland lanes, and by the time anyone at the works had finished arguing about invoices he would be over the Peaks. He had a boy and a woman aboard who could be set down at any village with a purse and a story. He had twelve crates that were not his. He had a Cantor with a ruined voice and a glass-armed man who had paid him in the Consortium's own scrip to do a thing that was going to get everybody involved hanged, and he owed neither of them anything at all beyond eight days of berths, which were, if you looked at it squarely, only four days more.

He turned the letter over and looked at the back of it, which was blank, and thought about a woman standing in the door of a tavern on the Salt Stair with the evening behind her, asking politely.

Then he went below to check the cargo, because he always did, at night.

---

Pim Carder was sitting on the deck-boards between the third and fourth crates with his ear against the pine.

The lantern was where his mother had left it, hanging on the hook by the ladder, and Nell was asleep on the cable-coil with her mouth open and the exhaustion of four days on her, and her son had got up without waking her, the way children do, and crossed the hold in the dark, and put his head against a crate, and was talking to it.

"—no," he was whispering. "No, that's the sound the ship makes. It does it all the time. It isn't anything." A pause, his head tilted, listening. "I *know*. But it's not dark-dark. There's a lamp. I'll leave it on, if you want."

Vane stood on the ladder and could not move.

"Boy," he said.

Pim looked up, unstartled, the way he might have looked up from a game.

"Her name's Annet," he said. "She's six. She's scared of the dark."

Vane came down the last two rungs.

"She kept saying *hello, hello, is anyone there*," said Pim. "She's been saying it a long while, I think. So I said hello. And she asked what my name was, and I said Pim Carder, from the Hollow." His face changed then, all at once, and the child came up through it, and he looked at Vane with real fear for the first time since he had come aboard. "Was that wrong? Lyra says you never answer a voice you can't see. But she's *in a box*. She's not in the dark outside, she's in a box on a ship, and she's six, and nobody's said anything to her for — I think for a long time."

Vane looked at the crate. Stencilled on its side, in black, in letters he had been not-reading for two years:

OFFICE OF THE CATALOGUE • RESONANT ENGRAMS • HANDLE IN SILENCE

"Go and sit with your mother," he said.

"Will you tell her I'm still here? Annet? If she asks?"

"Go and sit with your mother, Pim."

The boy went. Vane waited until he heard him settle, and the small sounds of a child pretending to sleep, and then he took the lantern off its hook and set it on the floor and got the pry-bar out of the locker under the ladder, and opened the crate.

It came up easily. Consortium nails, driven by clerks.

Straw. Good straw, packed by somebody careful. And bedded in it, in four rows of nine, thirty-six spheres of pale lilac glass, each the size of a small apple, each tied with a paper tag on a string.

He lifted one out.

It was cold, and lighter than it looked, and there was a fog inside it that moved a little when he turned it, like breath on a window. The tag was written in a fine clerk's hand, the kind of hand they teach at a Catalogue desk: **WG-41,004.**

He put it down. He lifted another: **WG-41,005.** Another: **WG-41,006.**

WG. Whisper-Grove.

Numbered in the order they were taken.

He knelt on the boards of his own hold with a lantern and thirty-six numbered people in front of him, and he lifted out the one from the corner of the second row, which was the one nearest where a boy's head had been resting against the pine, and he held it up to the light.

*—hello,* said the small voice in the glass, very close and very politely. *Hello? Pim? Are you still there? I don't mind about the lamp. I only wondered.*

He did not laugh.

That was how he knew how bad it was. He had laughed when the trench opened under the *Wave-Cutter* and he had laughed in the Boar & Dulcimer with a hound's mouth coming at his boots, and he had laughed at his own reflection in a brothel mirror in the Screaming Year with a girl dying in the next room, because that was the thing his body did when it was more frightened than it could hold.

He sat down on the floor of the hold with his back against a crate and the core of a six-year-old girl in his two hands, and he did not make any sound at all.

---

Mags came down at some point. She looked at the open crate, and at the tags, and at him, and sat down on the bottom rung of the ladder with her forearms on her knees.

Vane put the core back into the straw, in its place, in the corner of the second row. He was careful about it. Then he lifted his hands and signed.

*We're not delivering.*

Mags watched his hands. Then she looked at the crates for a while. Then she signed, slowly:

*Twelve crates. Four hundred-odd heads. Where do they go?*

*Whisper-Grove,* Vane signed. *Back where they came from. There's a scribe, they say. The one who wrote the catalogue. If anyone alive knows how to put a name back in a head, it's whoever took it out.*

*They say.*

*They say.*

Mags spat, neatly, into the bilge, which Vane had asked her a hundred times not to do.

*The Consortium will hunt us,* she signed.

*Yes.*

*The Catalogue will hunt us.*

*Yes.*

*The Church too, if the Cantor's business goes wrong.*

*Almost certainly.*

Mags considered this. Then she signed, without any particular emphasis, the way she might have noted a change in the wind: *Good.*

She had a sister, he remembered. Somewhere. She had mentioned it twice in nine years, both times drunk, both times in a hand-cant so fast he had not been entirely sure he'd read it. He did not ask.

*How many people can we lift?* he signed instead. *With the crates aboard. Get Pennick.*

---

Pennick did the figures on a slate by the lift-tanks, twice, and then a third time because Vane did not like the answer.

"Six," he said. "Seven if it's children, and if they're not — you know. Eating." He rubbed his eyes with the heels of his hands. He had not slept properly in three days; the tanks had been singing sour again. "With the cargo aboard, Captain, six. That's with the water down to a day and no ballast at all, and if the wind's foul over the Peaks we'll be crawling."

"The Cantor wants twenty."

"The Cantor," said Pennick, "is not an engineer."

"She's a Cantor. They think in *ought*." Vane looked at the slate. Six. Eighty-four souls in the Hollow, and nineteen of them children, and a warrant with a date on it. "What goes over the side?"

Pennick blinked. "Captain?"

"To get to twenty. Tell me what goes over."

The boy stared at him, and then, slowly, began to write. Water, most of it. The spare envelope canvas. The chain cable and half the anchors. The brandy, which was eleven cases and worth more than the rest of the ship's contents together. The iron ballast in the keel-sling, which was what kept her from flying like a leaf in a squall, and which meant they would ride high and mean and ugly and anyone with a decent glass would see they were running light.

"And the long gun," Pennick finished, not looking up. "The swivel, and the carriage, and the shot. Eleven hundredweight. That's the last of it, and even then it's — it's eighteen, Captain. Maybe nineteen. It isn't twenty."

Vane looked at the swivel gun in its tarpaulin on the foredeck. He had bought it in Driftwood Cay eleven years ago with the first real money he ever made, and he had fired it nine times, and it had saved his life twice, and the *Kestrel* without it was a fat brass goose over open country.

"Put it over the side," he said.

"Now?"

"Tomorrow, in the dark, over the slag-pit, so nobody hears the splash." He handed the slate back. "Eighteen it is. Tell the Cantor eighteen and let her argue me up to nineteen; she'll want to think she's won something."

Pennick took the slate. He looked at it, and then at the captain, as though he were recalculating something that had nothing to do with lift.

"Aye, Captain," he said.

---

Later, when the ship was quiet and the Pure Note came drifting up faintly from the town below — they had been singing it over Oakhaven for two days now, one note, without a break, and you could hear it even up here, thin and holy and endless — Vane went down into the hold one last time.

Pim was awake. Of course he was.

"Captain?"

"Boy."

"Is Annet coming with us?"

He could have said a great many things. He had made a career of saying a great many things, and most of them had worked.

"Yes," said Vane.

"Where to?"

"Home," said Vane. "Whisper-Grove. We're taking the lot of them home, and there's a woman there who's going to give them back their names, and I'm going to make her do it if I have to carry her up the ladder myself."

Pim thought about this with great seriousness.

"You should tell her," he said. "Annet. She'd like to know. She can't hear me unless I'm right up close."

So Captain Vane of the *Drift-Kestrel*, forty-one years old, master of a stolen ship and a hold full of stolen people, got down on the boards of his own hold in the lantern-light, and put his mouth close to the pine of the third crate, and said:

"Annet. You're going home."

Then he went up on deck, and took Oona Salt's letter out of his coat, and held a corner of it in the lantern flame until it caught, and dropped it over the rail, and watched it fall a hundred feet into the dark, burning, until the wind put it out.

He had cut one line in his life.

He found, somewhat to his surprise, that he was not going to cut another one tonight.

---
---

## CHAPTER NINE
### Beneath

> *The chosen voice shall not speak from the third day, that the instrument be not worn. She shall be given honey and raw egg. She shall be washed. She shall be told that she is fortunate.*
>
> — Rubric of the Rite of Unison

*Oakhaven. Hearthday.*

---

They did not let her speak for the last four days.

It was in the rubric, the sisters explained, on the first morning: the chosen voice is not worn out on ordinary speech. They were two of them, Church women in grey with gold at their collars, and they came up from the Chapel at first light on Memoryday with a basket, and the Legionnaires at the door stood aside for them, and after that Lyra's house was not hers.

They brought honey in a stone pot, and raw eggs, and a little bottle of throat-oil that smelled of almonds, and a slate, and they were kind. That was the part she could not get past afterward. They were not cruel once. They warmed the honey in their hands before they gave it to her. When she coughed in the night, one of them sat up with her and rubbed her back. They called her *daughter*, and they moved about her father's kitchen with the easy competence of women who have prepared a great many instruments, and every few hours, at intervals that she came eventually to understand were also in the rubric, one of them would stop what she was doing, and touch Lyra's hand, and say:

"You are very fortunate."

The first time, Lyra wrote on the slate: *I KNOW.*

The fourth time, she wrote: *DO YOU SAY THAT TO ALL OF THEM?*

The sister read it, and smiled, and wiped the slate with her sleeve, and said, "You are very fortunate," and went back to the eggs.

Over the rooftops, day and night, the Pure Note went on.

That was the other thing. It had not stopped since Dawnday and it would not stop until the Rite: thirty voices in the Chapel passing one note between them, breath by breath, forever. In the first hours the town had come out to stand in the square and listen to it with their hands over their mouths. By the second day nobody was sleeping. By the third, people had begun to plaster their windows with wet cloth and stuff wool in their ears, and children were crying in the lanes for no reason they could name, and old Bettany had come to the door and been turned away by a bell-helm, and gone home and put her head in her apron.

Lyra lay awake under it. It was not a loud sound. It was worse than loud. It was a lid.

---

Surgeon-Grafter Rooke came on Nullday, on the excuse of examining her throat, which he did, thoroughly, with a lamp and a little mirror, and pronounced it — with a flat glance at the sisters — "adequate for four hours, if she is not asked to speak before."

Then he snapped his bag shut, and said, "Cantor, your hands," and took them, and held them in the way a physician holds hands, turning them to the light, and while the sisters were busy at the hearth he read what she had written on her palm in ink an hour before.

He read it twice. His mouth went thin.

*IF THE FIRE COMES BEFORE I'M BACK — ORLA HALE — THE LONG DROPS. LET HER GO QUIET. DON'T LET THEM BURN HER IN THE CHAIR.*

He rubbed his thumb once across her palm, which smudged it into nothing, and put her hands back in her lap, and stood.

"I have never in my life heard of a Cantor dying of a four-hour Hold," he said, to the room in general, "and I have been thirty years in the Office." He picked up his bag. "Good day, sisters. Cantor."

At the door he stopped, with his back to the sisters, and looked at her, and gave her, very slightly, a nod.

It was the only promise anyone made her all week, and it was made with a thumb and a nod, and she held onto it for four days.

---

Kaelen came once, on Rootday night, because the Rite's supervising engineer had cause to see the Rite's voice, and the Consortium's green glass opened every door in Oakhaven.

The sisters withdrew to the far end of the kitchen and did not leave. Lyra sat at her father's table with her slate, and he sat across from her, and for a while neither of them wrote anything.

Then he took the chalk and wrote: *THE BAG IS PACKED.*

She nodded.

*8 BOLTS ON THE PORT PLATE. 40 SECONDS IF THEY DON'T WATCH ME. 3 MINUTES IF THEY DO.*

She took the chalk and wrote: *THEN DON'T LET THEM WATCH YOU.*

He almost smiled. She saw it happen and not finish.

He wrote: *THEY'LL BRING YOU DOWN AT NOON. THE HOLD STARTS WHEN THE HEAD IS ONE SPAN OFF THE CASING. I'LL BE AT THE DRUM ON THE COLLAR WALK. WHEN I MOVE, SING.*

*WHAT DO I SING?*

He looked at the slate for a long moment. Then: *THE FALLING FIFTH. YOUR ONE. THE ONE YOU USE ON THE BOY'S HANDS.*

Lyra stared at the words. Of course. Of course it was that. The little village trick from the margin of her father's notebook, the half-step let down before the string complains — used not on a child's fingers but on a machine the size of a chapel, at the bottom of a shaft, against the Iron Synod and the Church of the Pure Note and a god in a stone.

She wiped the slate and wrote: *IT'S A SMALL SONG.*

*IT'S THE ONLY ONE THAT'S EVER WORKED,* he wrote.

Then he sat looking at her throat for slightly too long, the way a man looks at a road he is going to have to drive a cart down in the dark, and she saw his jaw begin to set, and she took the chalk out of his fingers and wrote, in large letters:

*DON'T SHAKE.*

He read it. He did not look at her.

She wiped it away before the sisters could come and see.

---

They dressed her at dawn on Hearthday.

Not her clothes. A shift of white wool, new, with the seams turned so they would not chafe; the sisters were very particular about the seams. They took her boots and gave her sandals of pale leather. They took her shawl — she had had it eleven years; it had been her mother's — and folded it and set it on her father's chair, and one of them said, kindly, "It will be here when you come back."

They washed her hair. They combed it out. They oiled her throat with the almond oil, working it into the muscle with their thumbs, and buckled around her neck a gorget of thin gold, not the collar, only the gorget, which the rubric required, "that the Light may see the instrument."

Then one of them touched her hand and said, "You are very fortunate," and Lyra found she was not angry anymore. She looked at the woman and understood something with perfect clarity: that this woman believed it; that she had said it in this room for four days and would say it again next year in another room to another girl; and that she was not a monster but a pair of warm hands and a stone pot of honey, and that this was precisely how the thing was done.

Then they took her out into the road.

---

The whole town had come.

That was not the Church's doing. The Church had wanted a procession and had got one: the choir first, holding the Note, and the Flambeaux with their staves of glass-fire in the morning light, and the Inquisitor with the censer, and behind them the Governor in his chain and the Factor in her green lenses, and then Lyra in white between two Church sisters, with two bell-helmed Legionnaires walking at her shoulders.

But the people had come on their own. They lined Cooper's Row and the length of the market road and stood three deep at the mouth of the tannery lane, in their working clothes, on a Hearthday morning when there was work to do, and they did not cheer, because you do not cheer in Oakhaven.

Somewhere by the well-house, an old woman began to hum.

It was very quiet. It was exactly a hand's width of voice, the width the whole town had been taught since the Hedge went up, and it was the Root note — one hundred and seventy-four, the note of the Reach, the note that had been in the ground under their feet since before there were feet.

The dye-works women took it up. Then the carters. Then the men from the tannery, and the baker's wife with flour to her elbows, and Bettany, and Hetty Bram standing in the doorway of the Boar & Dulcimer with her arms folded, and the three Tanner cousins, and old women on stools at the crossings who had been humming all night anyway and now hummed something else.

Under the Church's one enormous holy note, the town of Oakhaven hummed its own, in the Narrow, so softly that no one more than ten feet away could have sworn to it.

The Flambeaux could not do anything about it. That was the beautiful part. There was nothing to punish. It was quieter than speech. It was quieter than praying. It went along the road with her, passed from doorway to doorway, a low warm ugly homely note under the pure one, and Lyra walked through the middle of it in her white shift with her throat oiled and her hair combed out, and wept without making a sound, which was a thing Oakhaven women had learned to do.

At the tannery lane, Lissa Merrow stood at the front with her bandaged forearms at her sides, humming with her mouth shut, and did not look away.

---

The quarry was full of people and iron.

They had built a stair of scaffold down the terraces and hung it with white cloth; they had swept the whole upper terrace; the censer-platform stood ready with its brazier lit. The night shift and the day shift had both been kept on, and they stood along the rails in their drab with their caps off. Along the top terrace, a line of Legionnaires. In the middle of it all, in front of the headframe, on a table with a cloth over it, lay the case of black leather with the Church's sunburst on the lid.

A young man in Consortium drab carried it down the scaffold stair ahead of her, with both hands, the way you carry something at a funeral.

He caught her looking at it, and went scarlet, and then — because he was young, and because nobody had told him not to — he said, "It's an honor to meet you, ma'am. Truly. I'm Tobiah Marsh. I'm on the night shift."

She could not answer. She had not spoken in four days.

"My mother used to take me to hear the Sky Choir," he said, in a rush, keeping pace. "Before. On the Pilgrims' Stair at dawn. When I heard it was to be a Cantor from the Reach I told everyone I'd — well. It's a great honor, ma'am. For all of us." He hesitated. "They say it doesn't hurt very much. After the first part."

Lyra looked at him, at his freckles and his sleepless eyes and his careful hands on the black case, and thought, with a clarity that surprised her: *You are going to remember this morning for the whole of your life, and one day it is going to take the skin off you.*

She smiled at him, because there was nothing else she could give him, and he went scarlet again, and carried the case on down.

---

The cage took them down four hundred feet, and the world changed twice on the way.

First the stone, sliding past in the lamplight, cut and drilled and rung with old quarry-marks. Then clay, grey and sweating, so close she could have touched it. And then the shaft opened, and they came down into the chamber, and Lyra forgot to be afraid, because she had never in her life seen anything so alive.

It was a room made out of a root.

The wall of it curved up and over them like the inside of a bell, grey-brown and ridged, and every ridge had veins in it — green-gold, glowing, thick as her arm at the bottom and fine as thread at the top — and the veins pulsed. Slowly. In, and out. The air was warm and wet and so full of the smell of sap that it coated the back of her mouth like syrup, green and sweet and dizzying, and under the sap was the sharper smell of hot iron.

And in the middle of it hung the bore.

She had seen it from the terraces as a black shape on chains. Down here it was a machine the size of a barn, and its head was buried in the wall of the Root to the collar, and it was turning, and where it went in, the Root bled.

The glass came out slow and thick. It ran down the grey-brown ridges in a broad tongue, guttering into the channels cut in the platform floor, and along the channels to the pumps, where a dozen men in grey smocks worked the handles in pairs, up and down, up and down, with a rhythm like clockwork.

One of them was an old man with white hair. One of them was perhaps nineteen. One of them had a wedding ring on and a scar through his eyebrow, and his lips were moving.

Nobody looked at them. Not the Church, not the Consortium, not the Legionnaires. They were part of the machinery, and you do not look at a pump.

Lyra broke away from the sisters before anyone could stop her, and crossed four yards of platform, and put her hand on the wrist of the man with the wedding ring.

He did not stop pumping. He did not look at her. But she was a Cantor, and her hands knew a body's business the way a carpenter's know wood, and she felt what was underneath: a pulse, and warmth, and a small arrhythmia in his breathing she could have named if she had been allowed to speak.

So she hummed instead.

Barely anything. Not even a note, with her throat as it was — a thread of the Root note, breathed rather than sung, straight down into the bones of his wrist.

The man's lips stopped moving.

For about the length of a breath, he was *there*. She felt it come up through his arm the way you feel a fish take a line. His head turned an inch toward her. His eyes, which had been as empty as a pond, made an effort — an enormous, terrible effort, like a man trying to lift a cart off himself — and something came up behind them nearly to the surface.

Then the sisters had her by both arms and were drawing her back, murmuring *daughter, daughter, the instrument, mind the instrument*, and the man's lips began to move again, and he went on pumping, up and down, up and down.

"Interesting," said Factor Vosk, from the rail. She had her green lenses on and she was looking, not at the man, but at Lyra. "Did you see that, Inquisitor?"

"I saw a Cantor be gracious to a tool," said Ignatius Crane, "which is an error, but a small one." He lifted the censer. "Bring her to the casing."

---

They set a stool for her on the casing platform, ten feet from the bore's collar, where the chain-drums came down and the noise was so great that men had to shout into each other's ears.

And there, sitting on a stool in a white shift with her hands in her lap, with the grinding of the Carrow Bore filling the whole world, Lyra Ashgrove heard, for the first time, what was underneath it.

She had known there would be something. Kaelen had told her, on Rootday, in a voice that made it clear he had never said it aloud to anyone. *There's something in it. I can hear it through the glass. It breathes.*

He had not told her — perhaps he had not known how — that it was singing.

It came up through the platform and through the soles of her feet and into the bones of her legs: enormous, and slow, and so low that it was less a sound than a pressure behind her eyes. Under the grinding. Under the pumps. Under everything.

Five notes.

Down, and down, and up, and a little down again, and back to the beginning. Over and over. A phrase with a shape her hands knew before her mind did, the shape her father's hands had made in the air when he taught it to her in the reed-rows when she was four years old. A tending note held long, and let fall, and gathered.

It was a lullaby.

It was a *Cantor's* lullaby, or the thing that Cantors' lullabies had come down from — the one that every mother in the Reach hums over a cot without knowing where she learned it, the one you use when the child will not settle and you are too tired to be clever, the five easy notes that go around and around until somebody sleeps.

And it kept losing the tune.

That was the part that undid her. Every twelfth or thirteenth time around, one of the five notes would come late, or flat, or not at all, and the phrase would stumble, and there would be a pause — a terrible small pause, like somebody catching themselves at the top of a stair — and then it would start again, from the beginning, patiently.

She knew that sound. She had made that sound herself, in her own kitchen, at four in the morning, with her father dead a month and a feverish baby in her arms and nothing left in her at all, humming five notes over and over because they were the only five she could still hold onto.

She had been brought down four hundred feet in a white shift, with her throat oiled, to hold that steady. To pin it. So the bore could go deeper.

Lyra looked at the wound in the Root, and the tongue of glass coming out of it, and the men pumping it away, and understood three things in the space of about four heartbeats.

That the thing in the stone was not a spirit, and not a law, and not an engine.

That it had been singing a lullaby to something, or someone, or everything, for a very long time, and it was so tired it could not hold the tune.

And that, in a little while, they were going to ask it — in her voice — whether it could stop.

---

Above her, on the catwalk, the Inquisitor began the rubric.

"Holy daughter. You are the hand that steadies the patient. You hold the Light still, that it may be relieved of its excess." The censer swung; the chain creaked at the top of its arc. Somewhere above, at the shaft-head, four hundred feet up, the choir was still holding the Pure Note, and a thread of it came down the shaft like a wire. "Blessed is the single note. Blessed is the throat that holds it. Blessed is the hand that keeps the throat."

The black case was opened on a cloth beside her.

She saw the brass collar, and the four tuning pegs, and the ivory spool of copper wire, and the long silver needle curved like a new moon, lying in its own fitted groove.

"Engineer," said Ignatius Crane.

And Kaelen Vance came across the platform and knelt down on the boards in front of her stool and picked up the needle, and Lyra looked at his hands.

They were shaking.

---
---

## CHAPTER TEN
### The Siphon Casing

> *STAGE 9 — UNISON HOLD. On failure of the hold, for any cause, the Sequence is not to be halted. Slippage is to be absorbed by the source.*
>
> — Vance Sequence, Rev. 7, marginal note in an unknown hand

*The Root-vault, four hundred feet beneath the Oakhaven quarry. Hearthday, noon.*

---

His hands were shaking.

He knelt on the boards with the needle in his fingers and looked at them as though they belonged to someone else, which in a way they did; they were the hands that had signed a procurement order without reading it, and drawn the third revision of a calibration sequence in a rented room at twenty-four, and held a bulkhead against a flood of glass while three boys got into a drainage conduit. They had never once, in all of that, shaken.

Lyra was looking at him.

She could not speak; she had not spoken in five days; and she could not have been heard in any case over the grinding of the bore ten feet away. So she did the only thing left to her. She turned her head a little to the side, and lifted her chin, and offered him her throat.

*Get on with it.*

He got on with it.

"Closer, Engineer," said Ignatius Crane, from the catwalk. "I must be able to see."

Kaelen shifted a half-pace on his knees, angling his shoulder so that the Inquisitor had a clear view of the collar, and the needle, and the ivory spool — and not, if the light stayed where it was, of the place beneath the jaw where his left hand would be. The glass hand. He held it flat against the side of her neck, thumb along the line of the muscle, the way the Tuning Men had done it. The glass was cold. She flinched and made herself still.

He set the collar around her throat first, as the rubric required. Brass, cool, the width of her neck; the tuning pegs at the quarters; the two eyelets at the front. He drew it to the second notch, which was one notch looser than it should have been, and buckled it there.

Then he took the wire on the needle.

The Rite called for the needle to go in above the collar, at the notch of the throat, angled up and back into the fold of the voice itself — you learned the anatomy in the second year at the Conservatory, in a room with a sheep's larynx on a tray — so that the wire lay along the vibrating edge and could be tuned, and so that every breath after that came out at the one pitch and no other. That was the Rite. He had signed for forty collars and forty spools and forty needles, in one line on one order, among two hundred.

Kaelen put the needle in a fold of skin.

He did it fast, because slow would look wrong: caught a pinch of skin along the side of her throat under the shadow of her jaw with the glass thumb, drove the needle through it, brought it out, and drew the copper wire after it with a little jerk exactly like the one a Tuning Man would use to seat the wire on the fold. Lyra's whole body went rigid. Her hands closed on the edges of the stool until the knuckles went white. She did not make a sound, because she could not, and because she had spent four days learning not to.

Blood came at once. It welled up along the wire and ran down under the brass and made a bright line on the white wool of the shift.

*Good*, Kaelen thought, with the sick clarity of a man doing arithmetic. *It has to bleed.*

He knotted the wire at the left eyelet, and carried it across, and knotted it at the right, so that it lay in a shallow bight against her throat looking for all the world like a wire seated in a fold. He took the tuning key from the case and set the pegs — one, two, three, four — turning each a half-turn, and with every turn the wire tightened against the skin, and Lyra's eyes watered and did not close.

The Inquisitor came down the ladder from the catwalk.

He came all the way to the platform, and crouched, and looked at the collar from a foot away with his hands on his knees and the censer's chain wrapped around his fist. Kaelen did not move. He could hear his own arm ringing under the felt, high and fast.

"Blood," said Ignatius. He sounded satisfied. "Good. It should bleed a little at the seating. The Foundry girls used to bleed for days." He straightened. "Speak, daughter."

Lyra's eyes flicked to Kaelen's for the length of a heartbeat.

Then she opened her mouth, and what came out was not speech. It was a sound pressed flat, every syllable ironed onto one pitch, no rise and no fall, the way a voice comes out of a throat that has been tuned like a string — the way Kaelen had heard forty voices come out for three hundred days.

"I. Am. Fortunate."

One hundred and seventy-four, all the way through. Not a hair off.

Kaelen had to put the heel of his right hand against the boards for a moment.

"She is," said Ignatius Crane, pleased, and blessed her with two fingers, and went back up the ladder.

---

The Hold began at noon.

Tobiah Marsh called the mark from the gauge platform — "Head at one span! One span and holding!" — and the bore-master brought the chains down another notch, and the grinding changed its note, and up on the catwalk the Inquisitor lifted the censer.

"One note," he said. "One law. One hand."

The chain creaked.

And Lyra Ashgrove, sitting on a stool on a platform four hundred feet under Oakhaven in a white shift with blood on the collar, began to sing.

One hundred and seventy-four. Clear, and hoarse, and absolutely steady.
