Kaelen stood at the calibration rail with his toolbag at his feet and listened to her do it, and understood two things at once: that she was very good — better than any of the Foundry girls had been, because she was not a wired instrument but a Cantor, and a Cantor can hold a note the way a mason holds a plumb-line — and that she had four hours of it ahead of her with a torn throat and a wire through her skin, and that she would not stop, and that this was somehow worse.

The Root heard her in the first minute.

He felt it in the arm before the gauges saw it. The vast slow breathing under everything — the in, and the out, and the five-note lullaby stumbling around and around — checked. Hesitated. And then, very slowly, it began to move.

"Pitch coming up," said Tobiah, at the gauges, delighted. "It's coming up to her, sir. It's — gods, look at it — it's matching."

"Mark it," said the Factor from the rail, and wrote in her own book.

The lullaby stopped.

Kaelen stood at the rail with his hand on the cold iron and felt the thing in the stone stop singing to whatever it had been singing to for ten thousand years, and turn, and listen to the one small human voice holding its note for it. Holding it *steady*. Holding it *up*.

And he thought, with a horror that came up out of his stomach like cold water: *Oh. Oh, no. That's what this is.*

---

He had read Revision Seven four times.

He had read it in the Greenhall in front of the Factor, and twice at the burner's hut with a lamp and a rule, and once in the night-shift office with Tobiah's charts beside it, and he had read it as an engineer reads: for the load paths, for the failure modes, for the assumptions hidden in the arithmetic. He had noted where they had improved his mathematics. He had noted the small pair of scales in the margin, and not understood it.

He had noted, and not understood, the little line at the bottom of Stage Nine in the same small beautiful hand:

*On failure of the hold, for any cause, the Sequence is not to be halted. Slippage is to be absorbed by the source.*

He had read that as a note about the machine. Every engineer writes notes like that. *If the buffer fails, dump the load into the flywheel.* It had not occurred to him — it had not once occurred to him in five days — to ask what the *source* was in a stage whose source was a person.

He looked at Lyra on her stool, with the wire in her throat and the blood on her shift, singing.

If the hold failed, the Sequence would not stop. The slippage — the whole shuddering harmonic recoil of a bore in a living casing — would go into the source. Into her.

She was not the hand that steadied the patient.

She was the fuse.

And that was the small thing, the engineering thing, the footnote. The great thing was standing in the room with him, and he could feel it through his arm, and it was this: that no one had needed a Cantor to drill a hole. Three bore-heads had shattered this month and the Consortium had gone on drilling; slippage was a cost, and the Consortium was very good at costs. You did not bring a Church, a warrant, an Inquisitor who had officiated at the Dawning, and a wire from the Organum, four hundred feet down a shaft, to save yourself a bore-head.

You brought them to ask a question.

*Is it time?*

He had heard it asked once. He had heard what the asking cost. Somebody in the Arbiterium, with beautiful handwriting and a pair of scales for a signature, had read the reports of the First Fracture and had not seen a catastrophe at all. They had seen an experiment. And they had spent seven years refining it, and shipped it to a quarry in the Reach in a black case, to find out whether it would work twice.

The pitch on the gauge was coming up, and up, and steadying.

Kaelen picked up his toolbag and walked along the collar walk to the drum.

---

"Sir?" Tobiah, trotting after him. "Sir, the readings are perfect, they've never been—"

"Slippage on the lock," said Kaelen. "I can hear it."

He could not, and the boy would know that he could not; the lock was singing like a bell in tune. But Tobiah Marsh had been fourteen when he read *On the Calibration of Resonant Bores*, and had underlined whole paragraphs in two colors of ink, and there was nobody in the world he was less able to contradict.

"Yes, sir," said Tobiah. "What do you want?"

"Spanner. Sit on the rail there and call the pitch to me every ten seconds."

The plate over the phase port had eight bolts. He had promised himself forty seconds and he took fifty-one, kneeling on the collar walk with the drum's warm brass against his shoulder and the bore grinding a yard from his ear, and the whole time the boy sat on the rail calling numbers and Lyra sang and the Inquisitor's chain creaked and the Factor watched from the platform rail with her green lenses, and nobody stopped him, because he was the supervising engineer, and he was doing what supervising engineers do.

The plate came off. He laid it down quietly.

Inside: the timing gear of the Vance Sequence, brass and glass and hair-fine wire, the machine that told the bore when it might bite. It had been built from his drawings. He knew every tooth of it the way you know a word you have said ten thousand times.

He put his hand into his toolbag and took out the reverse-phase coil.

"Sir," said Tobiah. His voice had changed. "Sir, what's that?"

Kaelen looked up at him.

The boy was standing now, on the rail, with his charts under his arm and the face of someone watching the man out of the book kneel down in front of an open port with a thing in his hands that was not in any procedure he had ever been taught.

There was a moment — Kaelen thought about it afterward a great deal — when he could have said something. *Get down. Get to the cage. Go now and don't look behind you.* He had perhaps two seconds and one sentence.

He said, "You'll want to hold onto something."

And he seated the coil in the port.

---

The lock threw.

It made a sound that nobody in the chamber had ever heard, because there had never been one: the Carrow Bore's harmonic lock, which held the bit a hair's breadth beneath the pitch at which the Root wished to fail, went suddenly and violently out of phase with itself. The drum screamed. The chains came up taut and rang like a struck anvil, and the whole bore-head, thirty tons of iron with its teeth buried in living wood, tried to turn and could not, and the shaft of it twisted, and twisted, and the crown of glass teeth snapped off inside the Root with a crack that went through the chamber like a bell breaking.

Then everything happened at once.

The platform lurched. A chain-drum ran away with a shriek, spun its cable off the sheave and came down through the scaffolding, and the scaffolding went with it. Men were shouting. A glass channel split lengthwise and a tongue of green-gold glass went across the platform in a wave two inches deep, and where it touched the deck-boards the boards smoked and sang.

On the catwalk, the Inquisitor was on his knees with the chain wrapped round his fist.

"*The hold!*" he was shouting. "*Hold the note! Daughter — hold the note!*"

Because the note *was* still going. Kaelen heard it under the wreck of everything: one hundred and seventy-four, hoarse and thin, and unbroken, because she was a Cantor and the last thing a Cantor does is stop.

And the thing in the Root, which had drawn in and in and in until it was nearly the size of a question, stayed drawn in.

*Now*, thought Kaelen. *Now, or it does what the Light did.*

He got up off the collar walk, and put his glass hand on the drum, and shouted across the shaking platform in a voice he had not used since the Foundry:

"LYRA! *SING IT DOWN!*"

---

She let it fall.

He heard the moment the note bent: the Root note sliding down a whole fifth, slow as a leaf coming off a branch, in a torn hoarse voice with blood on the collar of her shift — and then climbing, and falling again, the small patient village trick out of the margin of a dead man's notebook. *Down. Go back where you came from.*

The chamber changed.

The Root's pitch, which had come up to her and locked there like a hand closing, *slipped*. The bore's teeth, already broken, lost what little purchase they had left. The whole vast presence in the stone — the listening, the drawing-in, the terrible attention of something ten thousand years old waiting to hear whether its relief had come — wavered.

And then, very slowly, like a man who has stood up too fast and sits down again, it let go of the question.

Through the glass in his arm, Kaelen heard the lullaby start up again. Badly. Stumbling. From the beginning, from the first of the five notes, the way you begin again when you have lost your place.

He shut his eyes for exactly one second, which was all he could afford, and then the Steam Golems came onto the platform.

---

They were haulage machines, not soldiers: brass and boiler-iron, twice a man's height, built to lift bore-heads and carry chain, with grapples for hands and a single dim glass eye. The Consortium kept four at the casing, and the Factor turned them all at once, which meant she had made up her mind about him somewhere between the coil going in and the drum screaming.

"*Engineer Vance*," said Adela Vosk from the platform rail, quite calmly, through a speaking-trumpet, "*step away from the drum.*"

The nearest Golem swung a grapple at him that would have taken his head off his shoulders.

Kaelen went under it and hit the thing in the knee-joint with his glass fist.

He had never fought anything in his life until three days ago, and he did not fight now. He broke things. He had spent ten years learning exactly where machines wish to fail, and a haulage Golem wishes to fail at the knee, where the steam-line crosses the joint; the glass went through the brass housing and the line let go and the whole machine sat down in a screaming cloud of white vapor and did not get up.

The second one caught him across the chest with a grapple and threw him eight feet into the platform rail.

Something went in his ribs. He got up anyway, because the platform was on fire in two places and Lyra was still on her stool ten feet from a broken bore-head with a wire through her throat, and he could hear, under everything, the note she was still making.

He drank the second Golem.

That was what it felt like, afterward, when he tried to think about it: he got his glass hand flat on the boiler and the arm *pulled*, the way it had pulled the bell out of a Legionnaire's helmet, and the machine's own working noise — the hammer of the piston, the shriek of the valve, the ring of brass under strain — went into him. The Golem shuddered and went quiet and dead, and Kaelen's arm rang so loud and so full that men on the far side of the platform put their hands over their ears, and something in his shoulder went cold, and the cold ran down into his chest.

*The tideline*, he thought, distantly. *That will have cost me.*

The third Golem stopped where it stood. So did the fourth.

"*Enough*," said the Factor. The green lenses were fixed on him with an expression he could not read at all. "Engineer, I would very much like to know what you thought you were—"

And then the speaking-tube on the platform stanchion began to shriek.

---

Every man on the platform knew that sound. It was the three-note scream of the purge alarm, which was tested on the first Rootday of every month and had never once been run in earnest, and the whole chamber turned toward it — the shift-men, the Legionnaires, the sisters, the Inquisitor on his catwalk.

Kaelen got to the stanchion first and pulled the flap open.

His father's voice came out of it, small and tinny, from four hundred feet above.

"—*sealing the vault. Governor's authority, under the works charter. Factor, I'm sorry. The bleed-back's coming up the shaft and it's coming fast and there's four hundred feet of men above it.*" A pause; the sound of somebody being argued with, a long way off. "*Sixty seconds on the reservoir gates. Anyone below, get to the cage.*"

Kaelen put his mouth to the tube.

"Da."

The silence on the other end lasted three of the sixty.

"*...Kaelen.*"

"Don't run the purge. The Root's wounded — if you dump forty thousand gallons of raw glass into an open casing it will not seal, it will—"

"*I know what it will do,*" said his father. "*I've a bore-master here telling me exactly what it will do, and I've a Factor's clerk telling me the other thing, and I've got Tam's widow's grandson on the day shift on the third terrace and I have to choose between a hole and a town.*" His voice did not rise. It had not risen in years. "*Get to the cage, boy. I'm counting.*"

"Da—"

"*Forty-one*," said Aldric Vance. "*Forty.*"

---

Afterward Kaelen could never lay the next part out in order, which was a thing that had never happened to him before. He had always been able to lay things out in order.

He remembered the Inquisitor coming down the ladder from the catwalk three rungs at a time with his red robes burning at the hem where the glass had run, shouting for the Rite, for the case, for the collar, for the daughter — and the Factor taking him by the arm and saying something short, and Ignatius Crane looking, for one instant, exactly like a man in a Foundry with a censer chain in his fist and everything going wrong.

He remembered Tobiah Marsh helping a shift-man with a crushed foot toward the cage, and turning, and looking back at Kaelen with his charts still under his arm.

He remembered getting the collar off Lyra's throat: the buckle at the second notch, and the wire coming out of her skin easily because it had never been anywhere but her skin, and the blood, and her face, and her saying something he could not hear over the alarm and the running feet, and then saying it again with her hand on his jaw, which was: *"The pumps."*

And the pumps.

There were twelve men on the pumps. The cage held eight. When the alarm went, the shift-men ran, and the Church ran, and the Legionnaires walked, and the Hollows kept pumping, because nobody had told them to do anything else, up and down, up and down, with a rhythm like clockwork, while the platform burned behind them.

He remembered Lyra in her white shift going along the line and taking them off the handles one at a time by the wrist, and each of them stopping when she touched him, the way a cart stops when you unhook the horse, and standing there. He remembered getting four of them into the cage with Tobiah and the crushed foot and the sisters, and the cage going up, and the bell ringing for the next, and the next not coming, because the winch-house had already been cleared.

He remembered the man with the wedding ring and the scar through his eyebrow looking at Lyra's hand on his wrist with his lips moving.

And then, four hundred feet above, at the top of the world, the reservoir gates opened.

---

It did not sound like a flood.

It sounded like the largest bell in the world being poured full of sand. Forty thousand gallons of raw Aether-Glass came down the shaft in a column, and the noise of it arrived first, and the light arrived with it — green-gold, rising up the chamber walls, bright as a summer field — and Kaelen grabbed Lyra by the arm and put her behind a chain-drum and got the glass arm up over them both.

The column struck the platform and went through it and hit the open wound in the Root.

And the Root drank.

That was the thing. That was the thing he could never explain afterward to anyone who had not been in the chamber. The raw glass did not smother the wound; it did not seal it, or cool it, or quench anything. It went *in*, all forty thousand gallons of it, into a hole that had been bored into the flank of a living thing, and for a heartbeat every vein in the walls of that chamber blazed like a lamp with the wick turned all the way up —

— and the thing in the stone, which had been singing a lullaby badly for ten thousand years, stopped.

Then it screamed.

It was not a sound. It came up through the boards and through his boots and through his teeth and through the glass in his arm, which rang so violently that the bones behind it shrieked, and four hundred feet above, in the town of Oakhaven, every iron-oak from the quarry road to the Flume rang at once like the bells they had taken. Lyra's hands were over her ears and her mouth was open. The chamber's walls moved. The ridged grey-brown flank of the Root, the wall that was the room, flexed like a flank — like something rolling over in its sleep — and the first crack opened along it from the wound to the roof, thirty feet of it, and out of the crack, instead of glass, came green.

Green shoots. Growing. Fast, and fast, and faster, in the dark, out of a wound four hundred feet below a town.

"Up," Kaelen said. "*Up* —"

And he got her onto the ladder of the service-stair with the Root coming apart behind them and the whole shaft above them beginning, very gently, to fill with leaves.

---
---

## CHAPTER ELEVEN
### The Greening

> *Growth is the kindest of the eight laws and the least merciful. It has no idea what it is doing. It only knows the word more.*
>
> — T. Ashgrove, Cantor's notebook, 398 SR

*Oakhaven. Hearthday afternoon.*

---

There were four hundred feet of service ladder between the Root-vault and the daylight, in a stone chimney a yard and a half across, and they went up it in the dark with the world growing underneath them.

Lyra climbed. Kaelen came behind her, which meant that when her hands failed on the fortieth rung and the sixtieth and the hundred and tenth, there was a glass shoulder under her boot, and a voice saying *up*, and nothing else, because he did not have breath for anything else.

The shaft below them was filling.

You could hear it. Not a roar — a roar would have been easier. It was a rustling. It was the sound a hayfield makes when the wind crosses it, except that it came from beneath her and it was getting closer, and every so often something would go past them in the dark: a tendril, rising up the wall of the chimney, pale as a root-hair and thick as a wrist, feeling its way toward the light with slow deliberate turns of its tip, the way a snail's horns turn.

She counted as she climbed. She could not help it. She had been counting since the cage went up without them.

*Twelve men on the pumps. Four in the cage. Eight left.*

Eight men in grey smocks standing at the handles four hundred feet down, working up and down, up and down, with nobody left on the platform to tell them anything else.

At the hundred and seventieth rung the chimney went suddenly warm, and green light came up past them, and Lyra looked down between her own boots for the first and last time.

The shaft beneath was full of leaves.

---

They came out through the winch-house into a world that had already ended and was extremely busy about it.

The quarry was a bowl of green.

It had been grey stone that morning. Now every terrace had split, and out of every split came growth — not the neat green of a field but the ugly hurrying green of something that has been held back and let go. Grass a foot high on the second terrace and three feet on the first. Brambles pouring over the tanker bays. A young iron-oak, already twice the height of a man, standing in the middle of the censer-platform with the white cloth of the Rite hanging off it like a torn sail.

The headframe was coming apart. Something underneath it — the shaft, the Root, the whole vast wounded thing below — was pushing up, and the iron legs of the frame were bending outward, very slowly, with a noise of rivets going like gunshots one at a time.

The day shift was running up the quarry road. The Legionnaires were not. Four of them stood at the gate exactly where they had been posted, bells lifted, with grass coming up through their pewter feet, because nobody had told them to do anything else.

"Cantor!"

Tobiah Marsh, at the rim, with his charts under his arm and half a shift-crew behind him, hauling an old man by the belt. He had lost his cap. He looked at Lyra, and at Kaelen behind her, and his face did something complicated and unfinished, and then he shouted, "The road's still open! It's going over the fields — the road's still—"

And then he was gone in the crowd, and the two of them came out onto the quarry road and saw the town.

---

Oakhaven was drowning in its own ground.

The Greening had gone up the quarry road ahead of them and it was going through the town from the north, a swell of green moving over roofs and walls the way water moves over a weir: not fast, exactly. You could walk faster. You could not walk faster for an hour.

The iron-oaks had doubled. That was the thing she could not make sense of at first, standing on the road with her hand over her mouth. The great trees on the common, which had been a hundred and fifty years old that morning, were now three hundred years old, and still going, their crowns swelling and splitting the sky, their roots heaving the common into long ridges like something shrugging under a blanket. Every one of them was ringing. The whole town rang like a struck carillon, on and on, and there was not one bell in it.

The Hedge was rising.

She had helped plant it, two summers ago, with the whole town on the bank and the Consortium's cartloads of wool. It had been three men deep and twice her height. It was four times her height now and thickening as she watched, blackthorn and bramble braiding themselves into a wall around the town the way bread rises in a pan, and the wall was closing over the North Gate.

The Hedge had been built to keep the town's sound inside.

"The gate," Lyra said, and could not hear herself. "*Kael* — the gate—"

"I see it."

---

They went down into the lanes.

Afterward she could never put the next hour into an order, but she kept the pieces, and the pieces did not go away.

The churchyard, heaving. The sexton's salt lay in white drifts along the paths, and it had not mattered at all; the grass over the graves stood waist-high and dark and unnaturally glossy, and the mounds beneath it had risen so that the ground looked like a bed with somebody stretching in it, and out of one of them, in the corner where the paupers went, something pale had come up through the turf that was not grass and not root and was still moving.

The bakery, with the door burst outward and dough coming down the step in a slow white tongue, and inside, the oven cracked open like an egg, and the baker's wife standing in the middle of it with her hands in her hair, saying *I punched it down, I punched it down, I punched it down.*

A carter on his knees in the middle of Weavers' Cross with his hands pressed to the cobbles, unable to lift them, watching the fine white root-hairs come out of his palms and go down between the stones and take hold. He was not screaming. That was the part she would keep. He was looking at his own hands with an expression of dreadful embarrassment, as though he had spilled something in somebody's house.

And the Hollow, when she reached it, where every person in Oakhaven who had ever had a root in them was —

— she did not have words for the Hollow. She had a Cantor's training and thirty-three years of the Reach and an entire language for the ways a body can go wrong, and standing at the mouth of the tannery lane she found that she had nothing at all.

She went in anyway.

---

She started singing at the well-head, because there was a crowd there and the green was coming up the lane behind them and they had nowhere to go.

The falling fifth. Full voice, torn, ugly, with the wire-hole still weeping under her jaw. The Root note, and down; and up; and down.

*Go back. Go back where you came from. Go back to being a road.*

The grass at her feet slumped.

It was not a miracle. It was a half-step let down, on a scale she had never in her life tried, and it cost her the way it had always cost her, and it worked in a circle perhaps eight feet across — a circle of stillness in a world that had stopped being able to stop — and where she walked, the circle walked with her.

"*Behind me!*" she shouted, between phrases, and her voice came out like a torn sheet. "In a line! Hands on the shoulder in front! *Behind me!*"

Hetty Bram got them into a line. Bettany got the children into the middle of it. The dye-works women came out of a doorway and took the old ones by the elbows, and Surgeon-Grafter Rooke came up the lane in his shirtsleeves with his bag in one hand and blood on both, and started pulling people out of doorways and pushing them into the column, shouting names.

And Lyra Ashgrove walked down the middle of her own town singing a corridor open, with sixty people in a line behind her and the green closing over the road behind the last of them, three feet back, the way a wake closes behind a boat.

She got them as far as the square.

---

"Orla," she said.

Rooke's face, up close, was grey. "Lyra—"

"You promised me you'd—"

"I could not get near her." He said it flatly, the way he said *past cutting*, and she saw what it cost him. "The floor's gone. The whole floor of that house is roots up to my knees and moving, and I am fifty-two years old and I could not get across it with a bag in my hand. I tried twice." He held up the little brown bottle of long drops, unbroken, unopened. "Take it. I'll hold the line here. Go on, girl. *Go.*"

She went.

The house with the blue door was gone under bramble to the lintel. Orla's, two doors along, was not. It was worse. The roof had come off it — not fallen; *lifted*, pushed up and aside by the thing that had been a chair — and the walls stood open around a tree.

It was a young tree, ten feet high, green-barked, shining, with a crown of tender new leaves that were still unrolling as Lyra came through the door frame, and it had grown out of the floorboards and up through the middle of Orla Hale's chair, and Orla was inside it.

Not crushed. *Included.* The bark had come up around her to the chest and closed there, and her hands were gone into the arms of the chair that were gone into the trunk, and roots the thickness of Lyra's wrist stood out of the floor around her like the buttresses of a chapel. Her grey hair was full of leaves. Her face was the only thing left that was hers.

Her eyes moved. They found Lyra, and lit.

"You're late, lovey," said Orla Hale.

Lyra crossed that floor. She did not remember how. There were roots in it that turned toward her boots as she came, and she stepped over them with the great care of someone stepping over sleeping dogs, and she got both hands on the old woman's face.

"Does it hurt," she said. It did not come out as a question. Her voice was almost gone.

"No." A rustling, delighted whisper. "I told you. It's like being held." Her eyes went past Lyra, up, at the missing roof, at the sky. "It's very green, isn't it. I can hear it growing. I can hear the whole Reach growing. Isn't that a sound."

The tree shivered. The bark at Orla's collarbone tightened by a finger's width, and the old woman's breath went out of her in a small grunt, and her eyes came back, and she said, quite clearly:

"Now, girl. *Now.* All the lines."

Lyra got the stopper out of the brown bottle with her teeth and gave her the long drops on her tongue, three of them, the way she had watched Rooke do it forty times.

Then she stood in the ruin of a house in the middle of a dying town, with her hands on either side of a face in the bark of a tree, and she sang the harvest home.

Full voice. Every line. *Oh, the last sheaf's in and the barley's high, and the swallows are low in the harvest sky.* Her throat tore on the third line and she went on through it and the sound that came out was barely human and it did not matter, because it was loud, and because Orla Hale's eyes were shut and her mouth had come open a little and she was listening, the way she had listened on the risers of fifty harvest homes to a whole town sing the thing she had started.

The fourth line, which Davin could never hold, and which his father could never hold, and which Orla Hale had held for fifty years in a voice like a rusty gate.

Lyra held it.

Somewhere around the sixth line, the old woman stopped breathing. Lyra finished the song. All of it. And then she put her forehead against the bark for the length of three breaths, which was all she had, because by then the hounds had heard her.

---

They came into the Hollow through the canopy.

Kaelen met the first one at the door of the house with his arm bare and blazing, and she did not see what he did to it, only the light of it go across the wall, and the sound — the sound she could not hear over her own ears ringing.

"Lyra." He had her by the wrist. "*Lyra.* The line's moving. Rooke's got them to the square and the square's going. The gate—"

"The gate's closed," she said.

"Then we open it."

---

They did not open it.

Four hundred people of the town of Oakhaven stood at the North Gate in the last clear ground left inside the Hedge — a lozenge of trampled mud maybe sixty feet across, with the green coming in on three sides at a walking pace, and the Governor's men and the Consortium's men and the Church's men all shoulder to shoulder in it with everybody else, which Lyra would think about for years.

And where the gate had been there was a wall of thorn thirty feet high and twenty feet thick, and it was still growing.

They went at it with axes. They went at it with hedging-bills and hay-knives and a Legionnaire's halberd somebody had pulled off a Legionnaire who was no longer using it. The thorn grew back faster than they cut, and after a while the cutting stopped, and the town stood in the mud in the green light and listened to itself breathing.

And the Hedge said:

"*Lark.*"

Lyra shut her eyes.

"*Lark, there's a way through down here. I can see it from this side. Come along the thorn to me, lark, and bring them with you.*"

Her father's voice. Warm, and a little hoarse, and patient.

Four hundred people turned their heads.

"*Tammy,*" said the Hedge, in old Tam's cracked tenor, from thirty feet further down. "*Tammy, there's a gap here. Bring the little ones first.*"

"*This way,*" said the Hedge, in Lyra's own voice — her whole voice, the one she had given it on forty-one nights on a stone behind a cooperage. "*This way. Follow me. I know the way out.*"

"*The last sheaf's in,*" sang the Hedge, very softly, in Davin Hale's voice, "*and the barley's high—*"

Somebody in the crowd made a sound like a hinge.

"*DON'T ANSWER IT!*" Lyra screamed.

It took everything she had left. It came out of her ruined throat like a plank splitting and she felt something tear that was not going to come back for a long time, and she got up on the mounting-block by the gatepost with Kaelen holding her by the belt so she could be seen, and she screamed it again.

"*DON'T ANSWER IT! IT ISN'T THEM! IT WAS NEVER THEM! WHATEVER IT SAYS, WHATEVER VOICE — DON'T ANSWER, DON'T GO TO IT, DON'T LET GO OF EACH OTHER—*"

And down at the north end of the thorn, Mistress Pellow said, "Tobin?"

She was fifty-one years old. Her youngest had had a marsh-fever two springs ago, and had been going quiet in her cot, going out like a lamp, and an orchard-cantor had stood up in that kitchen at four in the morning and said *Well, there's nothing for it*, and opened his mouth, and sung her back, and walked out into the yard singing so that the hounds would follow him and not the house. Mistress Pellow had put a bowl of new milk on Lyra's step every week of the two years since, and never once said why.

"Tobin Ashgrove," she said, to the thorn, in a perfectly ordinary voice, "is that you?"

"*Hello, Ada,*" said the Hedge.

She went into it.

The thorn did not part for her. That was the thing Lyra could not stop seeing afterward: it did not part. She climbed into it, with her arms up over her face, and it closed over her, and for four or five seconds there was the sound of a woman going into a hedge.

Then there was no sound at all.

And then, from somewhere on the other side, in the voice of Ada Pellow, the Hedge said: "*Oh — it's easy, come on. It's easy from this side.*"

Nobody else moved. That was the only mercy in it. Four hundred people stood in the mud and held onto each other and did not move, and the Hedge went on talking to them in the voices of their dead, patiently, name by name, for as long as they stood there.

---

Across the town, the Chapel of the Pure Note fell down.

They heard it go: a long grinding crash from the square, and a great gout of green going up where the roof had been, and then the thing that everyone in that crowd noticed in the same half-second, the thing that made four hundred people look up at once.

The Note had stopped.

Five days. Day and night, one note, without a break, until the town had stopped hearing it the way you stop hearing your own blood. And now it was gone, and in the place where it had been there was nothing but the Greening: the crackle and stretch and sigh of ten thousand things growing at once, and the ringing of the iron-oaks, and the Hedge, saying their names.

The silence where the Note had been was the most frightening sound Lyra had ever heard.

Kaelen's hand closed hard on her shoulder.

"Lyra," he said. "Look up."

She looked up.

Out of the low green sky over the ruin of Oakhaven, brass-ribbed and patched and riding light as a leaf, with her lift-tanks burning cobalt and a line already uncoiling from her winch, came the *Drift-Kestrel*.

---
---

## CHAPTER TWELVE
### Grab the Line

> *The line is not a promise. The line is a rope.*
>
> — Rust Coast saying

*Above Oakhaven. Hearthday afternoon.*

---

The mast went first.

Vane was on deck when it started, watching the quarry fill up like a basin under a pump, and the first he knew of it was Mags hammering the rail with the flat of her hand: three strikes, which meant *look at me now*, and then one finger pointing straight down.

The Consortium's mooring mast was a hundred and twenty feet of riveted iron on four splayed legs, sunk into the bedrock of the quarry rim. There was a young oak in the middle of it. It had not been there twenty minutes ago. It had come up between the legs, and it was still coming, and as Vane watched, the iron on the north leg went from straight to not-straight with a sound like a bell being hit with a hammer, over and over, as the rivets let go one at a time.

*Cut,* he signed. *Cut cut cut—*

Mags already had the axe.

The mooring cable was two inches of wound steel, and she put four blows into it and then the mast leaned and the cable did the rest, parting with a crack that went across the whole quarry, and the loose end came whipping back and took a four-foot splinter off the *Drift-Kestrel*'s starboard rail an arm's length from Pennick's head.

And then they were free, and rising, and turning slowly, and the town of Oakhaven came out from behind the quarry rim below them like something being uncovered.

"Oh," said Pennick.

He had come up the ladder with his tuning-hammer still in his fist and his mouth open, and he stood at the rail and looked down at it and said *oh*, in a small voice, and did not say anything else for a long time.

From a thousand feet, you could see the shape of it. The Greening was not a fire and did not move like one. It moved like a tide coming up a beach: a green edge, advancing, and behind the edge, everything changed. It had the whole north of the town. It had the quarry road and the common and the churchyard. As Vane watched, it took the roof of a long building near the square — the roof did not burn or fall, it simply came apart into green — and something inside it that must have been a beam went up thirty feet in about four seconds, putting out leaves.

Nell Carder had come up out of the hold. Nobody had stopped her. She stood at the rail with her son's hand in hers and looked down at the Hollow, and did not make a sound, because she was from Oakhaven, and that was what they had all been taught.

"That's the tannery lane," said Pim.

"Get below," said Vane.

"That's our—"

"*Below*, boy."

---

"Eighteen," said Pennick.

They were at the rail with the slate between them and the town going under them and the wind pushing the *Kestrel* south-east, away, toward clean country, which was the direction Vane had spent twenty-five years going.

"Eighteen with the crates," Pennick said again. His voice had gone up half an octave and was staying there. "I told you. That's water down to a day and no ballast and the gun over the side, and that was *this morning*, Captain, and since this morning we've had —" he waved the slate at the rigging — "wet canvas, and I've no idea what the envelope's picked up out of that air, and the tanks are singing sour again because everything for ten miles is *growing*—"

"How many are down there?"

"What?"

"In the town. How many people."

Pennick stared at him. "I don't — four hundred? Four hundred and something. It's a town."

Vane looked down at the green coming up Cooper's Row.

He thought about the sensible thing, which was south-east, and about Oona Salt's four hundred and eighty sovereigns, and about a woman with violet eyes asking politely in a doorway on the Salt Stair, and about six-year-old Annet Sowerby in the third crate asking whether anyone was there.

"Take her down over the north gate," he said. "Mags — line and winch. Knots at four-foot. And get the boy's mother away from that rail."

---

They found the town in the only clear ground left: a lozenge of trampled mud inside the gate, sixty feet across, shrinking.

Vane brought the *Kestrel* down to forty feet and held her there with the envelope taking a beating from the updraught off all that new wood, and Mags put the line over, and four hundred people looked up at it.

They did not rush it. He would think about that for the rest of his life, too. Somebody down there — a big woman with her sleeves rolled, standing on a mounting-block by the gatepost — was pushing people into a queue with her forearms, and an old woman in an apron was going down the line pulling children out and moving them forward, and a man in shirtsleeves with a surgeon's bag was doing triage on the ground with two lamps and no table.

They took the children first. Four at a time on the knotted line, the biggest child with the smallest one tied against her chest with a shawl, and Mags on the winch drum taking them up hand over hand with her jaw set, and Vane counting them onto the deck.

Four. Eight. Eleven.

"Captain—" said Pennick.

"I can count."

Thirteen. Fifteen.

The *Kestrel* was settling. He could feel it in the wheel and see it in the way the horizon crept up the shrouds: she was a hundred feet lower than she had been ten minutes ago, and the iron-oaks on the common were not a hundred feet lower than they had been ten minutes ago. They were higher.

That was the thing nobody had said out loud yet. The trees were coming up at them.

---

At eighteen, the hounds came into the canopy.

He heard them before he saw them, because that was how it always was: from the new green of the oaks below and to starboard, a woman's voice, singing something sweet and patient about lambs and dusk, and then a man's cracked tenor going up and up for a high note and missing it, and then, very clearly, a child laughing.

"*Up*," Vane said. "Mags — *up*, get them—"

The jitter came out of the crown of the nearest oak and dropped onto the line.

There were four on the rope: a young man near the top, then two women, then an old man at the bottom with his boots hooked in a knot. The jitter went down the line the way a spider goes down silk, and it did not stop at the old man. It went through him.

Vane saw it happen from thirty feet away in clean afternoon light. The man's shape went soft at the edges, and then grainy, like a charcoal drawing with a thumb dragged across it, and the grain came apart, and the screaming — he was screaming, and everyone below was screaming — pitched up and thinned into static in the space of a breath and a half.

His hands stayed on the rope.

Just the hands, gripping the knot, for perhaps a second, before they came off and fell.

Then the thing went up the line after the women.

Kaelen Vance came over the port rail at that moment with his left sleeve gone and his arm blazing like a lamp with a white-hot wick, and put himself between the winch and the line, and when the jitter came over the rail he took it out of the air with his glass fist and it burst into a spray of bright falling notes and a laugh that Vane recognized, and which he was going to hear again, he knew, for years, at three in the morning.

"*Get them up!*" Kaelen shouted.

They got the three up. The old man on the deck-boards was a pair of boots and a knitted cap and nothing else, and one of the women who came over the rail after him had his blood on her, though there had been no blood, and Vane understood that she had bitten through her own lip getting up the rope.

Twenty-one.

"Captain." Pennick had hold of his arm with both hands. He was crying and did not seem to know it. "Captain, we are at twenty-one souls and the crates and we are *going down*. Not slow. Look at the trees. Look at the *trees*, Captain—"

---

Lyra Ashgrove came up with the last four before the last.

She came over the rail like a dropped coat, and the big landlady and the old midwife got her under the arms, and she could not stand, and there was blood down the front of a white shift that had been white that morning and a hole under her jaw where a copper wire had been. She got a hand into Kaelen's collar and pulled his ear down to her mouth, and Vane watched her try to say something, and nothing come out at all. Not a rasp. Nothing.

Kaelen said, "I know. I know. They're coming."

And Mags put the line down for the last load.

Five went onto it.

Vane, leaning over the rail with the wind of the envelope beating up into his face, saw them from above: a girl of eleven with bandaged stumps for hands, roped to the knot under her arms by two men; an old woman in a dye-works apron — the same one who had been pulling children out of the queue, who had, somebody told him later, taken over the night-hummer's stool at the Flume crossing when the old man died; a young man in Consortium drab with a roll of charts stuffed down his shirt; a woman who was screaming somebody's name up at the ship.

And at the bottom, last, with his boots on the lowest knot and one arm through it and the other hand still on the shoulder of the man he had just pushed up ahead of himself, was a big grey-headed old man in a Governor's chain of bronze oak-leaves.

He had loaded his town onto that rope. He had been doing it for twenty minutes, Vane realized: every load, the same figure at the foot of the line, taking people by the elbows and putting them on and sending them up, and stepping back for the next one.

"*Take her up*," Vane said.

Mags wound. The line came up. And the *Drift-Kestrel*, sixty feet lower than she had been at the start of it, with twenty-one souls and twelve crates and four hundred gallons of nothing left to throw, did not go with it.

She went sideways.

---

It happened the way it always happens: slowly, and then in about ten heartbeats.

The wind came round off the burning of something in the south and pushed her, and she had no ballast and no grip and she slid, and the line and the five on it swung out and down, and the crown of an iron-oak that had been a sapling at breakfast came up through the underside of that arc like a hand closing.

The rope went into the branches. Then the rope snagged.

Everything after that was arithmetic. Vane had never in his life been good at arithmetic and he did it in about a second and a half. A wooden ship at forty feet with no lift, anchored by her winch drum to a tree that was growing, and the drum bolted through the deck-beams, and the deck-beams holding the hull together.

Pennick was howling. "The drum — Captain, the drum's coming out of the *deck*—"

*One.*

Twenty-one people on the deck. Nine of them children. A boy in the hold with his ear against a crate.

*Two.*

The old woman on the line had got her feet onto the branches and was trying to get the girl with no hands off the rope and into the tree, which would have been clever if the tree had been a tree and not the thing that was eating the town.

*Three.*

Mags, at the winch, with the axe already in her fist because she had been at sea for thirty years and knew exactly what came next, and she was looking at him, and she was not going to swing it without an order, because she never had.

*Four.*

The deck-beam under the drum went off like a musket.

*Five.*

Vane took the axe out of Mags's hands, because if it was going to be anyone it was going to be him.

*Six.*

And Kaelen Vance came up beside him at the rail and looked down the line at his father.

*Seven.*

Aldric Vance looked up.

It was forty feet. Vane could see his face perfectly well. He was a big old man with a jaw like a gate-post and grey hair combed long over one temple, and he looked up past four people at a ship that was coming down onto a tree, and he understood the arithmetic faster than Vane had, because he had been a Governor for thirty years and it is all any of them ever do.

*Eight.*

He let go of the rope with one hand, and raised it, palm out, the way a man waves a cart on past a hole in the road.

*Nine.*

"Do it," said Kaelen.

*Ten.*

Vane cut the line.

---

She came up like a cork.

That was the obscene part. The instant the rope went, the *Drift-Kestrel* shed three hundred pounds and the tree's grip together, and she leapt — bodily leapt — a hundred and fifty feet into the green afternoon, and everyone on deck went down on their knees with the surge of it.

Vane kept his feet, because he had the rail.

So he was the only one who saw it, except Kaelen, who did not look away either: five people falling forty feet into a canopy that had not existed that morning, and the canopy closing over them, and a sound that the wind took most of.

Then there was nothing down there but leaves.

---

They lifted out of it into cold clean air at eight hundred feet, and the town went small below them, and the green kept coming.

From up here you could see the whole shape of the Reach: Oakhaven going under, and the Greening spreading out from the quarry in a rough circle four miles across, and the spearhead of it already reaching down the Greenroot road toward the south, following the ditches, the way water follows a furrow.

And in the trampled mud by the North Gate, three hundred and eighty people stood in the last clear ground in the world and watched the only ship go away.

Some of them were waving. That was the thing Vane could not put down afterward. Not shaking fists. *Waving* — the way you wave a cart on past a hole in the road.

He stood at the rail with the axe still in his hand until Mags came and took it off him, gently, the way you take a cup off a drunk.

Pim Carder was at the hatch. He had come up in the middle of it, and nobody had sent him down, and he had seen all of it, and he was looking at Vane now with his mouth a little open and his hands held out stiff in front of him.

Vane opened his mouth to say something to him.

He found that his body, which had laughed in a brothel in the Screaming Year with a girl dying in the next room, and laughed in the Boar & Dulcimer with a hound's mouth coming at his boots, and laughed at his own reflection every bad morning of his adult life, was not going to do it this time. Nothing came up at all.

He shut his mouth and went aft and stood at the wheel and set a course north-east, into the wind, away from the coast, toward the Spectral Woods.

There was six feet of rope hanging from the winch drum, cut clean, swinging.

Nobody coiled it. It hung there for four days.

---
---

## CHAPTER THIRTEEN
### Ash in the Hold

> *A structure does not fail where it is weakest. It fails where the load finds it.*
>
> — K. Vance, *On the Calibration of Resonant Bores*

*Aboard the* Drift-Kestrel*, north-east over the Reach. Hearthday night into Rootday.*

---

There were twenty-one of them on deck and nowhere to put any of them.

The *Drift-Kestrel* was sixty feet of working deck and a hold full of crates, and by the second hour of the night it had become a field hospital run by an eighty-year-old midwife with no lamp oil. Bettany had the wounded along the port rail under a spare staysail: a man with a crushed foot from the winch-house; a woman with a broken collarbone from the line; four people with hands flayed to the meat by forty feet of knotted rope; a girl of eleven who had no hands to flay, who had been carried up roped under the arms, and who had not spoken since.

Hetty Bram had the children in the lee of the deckhouse in a nest of canvas, counted twice an hour, which Kaelen understood was not about safety.

Nell Carder had her son.

And at the stern rail, wrapped in Vane's own coat with her white shift stiff and black down the front, sat Lyra Ashgrove, who could not make a sound.

Not a whisper. Not a rasp. Bettany had looked into her throat with a lamp and a spoon and then had sat back on her heels and said nothing for a while, and then said, "Rest it. Don't try. Not for days," in the flat voice she used when she was frightened.

Kaelen went to her twice.

The first time he crouched down beside her and said, "Bettany says days. Not weeks. She's seen worse," and Lyra looked at the black country going by underneath and did not turn her head.

The second time, an hour later, he brought her the slate.

She took it, and the chalk, and held them in her lap for a long moment. Then she wrote four words and turned it around.

*YOU SAID DO IT.*

Kaelen looked at it.

"Yes," he said.

She waited. Whatever she was waiting for, he did not have it. He had spent seven years becoming a man who did not have it.

She wiped the slate with the heel of her hand, and gave it back to him, and turned her face to the dark.

---

He went below.

The hold smelled of pine and straw and cold glass, and the crates talked among themselves the way they always did, and he sat down on the boards with his back against the third crate and his glass arm out in front of him and let the murmur go over him.

Through the arm, faintly, four hours behind and four hundred feet down, he could still hear the Root.

He had expected it to be gone. He had done the arithmetic, in the shaft, climbing: forty thousand gallons of raw glass into a wound in a living anchor; a bore-head broken off in the casing; the law running wild across four miles of the Reach. He had expected to hear whatever he had heard at the Dawning Spire: the chord stopping, and the floor going out from under sound.

It had not stopped.

It was singing. Badly, and slowly, and in the wrong place, with two of the five notes missing altogether now, going around and around and losing itself and starting again — and it was, he understood, *still holding*. Whatever had been done to it, the thing in the stone had gone on doing the one thing it had agreed to do a very long time ago, and was doing it with a hole in it.

*Somebody has to hold it,* he thought.

He did not like the shape of the thought. He filed it where he filed everything.

Vane came down the ladder with a bottle and sat on the bottom rung.

"She'll speak again," he said.

"I know."

"I've seen a man lose his voice on a foul tow and get it back inside a month." Vane drank. He did not offer the bottle. "Not the same voice. But a voice."

They sat in the dark with four hundred murmuring people stacked in straw between them.

"Thirteen hundred," Vane said at last.

Kaelen said nothing.

"In two years. Whisper-Grove to the Organum, Whisper-Grove to Fortis, Whisper-Grove to the garrison at Redoubt Zero. Thirteen hundred-odd crates' worth, and I signed for every one, and I knew what was in them by the fourth run, and I kept taking the work, because the *Kestrel* was four hundred and eighty sovereigns and I had sixty." He looked up. "That's the whole confession. You can have it now, because I'd rather you had it from me than from the manifests."

Kaelen reached into his coat and took out the cracked lilac sphere and set it on the lid of the crate between them.

"Tamsin Reed," he said. "She ran a Legionnaire at the quarry. I broke her because she'd seen me lift a coil." He looked at it. "She spent her last three seconds saying her own name so somebody would have it."

Vane looked at the sphere for a long time.

"Whisper-Grove," he said. "There's a scribe. The one who wrote the catalogue, they say — signed it, then stole the ledger and walked off into the mist. If anybody alive knows how to put a name back into a head, it's whoever worked out how to take it out."

"And if she won't?"

"Then I'll be extremely charming," said Vane, "and if that fails, I'll be extremely unpleasant, and if that fails I'll carry four hundred people around in my hold until somebody tells me what to do with them, because I am not giving them to the garrison and I am not putting them in the ground." He drank again. "That's the course. North-east. We'll want water at Greenroot if Greenroot's still there."

He stood, and stopped, with his hand on the ladder.

"You didn't have to say it," he said. "Up there. I'd have cut it anyway. I had the axe in my hand and I'd already done the sum."

"I know," said Kaelen. "That's why I said it."

Vane stood with his back to him for a moment.

"Aye," he said. "I thought that was it." And went up.

---

At the fourth hour, Pennick started screaming.

Kaelen got to the deck four seconds behind Vane and found the boy on his knees at the foredeck with a lantern, tearing canvas away from the planking, and under the canvas the deck of the *Drift-Kestrel* had gone green.

Not moss. Roots. Fine white ones, thousands of them, standing up out of the seams between the planks like hair on a cold arm, and swelling as he watched, and at the center of them, sitting with his back against the deckhouse and his boots stretched out in front of him and a blanket over his knees, was Hob Wendle.

He had been on the line in the second load. Field-hand, forty, big-shouldered, sheepish. Three toes short. Rooke had taken them clean on Rootday with a hush-saw and a sap-iron, and he had walked afterward, as Rooke had promised.

His boots were gone. The bandages were gone. What was left of his feet had come open like split fruit, and out of them, into the good dry oak of Vane's deck, had gone a mat of roots six feet across, and the planks around them had begun, very gently, to put out leaves.

"I did try," said Hob.

He was awake. That was the horror of it: he was perfectly awake, and perfectly lucid, and had been for some time, and had lain there with a blanket over his knees while it happened and not called out, because he was from Oakhaven and it was the middle of the night and there were children asleep eight feet away.

"I kept 'em dry," he said. "Like she told me. I kept 'em warm and dry the whole — but it's in the wood, see. It's got into the wood." He looked up at Vane with an expression of absolutely unbearable apology. "I'm sorry about your ship, Captain."

Pennick was on his knees with his hands in his hair. "It's a *wooden ship*," he said, to nobody, to the deck, to the roots. "It's in the beams. Captain, it's in the *beams*—"

Mags took him by the collar and lifted him up and put him at the rail and stood between him and the foredeck.

Kaelen knelt down and put his hand into the mat of roots. They turned toward the warmth of him, slowly, the way they always did. He followed them with his fingers to where they went into the seam, and in under the plank, and along the beam beneath, and he did the arithmetic he had spent his life doing.

"Two hours," he said. "Maybe three. After that the beams go and she comes apart in the air."

Nobody said anything. In the stern, one of the children turned over in the canvas nest and muttered something.

Lyra was already on her knees beside Hob with the little brown bottle in her hand.

Rooke's bottle. She had carried it up a four-hundred-foot ladder and out through a dying town and up a knotted rope in her fist, and there were, Kaelen saw when she turned it in the lantern-light, three doses left.

Hob Wendle looked at the bottle, and then at her, and his face did something complicated.

"Ah," he said. "Right you are, Cantor."

Lyra opened her mouth.

Nothing came out of it. Nothing at all: not the tending note, not the Root note, not a rasp — only air, moving over ruined ground. She tried again, and her whole body went into it, and the third time her hand went up to the hole under her jaw where the wire had been and stayed there, and her face came apart.

Old Bettany got down on the boards beside her with her knees cracking.

"Give it here, girl," she said. "You hold his hand."

So Lyra Ashgrove held Hob Wendle's hand, and Bettany the midwife gave him the long drops on his tongue, three of them, the way she had watched it done forty times — and then Bettany, who had delivered half of Oakhaven and buried a good deal of the rest, and who had a voice like a saw in a knot, hummed the tending note over him, badly, in the Narrow, for as long as it took.

Kaelen held his shoulders. He weighed nothing by the end.

They cut him out of the deck with a chisel and a hand-saw, and they took up four planks and five feet of good oak beam with him, and they put all of it over the side together into the dark, because there was nothing else to be done with a thing that would grow wherever it was put down.

Vane said the words. He knew them; he had said them a great many times at sea. Then he went aft to the wheel and stood there for an hour and would not be spoken to, and Pennick went round the whole deck on his hands and knees until dawn with a lamp and a chisel, prising up every seam that showed green, and did not find any more.

Not that night.

---

Tobiah Marsh came to him at first light.

Kaelen was at the port rail watching the Reach go by underneath: eight hundred feet down, in the grey, a country that had been fields yesterday. Four miles of canopy where Oakhaven had been, and the green running south down the Greenroot road ahead of the light, following the ditches. Somewhere in the middle of it there was a dirty orange smear that had to be fire — a ring of it, burning in a circle around something, and Kaelen looked at it for a while before he understood.

Firebreaks. Glass-fire, in the square, burning a ring in the green.

The Church had come to Oakhaven to purify ground for a Rite, and was now down there in the dark burning the ground to save what was left of the people it had come to burn. He wondered whether the Inquisitor had noticed that yet. He rather thought the Inquisitor had.

"Sir."

The boy had his charts under his arm. He had been up all night; they all had; but he had the particular grey look of somebody who has been sitting with a thing and turning it over.

"Marsh."

"I saw what you put in the port," said Tobiah Marsh.

Kaelen turned around.

"I was sitting on the rail calling the pitch to you," the boy said. "You asked me for a spanner. And you took the plate off the drum and you put something in the phase port, and forty seconds later the lock threw." He was not shouting. His voice was shaking very slightly, in a way he was working hard to control, and that was worse. "Marten and Old Coyle and the Tanner twins died of slippage, sir. In the spring. I helped carry them up. And you put slippage into that machine on purpose, with two hundred men in the quarry and a Cantor ten feet from the collar."

"Yes," said Kaelen.

"*Why?*"

So he told him.

Not all of it. Not the part that mattered most, which he had never told anyone and was not going to tell a twenty-four-year-old on a deck at dawn. But he told him about Stage Nine, and what a wire does to a throat, and what the Rite of Pure Unison had been for, and what he had understood standing at the calibration rail while the pitch came up and the thing in the Root stopped singing and started *listening*. And last, because the boy was an engineer and would need the one sentence that made the rest of it undeniable, he told him about the note in the margin of his own Sequence, in a small beautiful hand, under the scales.

*On failure of the hold, the Sequence is not to be halted. Slippage is to be absorbed by the source.*

Tobiah Marsh stood very still.

Then he pulled the roll of charts out from inside his shirt — a month of readings, every quarter-hour, in his own small careful hand, and pinned around them with a bootlace, a fair copy of Revision Seven that he had made for himself because he wanted to learn it properly — and he held it out.

"Take them," he said.

"Marsh—"

"*Take them.*" His eyes were streaming; he did not appear to notice. "They'll say it was slippage. That's what they always say, and now there's a whole town under a wood and they'll say the Root was unstable and the bore-head was faulty and the Consortium will pay a bounty to the families of the shift-crews, and in the spring they'll sink another one somewhere else, at Greenroot or in the Peaks, with the same Sequence and the same stage nine and some other town's Cantor." He pushed the roll into Kaelen's hands. "That's the only copy outside the Organum that has the margin on it. I copied the margin because I thought it was clever."

Kaelen took it.

"I'm not going to tell anyone what you did," said Tobiah Marsh. "I want you to know that I thought about it. All night. I sat with the children and I thought about it." He wiped his face with the back of his wrist, once, angrily. "My mother's in Sol-Luminary. In the Umbral Tiers. If they hear my name at the works they'll go and ask her where I've gone."

"Then we won't use your name," said Kaelen.

The boy nodded. He stood there a moment longer, at a loss, the way people are when the thing they have been carrying all night is suddenly out of their hands.

"It was a beautiful Sequence, sir," he said, wretchedly. "That's the worst of it. It's the most beautiful piece of engineering I ever read."

"I know," said Kaelen. "I've thought so for fifteen years."

---

The sun came up somewhere behind the haze, pale as a coin under water.

And out of the canopy four miles behind them — from a country that had no towns left in it, across eight hundred feet of cold morning air, faint and clear and perfectly in tune — somebody began to sing the calling-song.

In Lyra's voice.

Not the ruined one. The whole one. Warm, and round, and patient, the way she had sounded on a stone behind a cooperage on forty-one nights, singing lost lambs home to the fold.

The whole deck heard it. Hetty Bram's head came up. Two of the children began to cry. Mags, who heard nothing, saw every face on that deck change at once and put her hand on the axe.

Lyra was at the stern rail. She did not turn around. She listened to her own voice come up out of the leaves and follow the ship north-east at eight hundred feet, for as long as it went on, and her hands on the rail did not move.

Kaelen came and stood beside her and did not say anything, because there was nothing to say that she could answer.

After a while the singing stopped. It did not fade. It stopped, the way something stops when it has finished doing a thing it meant to do, and the sound of the *Kestrel* came back: the hum of the tanks, the creak of the envelope, the wind.

*Once it has your note,* the old rhyme went, *it can find you anywhere.*

---

That night, in the hold, by the light of the lantern, Kaelen Vance found a nub of charcoal in his coat pocket and wrote on the underside of the deck-beam over the third crate.

First the number he had written on a wall in a charcoal-burner's hut every morning for seven years, because it had moved again and there was no wall now to write it on.

*3½.*

Then, under it, because somebody had to have them and because he had watched a young woman spend her last three seconds trying to make sure that somebody did:

*Tamsin Reed*
*Aldric Vance*
*Davin Hale*
*Tam — (no surname. ASK.)*
*Orla Hale*
*Hob Wendle*
*Ada Pellow*
*8 men at the pumps — no names. FIND THEM.*

He sat back and looked at it: nine lines of charcoal on a ship's beam, above four hundred people in straw who each had a number and no name at all, flying north-east through the dark toward the one woman in the world who had written all of their names down and then run away with the book.

He did not yet know what the list was for.

He only knew that somebody had to keep one — and that every single person who had ever kept one before him had been the Catalogue.

---
---

## PART THREE: THE FORGETTING

---

## CHAPTER FOURTEEN
### Entry

> *Form 9: Intake. The subject is to be asked three questions and no more. Name. Age. Trade. Further speech is not to be recorded, as it lengthens the queue.*
>
> — Office of the Catalogue, standing forms

*Whisper-Grove, in the Spectral Woods. Memoryday.*

---

At dusk, the archivist called Sable went down into the Weeping Plaza to read to the dead.

They were not dead. She was careful about that, in the Ledger and in her head, because the distinction had cost seventy-four thousand people everything they had and the least she could do was keep it. *Dead* was a thing with an entry and an end date. These had neither. The Office's word for them was *residual*; the word in the villages was *Hollow*; the word Sable used in the Ledger, because it was the only honest one, was *unnamed*.

The plaza had been the civic heart of Whisper-Grove: a square of white marble the size of four fields, with a colonnade on three sides and the Moot-house on the fourth, and a shallow ornamental basin down the middle of it in which the city had once floated lamps on festival nights. When the Anchor of Memory was drilled in 416, the water table had come up. Now the plaza stood under a foot of still water from end to end, the colonnade rose out of it like a row of teeth, and at dusk the water lit itself: a cold lilac phosphorescence, no brighter than a candle but everywhere at once, so that the whole square glowed from underneath and the mist over it glowed too, and everything that moved in it cast its shadow upward onto the fog.

Sable came down the Moot-house steps into the water with her skirts already sodden from three years of it, and the Bound Ledger came with her, floating at her left elbow at the height of a writing-desk, open, its pages turning themselves.

There were perhaps two hundred of them in the plaza tonight.

They did not gather. They simply ended up in the same place, the way leaves do. A woman in a good wool coat walked from the third pillar to the basin's edge, stopped, turned, and walked back to the third pillar, and had been doing it since the spring. Two men stood facing each other at the corner of the colonnade, and one of them lifted his hand at intervals to make a point in an argument that had finished three years ago, and the other waited for him to finish, forever, politely. A young man crouched at the water's edge washing a plate that had no dirt on it and had not had for a thousand days. Further out, near the drowned fountain, an old man was setting a table.

He did it every evening. He had built the table himself out of a door and two barrels, and every evening he set it for four: four flat stones for plates, four twigs laid across them, and a fifth stone at the head, which Sable had decided long ago was a bread-loaf. Then he stood behind it with his hands folded and waited for the people who were coming.

His entry was twelve thousand two hundred and seven. Halder Wick, fifty-eight, joiner, of the Bell Yard. Taken on the second day, in the first hour, because he had lived near the works and the works had started at the end of his own street.

"Good evening, Master Wick," said Sable.

He did not look up. They never did.

She opened the Ledger to the place she had left it and began.

"Entry forty thousand, one hundred and one. Ivo Renn, forty-one, cooper, of Ashlane. Taken on the fourth day." She read it the way the Veil had taught her to read, flat and clear and unhurried, in the voice of a person reading a will. "He asked whether he would be permitted to keep his hands. He was told that he would."

The water moved gently around her knees. The mist glowed.

"Entry forty thousand, one hundred and two. Bettis Renn, thirty-eight, his wife. Taken on the fourth day. She asked to go second."

Nothing happened. Nothing had ever happened, except four times in three years, and each of those four times was recorded in the back of the Ledger in her own hand under the heading OBSERVED EFFECTS, and each of them had amounted to a Hollow stopping what it was doing for between one and nine seconds.

She read twenty names a night. She had done the arithmetic on the first night, standing in this water with her hands shaking: seventy-four thousand and six hundred and eleven entries, at twenty a night, is ten years and two hundred and twenty days, and then you begin again at the first name, and by then the ones you started with will have been standing in the rain for fourteen years.

She read twenty names a night anyway.

It was not a cure. She had never once believed it was a cure. It was the only thing she had been able to think of that was not simply leaving.

---

When she had finished the twentieth, she closed the Ledger and did the inventory.

This was not in any rule of the Veil. She had invented it herself, in the second month, when she noticed that the word *scriptorium* had begun to arrive a half-second late.

The Bound Ledger holds what is written in it. That is its whole nature and its whole price. It is not paper; the Veil bound the first of them eleven hundred years ago out of something that is not paper, and what it holds, it holds against the world — against fire, against water, against the Catalogue, and against the Office's habit of amending the record. But it holds nothing for free. It is a set of scales. Weight for weight: to keep a thing, the scribe gives up a thing of her own, and the Ledger decides what, and the Ledger is not cruel about it and not kind about it either. It takes what is nearest to hand.

She had written seventy-four thousand six hundred and eleven names into it in eleven days.

So: the inventory. Every night, before sleep, in order, out loud.

"One. My mother's hands, braiding my hair. She did it too tight. She had a ring on the third finger of her left hand, a plain one, and it caught, and she said *sorry, chick* every time and never once loosened her grip."

The mist moved.

"Two. The scriptorium at the Veil-house. Cold stone, tallow, wet wool, and vinegar, because we cleaned the desks with vinegar on the last day of every month. Fourteen desks. Mine was by the second window."

She stopped there for a moment, as she always did, and made herself say the third.

"Three. My name is—"

And she said it, out loud, to a plaza full of people who had none, and heard herself say it, and it was still there.

Three fragments. There had been more. She could not have told anyone how many more, because the nature of the loss is that the shelf does not look empty; it looks like a shelf. She knew about these three only because she had written them down on the flyleaf on the first night, in the scriptorium, before she understood what she had agreed to — a young woman being clever, making a list of the things she would mind losing.

She had put the list at the front of a book that eats lists.

She would think about that, most nights, at about this point in the evening.

---

The Office was three days behind her, and closing.

She had seen the first proctor on Stasisday, from the Moot-house gallery: a grey figure in oilcloth at the edge of the mist, standing still in the way that only Catalogue field staff stand still, watching the plaza for a full hour without once shifting his weight. She had seen two of them on Dawnday, together, walking the Bell Yard.

And on Driftday she had found the twice-taken man.

He was in the Ashlane, sitting against a wall with his legs straight out in front of him, and he was not doing anything. That was how she knew. Hollows always do something: they walk their loop, or wash their plate, or set their table. A Hollow is a person with the top layer lifted off, and what is left underneath keeps working, the way a mill keeps turning when the miller has gone.

This man was doing nothing at all. His eyes were open and wet and they tracked her when she crouched in front of him, and there was nothing behind them, and there would never be anything behind them again, because the Office had pressed him a second time.

They had done it to find out whether she had spoken to him.

She had. On the eleventh of Leaffall, she had sat with him for an hour because he had been walking a loop that took him past her door and she had wanted the company. She had read him four names. He had carried on walking.

Sable had knelt in the road with her hands in her lap for a long time. Then she had opened the Ledger and written his entry, and underneath it, in the margin, she had written: *This was done because I spoke to him.* And then she had drawn a line through the margin note and written it again properly, without the excuse in it: *He was pressed a second time by the Office of the Catalogue on the fourteenth or fifteenth day of Leaffall, 419, in the course of an inquiry into the theft of this book.*

The Veil keeps three rules. Write what happened. Write who it happened to. Do not comfort yourself in the margin.

---

She was climbing the Moot-house steps out of the water when she heard the ship.

She heard it long before she saw it, because Whisper-Grove is the quietest city in the world and has been since the drills, and a working skyship at three hundred feet sounds like a barn being dragged slowly across the sky: the hum of lift-tanks out of tune, the creak of an envelope, the slap of loose canvas, and under all of it a donkey-engine with a bad bearing.

Then it came over the willows from the south-west with the last of the light behind it, and Sable stood on the steps with lilac mist to her knees and looked up.

Brass ribs. Oak planking. A grey envelope patched like a quilt, four lift-tanks under the keel burning cobalt, and a swivel-gun mount on the foredeck with no gun in it. She was flying light and she was flying badly, down by the head, and there were people on her deck — a great many people, far more than that class of hull was ever meant to carry, standing at the rail in a crowd and looking down.

On her bow, under two coats of paint that had been put on to cover the last name and then the one before it, was the ghost of a registry mark.

OK-114.

Sable stopped walking.

She knew that mark the way a clerk knows any mark she has read eleven times. She could have told you the color of the ink the carriage-office used for it. She had signed the consignment notes underneath it: *attested, dry, secure, unopened*, and a date, and the Office's stamp, and then her own initials, because the Registrar-General required initials on carriage as well as intake.

Eleven consignments. The first one twelve crates, the second sixteen, and then twenty-four, and twenty-four, and twenty-four.

The wind off the willows turned and came over the plaza, and it brought down from three hundred feet, faint and overlapping and perfectly familiar, the sound of a hold full of people talking in their sleep.

---

She did not move until the ship had gone over the plaza and away north toward the old sky-dock at the Cloister road, and the sound of it had gone out of the mist, and the Hollows had gone on walking their loops through the glowing water as though nothing whatever had happened, which, for them, was true.

Then she went up to the room she had made for herself in the Moot-house muniment store, and lit her lamp, and set the Bound Ledger on the desk, and opened it to the current page, and took up her pen.

She wrote:

*Entry. Memoryday, the sixteenth of Leaffall, 419. At dusk, a skyship over the Weeping Plaza, flying no colors, riding light, down by the head, carrying an estimated twenty to thirty persons on deck. Registry OK-114, twice painted over. Carriage attested by this hand on eleven occasions between Greenleaf 417 and Ashfall 419: total consignment one thousand three hundred and forty-two cores.*

*The hold is occupied.*

She stopped. She looked at the sentence for a while.

Then she wrote, under it, in the same flat hand:

*Note: the Office's proctors are in the Bell Yard and they will have heard it too. They will reach the sky-dock before dawn.*

*Note: I have spent three years reading twenty names a night to people who cannot hear them, because I could not think of anything else to do.*

*Note: that ship can carry a book.*

And then, because the Veil keeps three rules and the third one is the hardest, she made herself write the last line plainly, without any comfort in the margin at all:

*Note: I am going to go and look at it. This is the first time in three years that I have written the word I.*

---
---

## CHAPTER FIFTEEN
### Statistical Anomaly

> *The Veil keeps three rules. Write what happened. Write who it happened to. Do not comfort yourself in the margin.*
>
> — Precept of the Silent Veil

*Whisper-Grove. Memoryday night.*

---

They moored at the Cloister road sky-dock an hour after dark, and Kaelen went down into the city with Lyra and a lantern and found out what a law looks like after it has been switched off.

The Reach was dying of too much. Whisper-Grove had died of not enough, three years ago, in a single night, and then had gone on standing there.

Nothing had fallen down. That was the first wrong thing. The streets were swept; the shutters were latched; there were flowers in a window-box on Bell Yard, dead flowers, three years of them, one summer's crop fallen and the next grown up through the rot and fallen in its turn. There were carts in the carters' yard with their shafts up, and horses long since gone, and a cat — a perfectly ordinary tabby cat, fat and glossy, which came out of a doorway and walked alongside them for forty yards and then lost interest. Animals did not take the Forgetting. Nobody knew why. The Office had a paper about it.

The people were in the streets.

The first one they passed was a woman in a good coat walking from a pillar to a basin and back. The second was a lamplighter with his pole, going along Bell Yard lighting lamps that had no oil in them and had not been filled since 416, touching the taper to the wick and waiting, and moving on to the next one, and at the end of the row turning around and coming back to the first.

Lyra stopped in front of him. She had been silent for five days — not by choice; there was still nothing in her throat but air — and she stood in the fog with her lantern up, watching an old man light a lamp that would not light, and Kaelen watched her hands open and close twice.

She got out the slate. She wrote, and turned it to him.

*CAN THEY EAT?*

"Sable's paper says they feed themselves if food's put where their loop goes," said Kaelen. "The Office kept four thousand of them alive for two years at Whisper-Grove to work the pumps. So yes."

She wiped the slate with her cuff and wrote again, harder, so the chalk squealed.

*I MEANT ARE THEY HUNGRY.*

He did not have an answer to that.
