He had not, in fact, thought of the question, and he stood in a dead street in the fog and understood that for three days he had been thinking of four hundred glass spheres in a hold as *cargo to be delivered somewhere useful*, which was precisely the phrase the Catalogue used, and that this was how it happened, and that it took about a week.

The Weeping Plaza glowed at the end of the street like a fire under a door.

---

They went down the Moot-house steps into a foot of lit water, and the mist took the lantern-light and gave it back tenfold, and out in the middle of the plaza, under the colonnade, a woman in grey was reading aloud.

She was small, and thin, and her hair was cut off blunt at the jaw the way a clerk cuts her own hair in a mirror. She stood in the water in sodden skirts with her back to them. And beside her, at her left elbow, at exactly the height of a writing-desk, an open book hung in the air with nothing under it, and its pages were turning by themselves.

"Entry forty thousand, one hundred and nine," she said. "Ketter Dell, nineteen, apprenticed to a fuller. Taken on the fourth day. He gave his age as twenty so as not to be sent to the back of the queue."

"We're looking for the scribe," Kaelen said. "The one who wrote the catalogue."

"You have found her, Kaelen Vance," she said, without turning round. "Chief Resonant Engineer, Consortium of Bores, badge four-oh-eight-A. Conservatory of Bores, 402 to 406, first in your year. Author of the Vance Sequence, revisions one through three." The Ledger turned a page for her. "Last recorded at the Siphon Foundry, Sol-Luminary, on the morning of the Great Foundry Rupture, year 412. Office estimate of survival for persons at your station on that gantry: two percent."

She turned around.

Her face was much younger than her voice and very tired, and there was ink to the second knuckle on both hands.

"You are a statistical anomaly," she said. "I wrote you into the ledger of the dead nine days after the Fracture, and I have never had cause to correct an entry of that kind before or since. Do you know how few people that is? Out of seventy-four thousand and six hundred and eleven here, and eleven thousand at Sol-Luminary, you are one of two."

"Who's the other?"

"I don't know," said Sable. "She fell into the Spire."

---

She would not come.

He put it to her three ways in ten minutes, and he was not, he realized somewhere in the middle of it, doing it well; he was doing it the way an engineer specifies a part.

"There are four hundred and thirty-two cores in the hold of that ship," he said. "Whisper-Grove stock. The tags run in the forty-one thousands. They were consigned to a Carillon garrison in the Reach and the garrison is under a wood now, and the man who was carrying them has decided not to deliver them, and I need to know whether what was done to them can be undone."

"No," said Sable.

"You wrote the catalogue—"

"I wrote the *catalogue*." She said the word as though handing him a tool and correcting his grip on it. "I am a list, Engineer. I am not a cure. I recorded what was taken, in the order it was taken, with the name and the age and the trade and any last thing the subject said that I could hear from the door. I have never operated a press. I could not tell you the first thing about how one works, except what it sounds like from twelve feet away, which is like a tuning fork struck against a rib." She paused. "Which you may find you already know, because I am told your Sequence assumes a hundred and seventy-four hertz, and the Office's press is tuned to seven hundred and forty-one, and they are cousins."

"Then come and look at one with me."

"There is a harvest-house four miles north of us with two presses in it and eleven staff, and I have been within a quarter-mile of it twice." Her voice did not rise at all. "I went back the second time because I had begun to believe I might be brave. I stood in the trees for two hours, and then I came home and read twenty names."

Lyra came forward through the water then, and held up the slate.

*HOW MUCH DOES IT COST YOU TO PUT ONE BACK?*

Sable looked at it. Then at Lyra — at the white shift under the borrowed coat, and the mark under her jaw where a wire had been, still scabbed, still weeping a little.

"Unison hold," she said. "Stage nine. Collar and wire." She said it as a clerk reads a form. "You are the first person I have met who has come off one alive. The Office's carriage records list forty collars sent to Sol-Luminary in 411 and four returned." She looked back at the slate. "You are asking the right question and the answer is that I do not know what one costs, because I have never dared do it. Weight for weight. A name out of the book and into a head, and something of mine goes onto the scale to balance it. I have three things left that I am certain of. I can name them. That is not a great deal of currency for four hundred and thirty-two people, or for seventy-four thousand."

"Then—"

"Then nothing, Engineer. Then I am a woman with a book who reads twenty names a night, which is what I can do, and what I can do is not enough, and I have made my peace with that in the only way anyone ever does, which is badly."

She turned away, and the Ledger turned with her.

"Get your ship out before dawn," she said. "The Office has proctors in the Bell Yard. They came for me and now they will come for you, and you have children on that deck; I could hear them from the steps."

And then Halder Wick, who was setting his table forty feet away in the glowing water, stopped setting his table.

---

Kaelen's arm heard them before anyone saw them.

It went up a quarter-tone — the glass pulling toward something the way it always did, a hard high sympathy — and he had time to say "Lyra" and get his back to the nearest pillar before the first proctor came out of the mist behind Sable with a rod already swinging.

They were not soldiers. That was the thing he understood in the first two seconds and never forgot: nobody was shouting, nobody had a sword, and there was no noise at all. Three figures in grey oilcloth with masks of fine silver mesh, moving through knee-deep water without splashing, because they had been trained in a city where sound brings the Cacophony and a screaming subject lengthens the queue.

The rods were lead-cored and wrapped in felt. They were made to break a person quietly.

The first blow took Sable across the shoulder-blades and put her face-down in the water.

The Ledger did not fall. It hung exactly where it had been, at the height of a writing-desk, open, its pages turning, over its drowning owner — and the proctor reached for it with his free hand and his hand went through it like smoke, which told Kaelen something useful about the book and nothing useful about the next four minutes.

He came off the pillar and hit the man in the side of the head with the glass.

Ribs. His ribs went off like a dropped tray the instant he swung, and the pain folded him a half-beat early, so what should have been a killing blow went in shallow and low and took the proctor across the jaw instead of the temple. The mesh mask tore. The man went sideways into the water and came up on one knee with his jaw hanging visibly wrong, and he *still did not make a sound*, and he went for his belt.

What came off the belt was not a weapon.

It was brass, about the size of a hand, shaped like a squat two-tined fork with a glass ampoule seated between the tines, and Kaelen had seen the drawings in a supply schedule and had never in his life seen one held.

A hand-press.

The proctor struck it against the pillar.

The tone went through the plaza — a clean bright seven hundred and forty-one, held and swelling, the same note the drowned Anchor under this city had held for ten thousand years — and every piece of glass within thirty feet answered it, including the piece Kaelen was wearing.

His arm shrieked.

It was not pain in the arm; there was no pain in the arm and had not been since 412. It was pain in the *seam*, the frost-line across his chest where glass met him, and in the bone behind it, and it went through his shoulder and down into his gut, and his left arm locked rigid from fingertip to collarbone like a bar of iron and would not answer him at all.

He went down on one knee in the water with a dead arm and three broken ribs and a man coming at him with a press in his fist and eight seconds of contact between him and being nobody.

---

Lyra hit the proctor with a marble finial.

It was the ornament off the top of the basin's corner post: a ball of white stone the size of a small melon, three years loose in its socket, and she got it up out of the water with both hands and brought it down on the back of his skull as he bent over Kaelen.

The mesh mask did nothing. The stone went through it into the bone and there was a sound like a boot in gravel, and the man dropped into the plaza and the water went dark lilac around him and kept going dark, spreading.

Lyra stood over him with the finial still in her hands, breathing through her mouth.

She had never in her life hurt anything larger than an apple-grub.

She dropped the stone and turned and was sick into the water, and while she was doing it the second proctor came around the pillar at her with his rod up.

---

Here is what a Cantor is for.

She could not sing. She could not scream a warning. But she had spent thirty-three years learning to hear a room the way other people see one, and for five days, on a crowded deck, with no voice at all, she had been doing nothing but listen.

She heard him coming. Not his feet — he moved through water without splashing, which was the whole of their training. She heard his *breathing*: a man working hard through a mesh mask, a fast wet rasp with a catch in it, coming from her left rear quarter at about eleven feet.

She dropped flat into the water.

The rod went through the space where her head had been, and the proctor's own momentum took him a stride past her into the open, and she came up out of the lilac water with her hand already going into the coat pocket where a Cantor keeps the thing every Cantor carries.

Not a knife. A tuning key: four inches of square steel, heavy in the hand, with a socket at the end for a peg.

She put it into the side of his knee.

He went down at last with a sound — the first sound any of them had made, a short flat grunt bitten off at once — and rolled, and got up, and she was already moving backward, because she was not a fighter and knew it, and what she did next was the only thing left.

She put two fingers in her mouth and whistled.

The falling fifth, sharp as a thrown stone, at a volume the whole plaza could hear: the Root note and down, twice, the signal she had invented on a doorstep for a seven-year-old boy.

Kaelen's head came round.

She pointed. Not at the man in front of her — at the third proctor, the one nobody had seen yet, who had come around the colonnade behind Kaelen with his press already in his hand and was four feet away and closing.

---

Kaelen could not lift his left arm.

He turned on his knee in the water and drove his right shoulder into the man's hips, which is not a technique, and the two of them went down together into a foot of glowing water among the legs of the colonnade.

The press went out of the proctor's hand and skittered away.

Then it was the worst kind of fight there is, which is two men on the ground who both want the same object, in the dark, in water, with one of them wearing an arm that will not move. The proctor got a thumb into the corner of Kaelen's eye. Kaelen got his right hand into the mesh of the mask and pulled until something in it tore and then kept pulling. They rolled, and the water went over Kaelen's face, and he swallowed some of it and it tasted of cold stone and lilac and old drowned marble. The man was stronger than he was, and rested, and unbroken, and his knee was grinding down onto three cracked ribs with the patient weight of someone who has done this before and knows that it works.

Kaelen's vision went grey at the edges.

And out of the grey, quite close, at about the level of the water, a voice said, very clearly:

"Emory Wain."

The proctor stopped.

It was not a flinch. It was a full stop, an absolute hitch in a machine, and it lasted perhaps a second and a half, and in that second and a half the man on top of Kaelen turned his head toward the voice with an expression that could be seen even through a torn silver mask.

Sable was on her hands and knees in the water eight feet away with blood coming out of her nose and down her chin, and the Bound Ledger hung open above her, and she read from it.

"Emory Wain. Twenty-six. Of Cooper's Rise in the Iron Peaks. Admitted to the service of the Office of the Catalogue in the spring of 414 under the standing terms, which require the surrender of the name and the family, so that the proctor may not be leveraged." Her voice was thick and flat and she did not hurry. "Your mother was Hanne. You had a sister called Ivy. You asked the intake clerk whether you could keep the name until the end of the week, and the clerk wrote it in the margin as a matter of interest, and the clerk was me."

Kaelen got his knee up between them and threw the man off and rolled on top of him and hit him in the mask with the only hand that worked until he stopped moving.

---

It took four minutes altogether. He knew, because Tobiah Marsh, who had come down the Cloister road at a dead run with a lantern and arrived in time for none of it, had heard the whistle and looked at his own watch, because he was that sort of young man.

The tally:

One proctor dead in the water by the basin, killed with a marble finial by a village Cantor who had never hurt anything in her life, and who would sit on the Moot-house steps afterward for a long time with her hands hanging between her knees.

One proctor — Emory Wain, twenty-six, of Cooper's Rise — unconscious, jaw broken, wrists bound with his own belt, and still, when he came round an hour later, absolutely silent.

One proctor gone. The second man, the one Lyra had put the tuning key into, had got up and limped away into the mist during the last of it, and by the time anyone thought about him he was three streets off, and he had a name and a number for the ship and four hundred and thirty-two crates' worth of reasons for the Registrar-General to send everything he had.

Kaelen's ribs: two of the three had gone from cracked to broken, and his left arm did not come back until the small hours, and when it did it came back wrong, ringing a full tone flat, and it stayed that way for two days.

And Halder Wick, fifty-eight, joiner, of the Bell Yard, was face-down in the water by his table with his skull broken, because during the fight he had walked into it — had walked into it, at the pace of a man going to answer his own front door, because his loop took him across that corner of the plaza at about that hour and nobody had told him not to — and one of the proctors had put a rod through the back of his head without breaking stride, the way you would push a chair out of a doorway.

The four stones were still on his table. The fifth one, the loaf, had fallen into the water.

Sable waded out to him and knelt down in the lilac glow and turned him over and closed his eyes with her thumb, and then opened the Ledger and wrote the entry while kneeling in the water with the book hanging in front of her: date, name, number, cause, and the last line of it, which Kaelen read over her shoulder because he could not stop himself:

*He set a table for four every evening for three years. I do not know who he was expecting. There is no one left to ask.*

---

She would still not come.

She said it standing on the Moot-house steps at midnight with a broken nose and her skirts black to the waist. "Take the ship out. Now, tonight, before that man reaches the harvest-house. Go south, go to the Archipelago, go anywhere the Office's writ is slow. I will not be on it."

"Why?" said Kaelen.

"Because there is a press four miles north of here in a building I helped design the queue for," said Sable, "and while I am in this city I am a woman who might one day be brave enough to go and take it, and the moment I get on a ship I am a woman who left." She picked up her lamp. "Good night, Engineer. I am sorry about your ribs. Your Cantor saved your life twice and she will not sleep for a month; see that somebody sits with her."

She went down the steps into the water and began to walk out across the plaza toward the colonnade, where two hundred people were walking their loops in the glow with a dead man among them, and Kaelen stood at the top of the steps with his arm hanging ringing and flat and called after her.

"There are four hundred and thirty-two of them in that hold."

She kept walking.

"Forty-one thousand and six," he said, louder. "Annet Sowerby. Six years old. She talks to the boy who sleeps against the third crate. She asked him to leave the lamp on."

Sable stopped.

She stood in the middle of the lit water with her back to him, in the fog, with the Ledger hanging at her elbow, and she did not turn around and she did not answer, and she stood there for a very long time.

---
---

## CHAPTER SIXTEEN
### Manifest

> *Registry OK-114. Consignment: twelve crates. The carrier attests that the consignment travelled dry, secure and unopened.*
> *Signature of carrier: (mark).*
>
> — Carriage note, Office of the Catalogue, Greenleaf 417

*The Cloister road sky-dock, Whisper-Grove. Before dawn.*

---

Vane had spent the night doing sums, which was not his trade, and doing them badly.

Twenty-one survivors out of a town of four hundred. Nine children. Two hundred gallons of water for thirty-odd people. Eleven feet of deck-plank pulled up and thrown overboard with a man in it. Four hundred and thirty-two crates' worth of somebody else's people in the hold, twelve crates to a load, eleven loads, and a number underneath all of it that he had refused to total since the first night out of Oakhaven and which had been totalling itself anyway, quietly, in the back of his skull, the way a leak fills a bilge.

He was at the stern rail with the empty bottle when Mags put a hand on his shoulder and pointed down the mast-ladder.

A woman was climbing it. In the dark, in a sodden grey skirt, with one hand on the rungs and the other arm held against her body wrong, and her face — when she came up over the rail and into the lantern-light — was a mess: nose broken, both eyes going black, a split lip, blood dried in a fan down her chin and throat.

At her left elbow, keeping pace with her the whole way up a hundred feet of ladder, an open book hung in the air with nothing holding it.

"Captain Vane," she said. "Registry OK-114."

"That's the ship."

"That is not the ship. That is the *carriage registration*." She put her good hand on the rail and stood breathing for a moment. "The ship has had four names. The registration has had one, and it is on eleven of my consignment notes with my initials beside it. Do you keep your carriage notes?"

"In the chart locker."

"Then you know exactly who I am," said Sable, "and we can save each other an hour."

---

He took her down into the hold.

He did not decide to. He was aware, going down the ladder ahead of her with the lantern, that he had not decided anything; he had simply stood aside from himself and watched a man lead an archivist of the Silent Veil into his own crime, which was a thing he had been half-hoping somebody would make him do since the night he opened the third crate.

The hold was as it always was: pine, straw, cold glass, and the murmur.

Sable stopped at the bottom of the ladder.

She stood with one hand still on the rung and looked down the length of the hold at twelve crates in two stacks, and the sound came up around her — the man counting, the woman giving directions to somewhere, the laughter at a joke told a long time ago, and four hundred other things underneath — and Vane watched her face do something that he did not have a word for and that he did not think he would ever forget. It was not horror. It was recognition. It was the face of somebody meeting a room full of people she knows by name at a party where nobody can see her.

"Bring the lamp," she said.

She went along the stacks reading the stencils. Then she knelt at the third crate, the open one, and looked at the tags on their strings without touching a single sphere, and read them out under her breath the way a woman reads a shelf of preserves in her own larder.

"Forty-one thousand and four. Ivo Renn, forty-one, cooper, of Ashlane; his wife is at the bottom of this row, they were taken within the hour. Forty-one thousand and nine, Ketter Dell, nineteen, fuller's apprentice, who said he was twenty. Forty-one thousand and twelve. Forty-one thousand and thirteen — those are the Aldritch brothers, they came in holding hands and the clerk had to write the second one's details twice because the ink ran." Her voice did not change at all. "Forty thousand nine hundred and ninety-one. That is Mirren Salk, twenty-six, wheelwright's widow. She was carrying her husband's coat and they let her keep it as far as the door."

"You've got the book open," said Vane. His voice came out wrong. "You're reading it off the book."

"The book is at my elbow and it is closed, Captain," said Sable, "and I have not looked at it since I came down that ladder. I wrote seventy-four thousand six hundred and eleven of them in eleven days and I have read twenty of them aloud every night for three years and I am not going to pretend to you that I need a book."

---

She stood up. She put her good hand flat on the lid of the third crate.

"Now," she said. "How many?"

"Four hundred and thirty-two. It's on the—"

"Not in the hold, Captain. How many altogether."

He had known the question was coming since the mast-ladder. He had known it, if he was honest, since a clerk with ink on his cuffs had counted his crates at Oakhaven and said *they do carry on, don't they*, pleasantly, and written twelve in a tally-book.

"Thirteen hundred," said Vane. "About."

"One thousand three hundred and forty-two," said Sable.

The murmur went on around them in the straw.

"Eleven consignments between Greenleaf of 417 and Ashfall of this year," she said. "Twelve crates, then sixteen, then twenty-four and twenty-four and twenty-four and twenty-four; the carriage office increased your load after the fourth run because you had not complained about the noise. There is a note to that effect in the file. It says: *carrier OK-114 is reliable and does not ask questions.*"

Vane put his hand out and found a crate and sat down on it.

"I want to do something for you," said Sable, "and I want you to understand before I do it that it is not a kindness, and that I have thought about it for the whole of the climb up your ladder, and that I am going to do it anyway."

"Do what?"

"Read you three."

"I don't—"

"You have been carrying one thousand three hundred and forty-two people for two years as *cargo*," she said, "and the reason you were able to do it is that cargo does not have a mother or a trade or a last thing it said at a door. I can give you three of them back. Not to them. To you. It will not help them in the slightest and it will not absolve you in the slightest, and you will carry them for the rest of your life, and that is the point, Captain. Somebody should be carrying them properly."

Vane sat on a crate in his own hold with an empty bottle in his hand and looked at a woman with a broken nose in the lantern-light.

"Go on, then," he said.

---

The Bound Ledger came round in front of her and opened itself, and she did not look at it.

"Entry thirty-nine thousand, eight hundred and two," said Sable. "Corwin Aye. Fifty-three. Cooper of the Ashlane, and a good one; there is a barrel in the Moot-house cellar with his mark burned into the head and it has not leaked in nineteen years. Taken on the fourth day of the harvest, in the ninth hour. He was pressed in the second room because the first room had a queue. What he said at the door was: *will I be able to keep my hands?*" She paused exactly long enough. "He carried in the second consignment, Captain. Your sixteen crates. He was in your hold for eleven days."

Vane did not move.

"Entry forty thousand, six hundred and ten. Mirren Salk. Twenty-six. Wheelwright's widow, of the Bell Yard, four months a widow when the drills came. Taken on the ninth day. She was carrying her husband's coat over her arm and she asked whether she might keep it, and the clerk said that she might, and it was taken off her at the door of the pressing room and put in a bin with eleven hundred other coats." Sable's voice stayed perfectly flat. "She is in the stack behind you. Fourth consignment."

The lantern hissed.

"Entry forty-one thousand and six," said Sable. "Annet Sowerby. Six years old. Daughter of Hesper and Tam Sowerby of the Cloister road. Taken on the eleventh day, in the hour after noon, with the rest of the little ones from the Cloister of the Lost Choir, because the Office had a requisition for small clear voices and the Cloister had forty-one of them in a choir under one roof." A beat. "She was third in the line. She would not let go of the hand of the boy in front of her, and rather than make a scene in front of forty children, the proctors took the two of them into the room together and pressed them in the same hour. I wrote both entries. Hers is legible. His is not, because my hand had begun to shake by then, and I have never been able to read the surname since, and there is no one left in this city to ask."

Vane heard, from somewhere very close by, the sound of a man's breath going out of him and not coming back in.

It was his own.

"She's in the third crate," he said. "Second row, corner. She talks to a boy. He's seven. He's asleep against that crate right now."

"I know," said Sable. "Your engineer told me. It is why I came up the ladder."

---

He got up at some point and stood with both hands on the edge of the open crate and made himself look at thirty-six spheres of lilac glass in good straw packed by somebody careful.

"Can it be undone?" he said.

"I don't know."

"You said you were the list, not the cure. Fine. Somebody knows."

"Yes."

"Who?"

"The press," said Sable. "There are two of them in the harvest-house four miles north of this dock, in the old malting on the Cloister road, and there are eleven Office staff in that building, and a proctor limped out of the Weeping Plaza four hours ago who will reach it by dawn and tell them the registry number of this ship." She said it without any change of tone at all. "So we have perhaps a day. I have wanted to walk into that building for three years and I have never once been able to make myself do it, and now there is a ship at this dock that can carry a book, and forty-one children of the Cloister choir in your hold, and one of them is talking to a boy through the side of a crate. So I find that I am going to be able to do it after all. That is not courage, Captain. That is arithmetic."

"And when we've got the thing?"

"Then I read the book to it," said Sable, "and we find out what the press does when you turn it the other way round, and whether anything comes back through it, and how much of me it costs to send one. And if the answer is that it costs three, then three people get their names back out of one thousand three hundred and forty-two." She looked at him. "Which is not enough. Which is why I have spent three years not doing it. I would rather you understood that before you decide anything."

Vane turned the empty bottle over in his hands.

"Three out of thirteen hundred," he said.

"One thousand three hundred and forty-two."

"Aye." He set the bottle down on a crate. "Well. I'm a smuggler, and smugglers don't do sums we like the look of. We do the one in front of us and then we do the next one." He wiped his mouth with the back of his hand. "You'll want the press moved. It'll be iron and brass and a stone bed under it, I should think, and bolted, and you'll not carry it out under your arm with a broken wing. That's a ship's job. That's my job."

Sable looked at him for a moment.

"Your ship is down by the head and overloaded and there are twenty-one refugees and nine children on her deck," she said.

"Yes."

"And you are going to fly her at an Office building."

"I'm going to fly her *near* an Office building," said Vane, "which is a different thing entirely, and which I will explain to you at some length when I've worked out what the difference is."

---

Pim Carder was awake when they came back down the hold.

He had not been asleep for a while, Vane suspected; he had the particular rigid stillness of a child who has been listening to adults and has understood some of it. He sat up in the straw with his hands out stiff in front of him and looked at the woman with the broken face and the book floating at her elbow with no fear whatever, because he was seven and had already seen the worst thing in the world twice.

"Are you the one who writes the names?" he said.

Sable stopped.

"Yes," she said.

"Annet's frightened," said Pim. "She can hear people moving about but she can't hear what anyone says unless you're right up on the box. I told her we were going home and she said which home." He looked up at Sable. "Can you tell her? She'd believe you. You've got the book."

The archivist of the Silent Veil stood in the hold of a smuggler's skyship for a moment with her one good hand at her side.

Then she got down on her knees in the straw — awkwardly, one-handed, with her ribs and her face and her arm — and leaned in until her mouth was a hand's width from the pine of the third crate, and the Ledger came down and hung beside her head, and she said, in the flat clear voice of a woman reading a will:

"Annet Sowerby. You are six years old. Your mother is Hesper and your father is Tam, and you live on the Cloister road, and you sang alto in the little choir under Mistress Dray, and you would not let go of the hand of the boy in front of you." She stopped, and her voice did not change, but she put her hand flat on the wood. "You are entry forty-one thousand and six. You are not lost. You are *filed*. And I am four miles from the place where they put you in there, and I am coming with the ship."

Inside the crate, quite close, a small voice said:

"*Is that you, Mistress Dray?*"

Sable shut her eyes.

"No, love," she said. "It's the clerk."

---
---

## CHAPTER SEVENTEEN
### The Cloister of the Lost Choir

> *Requisition 44: small clear voices, forty (40). Source: charitable choir, Cloister road, Whisper-Grove. Note: one roof, one hour, one wagon. Efficient.*
>
> — Office of the Catalogue, requisition file, Ashfall 416

*The Cloister road. Nullday, first light.*

---

The Cloister of the Lost Choir had been called something else before 416. Nobody could remember what.

That was the joke Sable made about it, walking up the road in the fog with her arm strapped across her chest, and it was not a joke, and nobody laughed. The building was low and long and built of good grey stone, with a slate roof and a bell-cote with no bell in it, and silver willows growing so close on the north side that their branches had come in through two windows and made themselves at home.

"Two hundred yards past the orchard wall is the Malting," Sable said. "That is the harvest-house. Eleven staff, two presses, a queue-yard, and a sky-dock they do not use because the Office's freight comes by road. We will see the chimneys from the choir-room windows. I stood in that room twice and looked at those chimneys and came home."

"Then we go in through the choir-room," said Vane.

"Yes."

"Is there anything in the choir-room?"

"There was an Aegis pattern on the requisition," said Sable. "Office property is guarded until the Office withdraws it. The Office has not withdrawn it."

Mags, who had been reading her lips by lantern-light and had missed nothing, made one short gesture: *how big.*

"Nine feet," said Sable.

---

Lyra went in first, because she was the only one of them who had a reason to want to.

The door had been left open. Three winters had come in through it: leaf-mould in the porch, a drift of it against the inner door, and on the flagstones the pale corkscrews of seedlings that had come up and died for want of light.

Inside, the choir-room went back forty feet in the grey dawn.

It was a good room. That was the first thing she thought, and she thought it in the language of her trade: it was a *good room*, high and narrow with a barrel ceiling of plastered lath, the sort of room that gives a young voice back to itself just slightly improved, so that a child who is not sure she can sing stands in it and hears herself and decides that she can.

Down the left-hand wall, at child height, a row of iron pegs.

There were cloaks on them. Eleven cloaks, small ones, wool, mostly brown, one of them red and much better made than the others. Three years of damp had taken them; they hung in their own shapes, furred green along the shoulders, and under each peg was a pair of boots or clogs, set neatly, toes to the wall.

Lyra stood in front of the red cloak for a while.

At the far end of the room were the risers: three tiers of oak, waxed by generations of small hands, with the middle tier worn into shallow dips where children had stood in the same eleven places for a century. Chalk crosses on the boards. And behind them, on the wall, the hymn board — a slate frame with the number chalked up for the last practice anyone had held in this room.

**HYMN 9.**

And on the floor between the risers and the door, a shoe. One. Small.

Kaelen came in behind her and stopped.

She heard him not say anything, and was grateful, and then she heard a second thing, underneath, and turned round with her hand up.

*Listen.*

Very faint, very slow, from the dark at the back of the room beyond the risers: a sound like a cupboard full of plates settling.

Then the chime.

---

It came out of the alcove behind the risers and it did not hurry, and it was nine feet if it was an inch.

Bronze and iron, green-black with three years of damp, built like a man in the way a bell is built like a mouth — a barrel chest, two long arms, legs of piston and strut that unfolded, that *unfolded*, so that the thing stood up out of its alcove in three separate motions and had to duck its head under the barrel ceiling. There was no face. There was a grille where a face would be, and behind the grille, dim, a single point of lilac light.

Across its chest-plate, stencilled in the Office's block capitals and kept clean of verdigris by something, three years after the last child had left this room:

**DEFEND THE SINGERS**

"Out," said Vane. "Out, out, back up, *out*—"

It hit Mags first because Mags was nearest the risers.

It did not swing at her. It *stepped*, and a piston in its shoulder let go with a crack, and the arm came across in a flat arc at the speed of a falling gate, and it took her off her feet and into the wall under the pegs twelve feet away with a sound Lyra felt in her own ribs. Plaster came down. One of the cloaks fell off its peg onto the floor.

Mags got up.

That was the moment Lyra understood how the next four minutes were going to go, because Mags got up with one arm hanging and blood coming out of her hairline and her mouth shut, and the Aegis had already turned its head an inch to track her, and the point of lilac light behind the grille had not changed at all.

Kaelen went at it with the glass.

He got one clean strike in — the arm came round and up and the fist went into the thing's flank with a noise like a cathedral door slamming, and Lyra saw the bronze *dent*, and saw Kaelen's whole body stop dead against three hundredweight of standing iron that did not move an inch.

Then it hit him.

Not with the arm. It brought its chest round and *opened* — a row of louvres in the barrel of its torso lifting like the ribs of a fan — and out of the chest came a chord.

Lyra had spent her life inside sound and she had never heard anything like it. Nine tuned bars and three bells, struck at once, at close range, in a stone room built to give voices back: a great bronze shout of a chord with a bite to it, and it went through the choir-room and off the barrel ceiling and came down on all of them at once.

Her knees went. The lantern went out of her hand. She was on the flags with her palms over her ears and her mouth open and the after-ring rolling around inside her skull, and she could not get her eyes to hold still. Ten feet away, Vane was down on one knee, retching. Sable had gone over sideways into the risers with her bad arm under her and was not moving.

Kaelen was on the floor screaming.

His arm had done what it did at the plaza and worse: the glass had caught the chord and held it, and was ringing with it, and could not stop, and the whole limb had gone rigid from fingertip to collarbone with the sound still pouring through it, and he was on his back on the flagstones of a children's choir-room with his own arm ringing him apart.

The Aegis took a step toward the nearest singer.

That was the part Lyra could not make sense of afterward, lying awake: it did not go for Kaelen, who had struck it. It went for the *risers* — for Sable, who had fallen into them — because the risers were where the singers stood, and the directive on its chest was four words long and had no clause in it for who was standing there.

And Mags Tennant, who had heard none of it, walked through the middle of the chord.

---

Deafness is not silence. Mags had told Vane that once, in hand-cant, at some length, and he had repeated it to Lyra on the deck two nights before without knowing why he was telling her. *You think it's a room with the door shut. It isn't. It's a room that was never built.*

So the chord that put four people on the floor of the Cloister of the Lost Choir did nothing at all to the bosun of the *Drift-Kestrel*, except move the air against her face.

She crossed twenty feet of flagstone with one arm swinging dead, got both hands into the collar of Kaelen's coat, and dragged him backward out of the thing's path in three long heaves, and then put herself between the Aegis and the risers with her boots planted and her one good fist up, which was, Lyra thought later, the single most ridiculous and magnificent thing she had ever seen a human being do.

The Aegis stopped.

It looked at her. There is no other word; the grille came round and the lilac point behind it steadied on her face, and for a moment the whole machine was absolutely still, and something inside its chest went *tick*, three times, unevenly, like a clock being wound by somebody nervous.

Then it lifted the arm.

---

Lyra was on her feet without knowing she had got up.

She could not shout. She could not sing. She had six days of nothing in her throat and a hole under her jaw and Bettany's *not for weeks*, and there was no version of the next four seconds in which a Cantor's voice was of the slightest use to anybody.

So she did the only thing she had left, which was the thing she had done in a water-meadow at dusk with a whistle and a seven-year-old, and the thing she had done in a lit plaza with a tuning key and two fingers in her mouth.

She pointed at the hymn board.

Sable, on her back in the risers, with her eyes streaming from the chord, turned her head and looked at where the Cantor was pointing.

**HYMN 9.**

And Lyra saw her understand — saw the whole thing land on the woman in about a second and a half, because Sable was the fastest reader of a situation Lyra had ever met and that was exactly her curse — and saw her face go the colour of the plaster.

"No," said Sable.

Lyra pointed again, hard.

"I can't. I have never sung in my *life*. I don't—" She was scrambling backward up the risers on one elbow as nine feet of iron came at her. "I stood in the *doorway*. I stood in the doorway with the intake sheets and they were singing it while we counted them, do you understand, they sang it the *whole way through the first eleven names*—"

Lyra got up onto the bottom riser in front of her, and turned, and put her back to the machine, and lifted both hands in front of the archivist's face in the oldest gesture of her trade — palms up, thumbs together, the way a Cantor asks a frightened child in a sickroom for a note.

*Give it to me.*

And Sable, entry-writer of the Office of the Catalogue, who had signed seventy-four thousand six hundred and eleven people into a book and had not sung since she was a girl, opened her mouth and sang Hymn 9 of the Cloister of the Lost Choir.

It was terrible. It came out flat and cracked and far too low, in the wrong key, in a voice that had been reading aloud and nothing else for three years:

*"Hold us in the little light — hold us in the little light until the morning comes—"*

The Aegis Sentinel stopped with its arm at the top of its swing.

---

It stood like that for the length of two more lines, while Sable sang the hymn to the floor of the room she had counted children in.

Then the louvres in its chest closed, one rib at a time.

And it knelt.

Nine feet of Office ironwork went down on one knee-strut on the flagstones with a crash that brought more plaster off the ceiling, and put its head down below the level of the risers, which is where a head goes when you are nine feet tall and you are looking up at somebody standing on the bottom tier.

And then, out of the shut chest, very quietly, it chimed.

Three bells and two bars. Not the tune Sable was singing.

The line under it.

Lyra's hands went up over her mouth. She knew what she was hearing the way you know your own name: it was the second line of a three-part hymn, the middle voice, the alto — the part you give to the children who are steady and can hold a note against a tune that is trying to pull them off it. It was in time. It was in tune. It was being played by a machine that had knelt down to do it, in a room where forty-one children had sung that exact part three years ago while a clerk in the doorway wrote their names in a book.

Sable stopped singing.

The Aegis went on for one more bar, alone, and then it stopped too, and waited — chest shut, head down, the lilac light steady — the way a child waits, on a riser, for the next line.

---

Afterward:

Mags: collarbone broken, three ribs, sixteen stitches in the scalp, and would not sit down until Vane physically sat on her.

Kaelen: the arm came back over the course of an hour, the way a leg comes back from being slept on, and rang a tone and a half flat for the rest of the day. When he could stand, he went out to the porch and was sick in the leaf-mould, which he seemed to find more humiliating than the screaming, and then came back in and measured the tideline with a piece of string, because he had left the tailor's tape in a hut in Oakhaven, and did not tell anybody the number.

Vane: bruised, deaf in one ear till noon, and unable to look at the row of pegs.

Sable: she got down off the risers, and stood in front of the kneeling Aegis for a long moment, and then she did the thing that Lyra would think about for months.

She stepped up and put her hand on the grille in its chest — the small grille low down under the stencilled words, where the core sits — and tilted her head to read the serial stamped in the bronze beside it in the Office's blocky numerals.

Lyra was four feet away. She saw the number too.

**WG-41,005.**

She did not know what it meant. She knew what it did to Sable, which was that every bit of colour went out of the woman's face in the space of one breath, and her hand came off the bronze as though it had burned her, and she took one step back and then another.

"Sable?"

She did not answer. She looked at the kneeling machine for perhaps five seconds.

Then she said, in a perfectly level voice, "We should move. The Malting has a shift-bell at the seventh hour and we want the yard crossing before it," and turned and walked out of the choir-room into the fog.

The Aegis Sentinel got up and followed them.

Nobody invited it. Nobody could have stopped it. It came out through the door it had been left to guard for three years, ducking its head, nine feet of Office property walking out of Office property, and fell in behind Lyra Ashgrove at a distance of about eight paces and stayed there, and when she stopped, it stopped.

She looked back at it once from the orchard wall. It had the red cloak from the floor of the choir-room hanging over the fingers of its left hand, picked up in passing, carried the way you carry something you mean to give back.

---
---

## CHAPTER EIGHTEEN
### The Harvest-House

> *Queue discipline is the whole of the work. A subject who has been standing for two hours and has been told three times what will be asked will answer in under nine seconds. A subject who is surprised will take a minute and upset the line behind her.*
>
> — S., *Notes Toward an Efficient Intake*, Office of the Catalogue, 416

*The Malting, Cloister road. Nullday morning.*

---

She had written the pamphlet.

It was four pages long. It had no author's name on it, only an initial, because the Office did not put names on procedural documents; and it had been copied out and sent to every harvest-house in the Spectral Woods and then, in 417, to the Iron Peaks and the Caldera, and Registrar-General Quist had told her at the time, kindly, that it was the most useful thing anyone in his department had produced in twenty years.

Nine seconds. That was the figure she had found. A subject who has been prepared answers the three questions in nine seconds; an unprepared one takes a minute; a minute per subject across four thousand subjects is sixty-six hours of daylight you do not have, and a queue that stands overnight in the cold will start asking each other questions and then you have a different problem altogether.

So: shelter over the queue-yard. Benches for the old. Water. A clerk walking the line every twenty minutes telling everyone, pleasantly, what would be asked. Name. Age. Trade. A second clerk at the door of the first room to repeat it. And a man at the end — she had specified this, in writing, on page three — to take coats and bundles and give a numbered ticket, because a subject holding a possession will hesitate at the threshold and a subject who has handed it over will not.

She had solved the whole thing in a fortnight. She had been proud of the benches.

---

The Malting had been a brewer's before it was a harvest-house: a long brick range with two kiln-chimneys at the north end and a cobbled yard behind a wall. From the orchard she could see the shelter over the queue-yard — her shelter, built to her specification, tarred canvas on a timber frame, forty feet by twelve — and the benches under it, and the water butt, and the man at the trestle with the ticket-book.

There was a queue under the shelter.

Sable stood in the wet grass with the fog coming off the orchard and looked at it for a long moment and could not make it make sense. Whisper-Grove had been finished in 416. There was nobody left in the Spectral Woods to stand in a queue.

Then the wind moved, and she saw what they were wearing, and she understood.

Reach clothes. Homespun and sacking and good boots caked to the ankle in dried green; two carters' smocks; a woman in the grey apron of a dye-works. Forty, perhaps fifty people, sitting on her benches with bundles between their feet, in a queue-yard with a painted board at the head of it that read, in fresh Office lettering:

**RELIEF STATION — REACH DISPLACED**
**FOOD · SHELTER · TRANSIT**
**REGISTRATION REQUIRED**

"They walked," said Kaelen beside her. His voice had gone somewhere flat and strange. "Out of the Greening. Down the Greenroot road, that's — that's four days on foot. They'd have come out at Wend's Ford and the Office would have—" He stopped. "They're registering them."

"They are harvesting them," said Sable. "Registration is the first room."

The shift-bell rang in the Malting, the seventh hour, and the queue shuffled up two feet, patiently, the way a well-run queue does.

---

The plan was Vane's and it was not elegant.

Kaelen, Vane and Mags would go in at the kiln end, where the Office's freight door stood open for the wagons and where, according to a three-year-old requisition, the presses were bedded on stone. They would take one. Mags could not lift with a broken collarbone, so Mags would hold the door and look enormous, which she could still do. Tobiah had come down the road with them and would run the tackle.

And Sable would walk in the front.

"Say that part again," Vane had said.

"Eleven staff," said Sable. "Nine of them are clerks and two of them are proctors, and every one of them has been told for three years that the Office's great outstanding irregularity is a scribe who walked off with a bound ledger. If I present myself at the clerks' hall, every person in that building who is not physically holding a subject will come and look at me. Including the Registrar-General, who arrived this morning; that is his carriage in the yard."

"And when they take you?"

"Then they take me in the clerks' hall, which is at the south end, a hundred and ten feet from the kiln floor, behind two doors." She had looked at him. "Captain, I have been within a quarter-mile of this building twice in three years and both times I went home. I am not able to creep in at the back of it. I have discovered that I am able to walk in the front. Let me use the only door I can get through."

---

The clerks' hall smelled of ink and wet wool and, faintly, under it, of the vinegar they cleaned the desks with on the last day of every month.

That was the thing that nearly stopped her on the threshold. Not the ledgers, not the shelves, not the intake sheets pinned in their hundreds on the boards — the *vinegar*, which was one of the three things she still had, and which she had been saying out loud to herself every night for three years in a plaza full of drowned marble, and which was suddenly in her nose, in a room, being real.

Nine clerks looked up.

"Good morning," said Sable. "My name is not on the door of this Office any more, but you will all have seen it at the foot of the intake procedure. I have the Bound Ledger of Whisper-Grove with me. I imagine somebody should be sent for."

The Ledger came round from her elbow and hung in the air, open, turning its pages, and forty feet of clerks' hall went absolutely silent.

Registrar-General Pellam Quist came out of the inner office nine seconds later, which she noted, because nine seconds was her own number.

He was a small, neat, elderly man with soft white hair and half-moon spectacles and ink on the side of his hand, and he had taught her how to rule a page when she was nineteen years old.

"Oh," he said, and his face did something complicated and unguarded, and for one horrible moment she thought he was going to be glad. "Oh, my dear girl. You look *dreadful*. Somebody find her a chair — Renshaw, a chair, and hot water. Your nose is broken."

"Registrar-General."

"Sit *down*, Sable."

She sat down, because it put her in a chair a hundred and ten feet and two doors from the kiln floor, with nine clerks and a Registrar-General looking at her, and because her legs had stopped being reliable somewhere around the vinegar.

---

He did not threaten her. She had known he would not; she had told the others so, in the orchard, and had not been believed.

He sat on the edge of a desk, which he had always done, and he talked to her for eleven minutes in a soft reasonable voice while a clerk cleaned her face with hot water, and he did not once raise it.

He said that the theft of the Ledger had been a great blow, but that the Office's own duplicate schedules were intact, so no data had been lost, which was the main thing.

He said that he had never blamed her. That the eleven days had been hard on everybody, that two of the clerks had never come back to work afterward, and that he had personally seen to it that both of them kept their pensions.

He said that the work in this building was relief work; that the Reach displaced were being fed, sheltered and moved on; and that registration was a requirement of the transit warrant, and that yes, a proportion of the registrations would result in a requisition, because the Legions' cores burn out at a rate of eleven a month per garrison and the alternative to requisition was conscription, and had she ever, in all her years of objecting, proposed an alternative that fed and housed the displaced?

He said: "You never once wrote down an alternative, my dear. You wrote down the queue."

And then he said the thing she had not braced for, because she had braced for cruelty and got carpentry instead.

"There is a desk free at the second window," said Pellam Quist. "You may have it back this afternoon. Nobody will ask you to attend a pressing; I know you never could. You could go on doing what you are doing now — you are reading names to them, aren't you, in the plaza? The proctors report it. You could do that with the Office's own ledgers open in front of you, and be warm, and eat, and not have a broken nose." He took his spectacles off and polished them. "I have wanted to say that to you for three years. I am aware that it sounds like a trap. It is not a trap. It is a desk."

Sable looked at the boards of pinned intake sheets, and the shelves, and the door at the far end with the brass plate that said PRESSING ROOM I.

"Registrar-General," she said. "Would you show me a pressing?"

Quist put his spectacles back on.

"Why?"

"Because I have written seventy-four thousand entries about a thing I have never watched," said Sable, "and I find that I am not able to accept a chair from you until I have."

---

He took her himself.

Pressing Room I was the old malt-store: whitewashed, clean, well lit by two high windows, with a drain in the floor and a smell of hot brass. There was a stone bed in the middle of it with the press bolted down on top: a frame of iron the size of a loom, and in the frame, mounted at the height of a seated person's breastbone, the brass fork — two tines a forearm long, with the glass ampoule socketed between them and a striker on a lever.

There were two staff and a subject.

The subject was a man of about thirty in a Reach smock, sitting on the stool with his hands on his knees, and he was being talked to, kindly, by a clerk with a clipboard.

"Name."

"Ansel Hay."

"Age."

"Thirty-two."

"Trade."

"Ah — I did the sluices. On the Flume." He swallowed. "Is it long, sir? Only they said there's food after."

"There's food after," agreed the clerk. "Sit back. Shoulders against the rest. That's it."

The second man brought the fork down until the tines rested on either side of the sternum, one on each side of the breastbone, not hard.

"Deep breath," said the clerk, "and hold it."

And struck the lever.

Sable had heard the note before at a distance of twelve feet through a shut door, forty or fifty times, in 416, while she worked in the next room. She had never heard it from inside the room.

Seven hundred and forty-one hertz. A single clean tone, perfectly held, much longer than a struck fork has any business sounding, because the ampoule between the tines is not glass but Aether-Glass, and it does not ring — it *drinks*, and what it drinks came out of the Anchor under this building ten thousand years ago and knows what a person is.

Ansel Hay's eyes went wide.

The tone went on. Eight seconds is not a long time to read about. It is a very long time to stand in a whitewashed room and watch a man's face while it happens: the first second, surprise; the second and third, a kind of straining, as though he were trying to remember something urgently; and at four, five, six, the thing she would have given every year of her remaining life not to have seen, which was that he began to look *relieved*.

At seven he stopped looking like anybody.

At eight the clerk lifted the lever, and the ampoule in the fork had gone from clear to a soft lilac, and the man on the stool sat with his hands on his knees and his eyes open and his mouth a little apart, and did not get up, and did not need to be held, and went on breathing.

"Gently now," said the clerk to the second man, and they took him by the elbows and walked him out through the far door into the yard, where he would be given food, because a Hollow eats if the food is put where its loop goes.

The whole thing had taken about a minute and a half. She had costed it at ninety seconds in the pamphlet. They were exactly on her figure.

---

"Now," said Pellam Quist, at her shoulder, not unkindly. "You have seen it."

"Put one back," said Sable.

"I beg your—"

"The press took it. The press can return it; the schedule of the machine is symmetrical or it could not have been designed at all, and you have known that for twenty years, and there is a line in the maintenance manual that says the polarity is not to be reversed under any circumstances, which is not a line you put in a manual about a thing that cannot be done." She was already walking to the shelves along the west wall, where the day's cores stood in their racks with their tags. "You have never done it. You have never done it because of the Office's *object*, which is the cores, and a returned name is a core spent. Not because it is impossible."

"Sable."

"Bettis Renn," she said. "Forty thousand one hundred and two. She is in the yard of this building. I saw her carrying baskets on my way in. Her core is in the long-term rack because her husband's was requisitioned and the pair were broken up and the Office does not like to break a pair, so hers has been sitting on that shelf for three years." She found it. WG-40,102, in a clerk's hand, on a string. "And I am going to put her back, and you are going to let me, because there are nine clerks in this building and two proctors and one of the proctors is in the yard and the other one came in from Whisper-Grove at dawn with a smashed knee, and the woman in front of you has nothing left to lose and a book that cannot be taken off her."

Quist looked at her for a long moment over the half-moon spectacles.

"You have never seen it done the other way," he said.

"No."

"No," he agreed, softly. "Nor has anyone else alive. Go on, then, my dear. I have wanted to know for thirty years, and I was never going to be the one who tried it."

---

They brought Bettis Renn in from the yard.

She came without any trouble at all, because Hollows do. She was thirty-eight years old, in a Whisper-Grove apron three years filthy, and her hands moved as she stood there: the small repeated gesture of a woman setting something down and picking it up again, which was, Sable knew, because she had written it in the margin of the entry on the ninth day of the harvest, the gesture of a woman putting a coat over her arm.

They sat her on the stool.

Sable put the core into the ampoule socket herself. It seated with a small click. She brought the fork down until the tines rested either side of the woman's breastbone, and her hand on the lever was perfectly steady, which surprised her.

Then she opened the Bound Ledger, which came round and hung in front of her at the height of a writing-desk, and found the entry she had written three years ago in a room twelve feet from this one, and read it aloud in the flat clear voice of a woman reading a will.

"Entry forty thousand, one hundred and two. Bettis Renn. Thirty-eight. Wife of Ivo Renn, cooper, of Ashlane. Taken on the fourth day of the harvest, in the sixth hour. She asked to go second."

She struck the lever.

---

The Ledger took its price before the tone had finished.

She had expected pain. There was none. There was a small internal event, like a page turning somewhere behind her sternum, and then a gap, and the gap had no edges, and she stood in a whitewashed room with her hand on a lever and knew, with absolute clarity, that there had been three things in the inventory and that there were now two, and that she could no longer say what the third one had been.

*The scriptorium. Cold stone, tallow, wet wool, vinegar. Fourteen desks; mine by the second window.*

*My name is —*

Still there. Both still there.

Something else had gone, and she would never, for the rest of her life, know what it was, except that she was fairly sure, standing there, that it had been warm and that it had had hands.

And then Bettis Renn began to scream.

---

Nobody had thought about the screaming.

Sable had thought about the moment of recognition; she had allowed herself, walking up the Cloister road in the dark, perhaps four seconds of imagining a woman's eyes coming back on and a hand going up to a mouth. She had not thought past it, because nobody in the history of the Office had ever had to.

Bettis Renn came back into her own head at the age of forty-one, having been six feet from her own front door with her husband's coat over her arm on a Tuesday in 416, and being now in a whitewashed room with a drain in the floor and a fork on her chest and three years missing — and not missing at all, because the whole of it was in there: the queue, and the benches, and the kind clerk who had told her what would be asked, and the eight seconds, and then every single day since. The basket-carrying. The rain. The standing.

That is the thing nobody in the Office had understood, because the Office had never put one back. The Hollow is not asleep. The Hollow is a person with the top lifted off, and underneath, the rest of her goes on standing in the rain for three years and cannot say so.

She remembered all of it.

She came off the stool at Sable with her fingernails, screaming in a voice that had not been used for three years and tore itself apart in the first ten seconds of use, and it took Quist and the clerk and Sable together to hold her, and she was saying words in among it, and the words were: *put it back, put it back, put it back, put it back.*

Sable held a woman she had catalogued on the floor of a pressing room with her one good arm and said, over and over, into her hair, "I'm sorry. I'm sorry. I'm here. Bettis. I'm here. Your husband is on a ship four miles from here, he is in the third stack, he is *whole*—"

Somewhere at the north end of the building, a hundred and ten feet away and two doors off, something enormous fell over on the kiln floor, and men began to shout, and the shift-bell started ringing and did not stop.

Quist, on his knees on the other side of the thrashing woman with his spectacles knocked askew, looked up at Sable across the body of the thing they had between them made.

"Now you understand," he said, quite gently, "why we don't."

---
---

## CHAPTER NINETEEN
### The Tuning Room

> *Engineer V. is not to be informed of the object of Stage 9. His calibration is measurably more accurate when he believes the object is extraction.*
>
> — Arbiterium cover-sheet, Rite of Pure Unison, 411 SR

*The Malting, Cloister road. Nullday morning.*

---

The presses were bedded on stone, exactly as the requisition said, and the requisition had been written by somebody who had never had to move one.

Kaelen got the kiln-floor door open at the sixth minute and had the tackle rigged by the tenth: a two-fall purchase off the malt-hoist beam, which was oak, ten by ten, and had been carrying two-hundredweight sacks since before the Synod existed. Tobiah Marsh ran the fall. Mags stood in the freight doorway with a broken collarbone and a hand-axe and looked enormous. Vane went along the wall lifting things and putting them down again in a manner that suggested that if anybody came in he would be delighted.

The press itself was four hundred pounds of frame and fork and ampoule socket, held down by eight coach-bolts through the stone bed. Kaelen had the plate off the first bolt-box in ninety seconds. He had cracked ribs, an arm that had rung flat since the choir-room, and a spanner, and he worked the way he always worked, which was the only thing he had that still functioned properly.

"Sir," said Tobiah, on the fall. "Sir, there's a door."

There was a door. At the back of the kiln floor, past the racks: a plain one, with a brass plate.

**ARCHIVE — OFFICE OF THE CATALOGUE — RECORDS RETAINED BY STATUTE**

"Bolts," said Kaelen.

"Yes, sir. It's only — you said the Office keeps everything, and if they keep everything—"

"Bolts, Marsh."

But his hands had already stopped.

---

The Office of the Catalogue keeps everything. It is the whole of its nature. It keeps intake sheets and requisitions and carriage notes; it keeps duplicate schedules; and because the Synod's departments bill each other for services and a billing department needs cause, it keeps the cross-files of every other department that has ever asked it for anything.

The Consortium of Bores had asked the Office for forty small clear voices in 411.

The archive was a long low room under the kiln, cold, dry, smelling of dust and brass polish, with shelves to the ceiling and a rolling ladder and a table with a lamp. The shelves on the north wall were cores: hundreds of them in racks, tagged, in the dark, murmuring faintly to each other like a room of sleepers. The shelves on the south wall were paper.

He found the box in four minutes because it was exactly where a man who had spent a decade in the Organum's filing system would have put it.

**CONSORTIUM — DAWNING WORKS — RITE OF PURE UNISON — 411/412.**

He put it on the table and opened it and stood there with the lamp.

His own pitch tables, in his own hand, from the Foundry. The daily calibration returns with his initials at the foot of each one, three hundred of them. The procurement order — one page, two hundred lines, line ninety-four *collars, tuning, brass, forty (40); wire, copper, filament, spools (40); needles, silver, curved (40)* — with *K.V.* against it in his own pen, because a chief engineer signs for what comes into his works, and he had signed for it between a requisition for lamp oil and one for scaffold clips.

Under that, the schedule of the Rite. Under that, the casualty return for the Great Foundry Rupture, eleven thousand and forty at Sol-Luminary, of which thirty-nine were listed as SKY CHOIR (CONSECRATED), which meant the Church had decided they were relics rather than corpses.

And under that, at the bottom of the box, clipped inside a plain grey folder with the Arbiterium's small scales stamped in the corner, four sheets of paper in a small, exact, very beautiful hand.

He had last seen that hand in a title block in the Greenhall in Oakhaven, eleven days ago, improving his own work.

The cover-sheet was dated the ninth of Greenleaf, 411 — eight months before the Fracture.

He read it standing up, with the lamp in his fist, and he read it twice, and then he sat down on the archive steps.

*The object of Stage 9 is not extraction.*

*The Anchors are not deposits and cannot be drained beyond their willingness. Eleven bores in forty years have demonstrated that a sustained extraction produces slippage, shatter, and collapse of the bore-head, and never yields above eleven percent of projection. The Consortium continues to believe this is a mechanical problem. It is not. It is a matter of consent.*

*An Anchor will yield without limit if it believes it has been relieved.*

*Accordingly the Rite of Pure Unison is designed to present to the Anchor of Light, in a form it cannot mistake, the appearance of relief: its own law, held externally, without deviation, for a period long enough to be credited. The instrument is forty throats tuned to a single pitch by wire. The duration is three hundred days. The date of the final morning has been selected to fall within the year the term expires.*

*The projected outcome is that the Anchor will release its law.*

*The secondary outcome, which this office regards as the primary value of the trial, is a demonstration that the method transfers to the remaining seven.*

There was a note at the bottom of the third sheet, in the same hand, smaller.

*Engineer V. is not to be informed of the object of Stage 9. His calibration is measurably more accurate when he believes the object is extraction. He has an unusually exact ear and a conscience of the ordinary sort; the two together are useful only if the second is not engaged. I would not waste him.*

*C. V.*

---

He sat on the archive steps for some time.

Seven years. He had done the arithmetic on that morning perhaps four thousand times: if he had checked the resonance at the risers on the ninth day instead of going back up the gantry; if he had read line ninety-four; if he had shouted a half-minute sooner. He had built an entire life out of the certainty that a better man standing where he had stood would have stopped it.

And here was a grey folder, eight months older than the morning, which said: *no, he would not have. That was the design. You were the part of the machine that had to believe.*

The relief came up in him like water in a cellar, and he put his head in his hands, and hated himself with a violence that astonished him, because he had no right to it — because relief is a thing you are allowed only if the dead get something out of it, and Coralie of the Sky Choir, fifteen years old, second riser, left shoulder, got nothing out of it whatsoever.

He put the four sheets inside his shirt.

Then he became aware that the room had been getting louder for some time.

---

It was the cores.

He had been hearing them since he came down the steps and had taken them for what they always were, four hundred sleepers murmuring in a rack. They were not doing that now. The whole north wall had come up in pitch — a hundred and forty voices in lilac glass, all saying different things, but saying them at the same *rate*, the way a crowd in a square falls into step without meaning to.

And under them, through the floor, through the stone bed the presses stood on, through the four hundred feet of clay and rock beneath that, the Anchor of Memory was reciting.

He had heard the Growth Chorister sing a lullaby that kept losing the tune. This was not that.

This was a voice going through a list.

It had the exact cadence of a thing being recited — the small pause, the rise, the drop, the next item — and it went on and on, and every so often it would stop, and go back, and take the same passage again from an earlier place, and get further, and stop, and go back again. It was the sound Kaelen had made at eleven years old, in a cold room in his father's house, learning the eight laws by rote with a tutor's stick on the table. It was the sound of something that has forgotten a piece in the middle and is trying to get past it by running at it.

They had drilled this one in 416. They had taken enough out of it to unname a city.

And it was still down there, three years later, working at the list, trying to get the whole way through.

Kaelen went down onto one knee on the archive floor with his glass hand flat on the flagstones, because he could not help it, and the sound came up his arm and filled him to the throat, and for a while he was not in the archive at all.

*— and the eighth is Decay, and Decay is the rest between, and the rest is not silence but the shape that holds the —*

*— no. The eighth. The eighth is —*

*— begin again. The first is Growth, and Growth is the word more —*

"You hear them too," said a voice.

---

It did not come out of the room.

It came out of his arm.

Kaelen had spent seven years with a piece of the Dawning Spire set around his bones, and he knew every sound it made: the high thin ring on a still night, the hard sympathy near a struck bell, the full low note after it fed. This was none of those. This was a voice, speaking, at conversational volume, from inside the glass, in the tone of a man who has come to sit by a bedside.

He got up off the floor very fast and stood with his back against the shelves and his right hand over the seam at his shoulder, as though he could hold it shut.

"Forgive me," said the voice. "There is no gentle way to do this. I have tried several and they are all worse. Sit down before you fall down; your ribs are broken and you have been on your feet since dawn."

"Who is this."

"You know who this is. You have been reading my handwriting for eleven days."

The archive was very cold.

"Casimir Vaelen," said Kaelen.

"Supreme Arbiter, yes, which is a title I have come to find embarrassing. I would much rather you used the name." A pause; the sound of an old man settling in a chair, absurdly, inside his own arm. "You are wondering how. It is not sorcery, Engineer, it is material. I am eighty percent Dawning glass; I have been since the year your grandfather was a boy. The glass in your arm came out of the same throat as the glass in my chest. Two pieces of the same Spire are not two things. They are one thing in two places, and if one of them is patient and listens for a very long time, it can hear what the other one hears."

"How long."

"Seven years," said Vaelen. "Since the morning it set around your arm. I have heard every word you have said aloud in an empty room since 412, and I want you to understand at once that I am not proud of that, and that I never once spoke, because you were in pain and it would have been an intrusion."

Kaelen thought about a charcoal-burner's hut, and seven years of saying things out loud because nobody heard.

"Then you heard—"

"Everything. Yes. Including the things you said in the second winter, which I will not repeat and have never written down." The voice was quite gentle. "And I heard you last night in the hold of that ship, saying nine names over a lantern while you wrote them on a beam. Tamsin Reed. Aldric Vance. Davin Hale. Tam, no surname, *ask*. Orla Hale. Hob Wendle. Ada Pellow. Eight men at the pumps, no names, *find them*."

"Stop."

"You are keeping a list," said Casimir Vaelen. "It is the first sensible thing anyone in the Reach has done in a hundred years, and I would like you to know that I have one as well. Mine is longer. Mine has eight names at the top of it and one of them is dead because of a calculation I made in a grey folder, and you have just read the folder, and I am the only other person alive who knows what is written on the third sheet."

Kaelen stood in the cold with his hand on the seam.

"You used me."

"I did."

"You wrote that I had a conscience of the ordinary sort and that it was useful if it wasn't engaged."

"I did," said Vaelen. "It was accurate, and it was contemptible of me to write it down, and I have thought about that sentence more often than you would believe." A breath. "Engineer, listen. You have spent seven years believing you murdered a god by being careless. You did not. You asked it a question, in a form it could not mistake, because I designed the question and I put the words in your mouth." Very quietly: "But it *answered*. That is the part nobody else on this continent understands, and you and I both heard it. It was asked whether it might stop, and it said yes, and it said yes *immediately*, and it was so eager that it did not even wait for the end of the morning."

The cores murmured on the north wall. Under the floor, something enormous went back to the beginning of a list it could not finish.

"They are in pain," said Vaelen. "All eight. They have been in pain for longer than there has been a word for it, and they were promised relief at the end of ten thousand years, and the ten thousand years ran out in the year your Spire broke, and nobody came. I hear them every hour I am awake. So, now, do you. How long do you think you will last?"

"You want me to help you kill them."

"I want you to help me let them *stop*," said Vaelen, "and I would like you to notice that you did not, in fact, ask me what I wanted. You told me. Because you already know, because you were kneeling on this floor two minutes ago with your hand flat on the stone, listening to the Anchor of Memory try to get past the eighth law, and some part of you was thinking: *somebody should help it*." A pause. "I am not going to argue with you today. It would be premature and you would resent it. I only wanted you to hear a voice that is not lying to you, once, before the Church gets to you, because the Church will."

Kaelen said, through his teeth: "Get out of my arm."

"In a moment." The voice did not change at all. "Two things, and then I will leave you alone for a while.

"First: the Cantor lived. Stage Nine was run and the source survived it, which has never happened, and the Arbiterium's file on her is two inches thick and growing, and the Church has already asked for her back. You will not be able to keep her hidden, and you should stop trying, and you should start thinking about what she is *for*, because she is the most important person alive on this continent and neither of you has understood that yet.

"Second." A pause; and for the first time, something moved under the gentleness, and it was not gentle at all. "You have four sheets of my handwriting inside your shirt, and a boy upstairs with a copy of Revision Seven with my margin on it, and if those reach the High Patriarch or the Consortium's seven chairs, they will not read them as I intend them. They will read them as a method. Every drill on this continent will have a Rite bolted to it inside a year, and there will be forty children with wire in their throats at the foot of every Spire, and it will be done badly, by people who do not hear them, for fuel.

"I would very much rather that did not happen. You may take that as a warning or as a plea; I find I do not care which, provided you take it."

Above him, through the kiln floor, the shift-bell began to ring and did not stop, and a woman started screaming somewhere at the south end of the building, and Vane's voice roared something about the tackle.

"That will be your archivist," said Casimir Vaelen. "Go. And Engineer — the eight men at the pumps. Whisper-Grove stock, consigned to Oakhaven in the ninth month of 417, which means they will be in the carriage file in the box behind your left shoulder, third shelf, grey spine.

"I told you. I keep a list too."

The glass went quiet.

Kaelen stood in the archive for three more seconds. Then he turned and pulled the grey-spined box off the third shelf and tore it open and found the carriage file, and the manifest, and eight names, and he tore that page out and put it inside his shirt with the other four, and went up the steps into the noise with his ribs grinding and his arm ringing flat, and did not let himself think about how easy it had been to do exactly what he had been told.

---
---

## CHAPTER TWENTY
### Voice at the Door

> *It cannot read. It cannot guess. It knows nothing about you that was not said out loud within its hearing, which is why the old women say: do your weeping on paper.*
>
> — *A Plain Account of the Cacophony*, Null-Watch Press

*The Cloister of the Lost Choir. Nullday morning.*

---

They left her at the Cloister because she had no voice, and she understood the arithmetic, and she hated it.

"Somebody has to hold the fallback," Vane had said, in the orchard, not looking at her. "If it goes wrong at the Malting we come back up this road at a dead run with the Office behind us, and I want a door that opens and somebody behind it who knows what to do with a wounded man."
