Which was true, and was not the reason, and everyone standing in the wet grass knew it. The reason was that a raid needs people who can shout.

So Lyra Ashgrove sat in a children's choir-room two hundred yards from the harvest-house with a slate on her knees and a nine-foot Office automaton standing against the far wall, and listened to the shift-bell ring the seventh hour, and waited.

After a while, because waiting was unbearable, she got up and started taking the cloaks off the pegs.

---

They came off their hooks stiff as board, furred green at the shoulders, and the damp had got into the wool so deeply that they weighed twice what they should. She laid each one on the end of the risers and did what her mother had done with the winter things every autumn: turned back the collar, looked for the tape.

Reach mothers sewed names into children's clothes. Everybody did. A cloak is a month's work and a cloak is handed down, and in a town where thirty children leave the same schoolhouse at the same hour, a name-tape stitched inside the collar is the difference between your coat and somebody else's.

Whisper-Grove mothers had done it too.

*M. ORRY*, in cross-stitch, red on undyed linen.

*BEN DRAY* — the choir-mistress's own, then.

*A. SOWERBY*, in a hand that had run out of room and put the Y up the side.

Lyra sat down on the bottom riser with that one in her lap for a while.

Then she went on, and wrote them on the slate as she went, in chalk, very small, so they would all fit: Orry. Dray. Sowerby. Halloran. Two Veskes, probably sisters. A Marchant. A Tipp. Nine, and then a tenth with the tape gone and only the holes where it had been.

Ten cloaks. Eleven pegs.

She looked up.

The Aegis stood against the west wall where it had put itself, head bowed under the barrel ceiling, perfectly still except for the small uneven *tick* somewhere in its chest. The red cloak hung over the fingers of its left hand, exactly where it had been since the orchard wall.

Lyra crossed the room and stood in front of it and held out her hand.

Nothing happened for long enough that she began to feel foolish. Then the lilac point of light behind the grille came round and steadied on her face, and the great hand closed.

Not fast. Not a snatch. The fingers folded in over the red wool with the slow deliberation of a thing being put away in a pocket, and the arm came in against the bronze chest and stayed there.

"Please," said Lyra, with no sound at all.

The Aegis Sentinel looked down at her and did not give it up.

She stood there a moment longer. Then she touched the back of its hand — the bronze was cold, and there was a dent in it where Kaelen's fist had landed — and went back to the risers and wrote on the slate: *RED CLOAK — NAME UNDER HIS HAND. HE WON'T.*

Outside, the fog had thickened until the orchard wall was a suggestion.

And the voice said, from the porch, "Lark."

---

She did not move.

Two years of practice held her: you do not turn, you do not answer, you walk on at exactly the pace you were walking. She sat on a children's riser with a slate on her knees and a dead girl's cloak across her lap and did not turn around.

"*I know you're in there, lark. I can hear you breathing. You always did breathe through your mouth when you were frightened; your mother used to say you sounded like a kettle.*"

Her father's voice. Warm, a little hoarse, patient. Two hundred yards away the shift-bell was still ringing at the Malting, and under it, faintly, people shouting.

"*Your friend in the hat was very kind to me,*" it said, in Tam's cracked tenor, companionably. "*Walked right out into the meadow for you, he did. Didn't make the high note.*"

Then: "*Lyra.*"

And that one was Kaelen.

Not an approximation. Not the way it had taken Davin Hale's voice in the tavern, a half-beat behind and a quarter-tone flat. It had had five days with him. It had stood in the clearing outside a charcoal-burner's hut for seven years listening to a man who talked to an empty room because nobody heard, and it had learned him the way it had learned her: completely, from the inside, with the rasp at the bottom and the Reach in the vowels and the particular flat way he said a thing he had decided not to feel anything about.

"*Lyra, I need to tell you something and I'm not going to be able to say it to your face.*"

She was on her feet.

---

The porch door stood open on a wall of fog. There was nothing in the doorway. The jitter was out past it somewhere, in the white, shifting, and the Aegis had come off the west wall and was moving — slowly, one piston at a time — to put itself between the risers and the door.

"*I calibrated the bore at the Dawning Spire,*" said Kaelen's voice.

Lyra's hands came up over her mouth.

"*The Carrow Bore. Revision three of the Sequence was mine, and the pitch tables on the gantry were in my hand, and I walked that bit down through three hundred days of a living casing a hair under the failure-pitch, which was the finest piece of work anyone in the Consortium had done in forty years, and I was proud of it. I was proud of it until about nine o'clock in the morning on the three hundredth day.*"

The fog moved.

"*I signed for the collars. Line ninety-four. Forty collars, forty spools of wire, forty needles, between the lamp oil and the scaffold clips, and I didn't read it, and I have told myself for seven years that nobody reads line ninety-four, and that is true, and it does not help.*"

She had her back against the riser. The slate was on the floor somewhere. She had not heard it fall.

"*On the ninth morning I came down the gantry to check the resonance at the risers and the girl on the top tier was bleeding into her collar. Nineteen, maybe twenty. The blood had gone down the front of her shift and dried and gone down again over the top of it, so it had been days. And she looked at me.*" A pause of exactly the length Kaelen would have left. "*And I went back up the gantry. That's the whole of it. Three seconds, and I went back up, and I did not look at the risers again for two hundred and ninety-one days.*"

Lyra made a sound.

It was not a word and it was barely a noise — a torn scrape of air out of a throat that had nothing in it — but it was a sound, and in the fog beyond the door, the thing stopped talking at once and *listened*, with an attention she could feel on her skin like a hand.

She clamped both palms over her own mouth and held them there.

After a moment, patiently, it went on.

"*At the end it asked a question. The Spire. I don't expect anyone to understand that who wasn't in the room — there were no words, it doesn't have words — but it asked, and I knew what it was asking, and I had about six seconds, and I used three of them being sure.*" Kaelen's voice did not change at all. "*Three seconds. I dropped the fork and I shouted stop the bore and the Inquisitor said hold the note and they held it, and it let go, and eleven thousand and forty people died at Sol-Luminary, and thirty-nine of them were girls I had walked past every morning for ten months.*"

"*That's what happened to the sky, lark. That's why it's grey. That's me.*"

---

She got the slate up off the floor.

She was shaking so hard the chalk broke on the first stroke, and she wrote it anyway, in letters six inches high, and carried it to the door and held it up into the fog with both hands.

**IS IT TRUE?**

The fog said nothing.

She held it higher. She shoved it out past the door-frame into the white, into the air where the voice was, and stood there with her arms straight and her heart going like a bird in a box.

"*Lark,*" it said, gently, "*I can't read.*"

The slate came down.

"*I only know what people say out loud. That's all there's ever been. Your da said your name out loud in a yard in the spring; the old fellow sang in a meadow; your engineer talks to an empty room every night of the world and has done since the year the sky went grey, and you —*" and here the voice slid, without any seam at all, into Lyra's own, the whole one, the warm one, the one she had given it on forty-one nights on a stone behind a cooperage — "*you told me about Pim Carder's hands.*"

She shut her eyes.

"*So say it out loud,*" said her own voice, kindly. "*Ask me anything you like, and I'll tell you. Is it true? Did he say it exactly like that? Did he cry when he said it, the second winter? You can ask. Nobody's listening but me.*"

She did not answer.

It was the single hardest thing she had ever done, and she had sung a woman down inside a tree, and she had held a wire in her throat for four hours, and she stood in a doorway at the Cloister of the Lost Choir with her hands over her mouth and tears coming off her jaw and did not answer.

---

So it changed tactics.

"*Hold us in the little light,*" it sang.

In Lyra's voice. The Cloister's hymn, from out in the fog, forty feet off: the tune Sable had cracked her way through an hour ago, played back perfectly, in Lyra's whole unbroken voice, by something that had heard it once.

Behind her, the Aegis Sentinel turned toward the door.

Lyra got in front of it.

She did not think about it; there was no time to think about it. Nine feet of bronze came off its mark with the floorboards flexing under it, DEFEND THE SINGERS stencilled across its chest and a singer outside in the fog, and Lyra Ashgrove put herself in the doorway with her back to the thing that wanted her and her arms out wide across the frame.

The Aegis stopped.

It stopped with its leading foot eighteen inches from her and its head bowed under the lintel, and for the second time in two hours Lyra watched a machine try to hold two instructions at once. The chest louvres lifted an inch and closed. The tick in its chest went very fast and then settled. The lilac light moved from her face to the fog and back to her face.

"*Hold us in the little light,*" sang the fog, "*until the morning comes.*"

Lyra kept her arms across the door.

It sang the hymn seven times.

Then it sang "Apples in the Rain," off-key on the fourth bar, in her father's voice. Then it sang the harvest home, all the lines, in Davin Hale's. Then it did the thing that she had not braced for, because nothing could have braced her for it: it made the sound of a seven-year-old boy whistling a falling fifth, badly, with too much air in it, the way Pim had done it on his own doorstep in the sunshine the day before the Rite.

Lyra stood in the doorway of the Cloister of the Lost Choir and bled from the mouth where something in her throat had torn again, and did not make a sound, and did not move.

---

It stopped when the others came up the road.

They came at a run and they came loud — Vane bellowing, the press on a hand-cart with two wheels, Mags one-armed on the shafts, Tobiah under one corner of it, Kaelen behind with his ribs and a woman slung over his shoulder who was screaming the same four words over and over, and behind all of them the Office's shift-bell still going and a column of smoke standing up over the Malting.

The fog went out of the orchard the way a held breath goes.

Lyra came down off the step with her arms shaking and her mouth full of blood, and Kaelen saw her and stopped dead in the road with the screaming woman on his shoulder.

"What happened," he said.

She looked at him.

Seven years. The stile when she was eight and he was nine and hopeless at the Root note. The letters that stopped. The arm in the glass. *I was supposed to hold something together, and I didn't.* Her hands on his wrist in her father's kitchen with the green warmth going up into the blue. *Don't shake.*

"Lyra. *What happened?*"

She picked up the slate. There was nothing on it; she had wiped it against her skirt at some point without knowing she had done it. She stood in the road with the chalk in her fist and found that she could not write the question either, because the moment it existed in chalk it would exist, and then one of two things would be true, and she was not ready for either of them to be true in a road in the fog with a woman screaming eight feet away.

She wrote: *IT FOLLOWED US. IT'S GONE NOW.*

And watched him read it, and watched him believe her, which was the worst part.

"Get her aboard," said Vane, behind them. "Get *everything* aboard, there'll be an Office cutter over this wood inside the hour—"

And as they went down the Cloister road through the orchard with the press jolting on the hand-cart and the Aegis bringing up the rear with a red cloak folded in its fist, the fog on their left said, in Kaelen's voice, so quietly that Lyra was almost certain nobody else heard it:

"*Ask him, lark. He'll tell you. He's been waiting seven years for somebody to make him.*"

---
---

## CHAPTER TWENTY-ONE
### Ten Heartbeats

> *An Office cutter carries listening gear forward of the mast. Running silent will not help you. Your cargo is not silent.*
>
> — Free Captains' handbill, Port Cantabile

*The Cloister road sky-dock. Nullday, mid-morning.*

---

They loaded in eleven minutes with a column of smoke standing over the Malting and every bird in the Spectral Woods going up out of the willows at once.

Pennick did his figures on the ladder, shouting them upward, which was how Vane learned that his ship was now carrying four hundred and thirty-two cores, twenty-one Oakhaven survivors, five crew, one archivist, one press at four hundredweight, one woman who could not stop screaming, and one thing that was walking up the mast-ladder at that moment and putting a hundred and sixty pounds on each rung.

"Captain. *Captain.* The lift's gone — we are eleven hundred pounds over what I told you yesterday and yesterday I was lying to make you feel better—"

"Can she fly?"

"She can *sink slowly*—"

"Then she can fly." Vane leaned over the rail. The Aegis Sentinel was two-thirds of the way up the ladder, climbing hand over hand with a red cloak still folded in its left fist, which meant it was climbing a hundred-foot ladder one-handed. "Water over the side. All of it. And the spare envelope canvas, and the tackle, and that chain."

"We'll have nothing to drink—"

"We'll have nothing to *breathe* if the Office puts us in the Bell Yard. Over the side."

Bettis Renn was screaming in the hold. They had got her down the ladder in a canvas sling with Bettany holding her head, and she had not stopped since the pressing room, and the sound came up out of the hatch and across the sky-dock and into the trees, and every single person on that deck knew exactly what sound carried in these woods and what came to it.

Hetty Bram took it in hand, because somebody had to. She put the nine children in the sail-locker with the door shut and Nell Carder in with them, and then she went down into the hold and sat on the floor by the sling and held the woman's hand and talked to her, steadily, about absolutely nothing, in the Narrow, for the next two hours.

The *Drift-Kestrel* came off the Cloister road at eleven minutes past the hour with her envelope straining and her tanks singing sour, and she went up like a wet sack.

---

The cutter came out of the south before they had six hundred feet.

Vane knew the class. Everyone on the Rust Coast knew the class: an Office courier-cutter, forty feet, lead-grey, no envelope at all but four hard little drift-tanks slung in a cage amidships, which made her brutally fast and meant she could not carry anything heavier than paper and proctors. A long gun forward on a swivel. A grapnel-thrower aft. And on the bow, where a figurehead would be on an honest ship, a brass horn the size of a wash-tub.

"What's the trumpet for?" Tobiah said.

"It's not a trumpet," said Vane.

It was a listening horn. A sounding-cone with a proctor's ear at the small end of it, the same kit the Office used to walk a street in a dead town and find the one house with somebody breathing in it. In clear air at a thousand yards, a man on that horn could hear a cough on a deck.

And the *Kestrel*'s hold had four hundred and thirty-two people murmuring in it, and a woman screaming.

*They don't need to see us at all*, Vane thought, with great clarity. *We're a bell. We've been a bell for two years and I've been the one ringing it.*

"Mags." He did not look away from the grey shape coming up out of the mist. "Down, and tell Hetty: everything they can stuff into the straw, round every crate. Blankets, coats, the canvas, the sail-locker spares. Pack it like glass for a long haul."

She read his lips and was gone down the hatch.

"Pennick, how low can you put her?"

Pennick looked at the canopy going past underneath — silver willow-tops, a hundred feet of them, with the drowned marble of Whisper-Grove showing through in patches.

"Captain, we're already low."

"Lower."

---

What followed was the worst twenty minutes of Vane's professional life, which was a field with serious competition in it.

He took the *Drift-Kestrel* down into the canopy of the Spectral Woods at forty feet with eleven hundred pounds too much aboard, and flew her up the line of the Cloister road because a road is the only straight thing in a forest, and the willow-tops came past the rail close enough that Tobiah, holding the forward shrouds, got a faceful of leaves twice.

The cutter went over the top of them at three hundred feet and lost them in the mist and came back.

It came back because of the horn. It found them the way you find a hive: by listening until the sound got louder. It came down the road behind them at twice their speed and the long gun on its bow went off, and the first shot went through the envelope's skirt and out the other side, which does nothing to a skyship, and the second took four feet of the port rail off and sent a splinter the length of a forearm through the meat of Pennick's thigh.

Then it was over them, and the grapnel came down.

---

Vane had been boarded three times in twenty-five years. Twice he had let them. Once, off Cape Dissonance in a crosswind, he had cut the hook and run, and been very lucky, and promised himself never again.

The hook came through the envelope's rigging and bit, and the cutter above began to haul, and the *Kestrel* came up by the nose and slewed sideways out of the road-line into the willows with branches raking down the starboard side like fingers on a drum.

Four proctors came down the grapnel-line.

They came the way they had come through the plaza — grey oilcloth, silver mesh, felted rods, no shouting at all — and they came down a rope at forty feet over a moving ship, which told Vane something he would have preferred not to know about the Office's training.

The first one hit the deck in a crouch, perfectly balanced.

The Aegis Sentinel picked him up off it and threw him over the side.

It was not a fight. There was no exchange. Nine feet of Office ironwork took an Office proctor by the arm and the belt and threw him out over the rail into four hundred feet of nothing without breaking stride, and the man made no sound going down, because he had been trained not to.

The second one it caught mid-drop and put through the deckhouse wall.

The third and fourth had the sense to go for the rope rather than the deck, and one of them got his rod into the back of the Aegis's knee-strut hard enough to bend something, and the machine turned round with the deliberation of a man noticing weather and caught him by the mesh of his mask.

*DEFEND THE SINGERS*, said the stencil on its chest, four feet from the proctor's face.

Vane looked away. He had the wheel. That was his excuse, and he kept it.

---

"The line!" Tobiah was at the shrouds with an axe he had no idea how to use. "Captain, the hook — if we cut it we drop—"

"Don't cut it."

"*What?*"

"*Don't cut it!*" Vane put the wheel hard over. "Mags! Starboard! Take her *starboard* — into the trees — I want that cutter's bow where we are now in four seconds—"

Mags could not hear him and did not need to. She had been watching his hands on the wheel for nine years.

The *Drift-Kestrel*, overloaded, holed, with a grapnel in her rigging and a forty-foot Office cutter tethered above her, put her nose down and went sideways into the crown of a two-hundred-year-old silver willow.

The *Kestrel* weighed a great deal. That was the whole of Vane's plan and the whole of his argument with Pennick for the previous nine days: she was fat, she was slow, she was carrying eleven hundred pounds too much, and a light fast courier-cutter tied to her by forty feet of rope was a terrier tied to a cart-horse.

The willow took the cutter's bow.

Vane heard it happen rather than saw it: a long tearing crunch of wet wood and brass, the horn going over the side, the cutter's drift-cage shrieking as it tried to climb with its nose full of tree, and then the grapnel-line parting somewhere above with a crack like a whip.

The cutter went into the canopy forty yards to starboard and kept going down through it, shedding pieces, and did not come up.

The *Kestrel*, relieved of it, rose about eleven feet.

"That's not a victory," Pennick said from the deck, grey-faced, with a hand clamped around a splinter in his leg. "Captain, I want to be clear, that was not a victory, that was two ships falling and ours is falling slower—"

---

And then the boy came.

Afterward, nobody on that deck agreed about how long it lasted. Tobiah said four seconds. Sable, who counted things for a living, said closer to two.

There was a sound like a sheet of tin being struck — not loud, but *wrong*, arriving from the wrong direction — and a smell of hot metal and summer storms, and a line of blue-white light came up the Cloister road underneath them, going the other way, at a speed nothing in the world goes.

It stopped on the foredeck.

He was perhaps seventeen. That was the thing Vane kept. He was a boy in a Synod courier's leather tabard, and he was made of light the colour of a lightning-strike afterimage, and he was not standing still — he could not stand still; every part of him was moving, vibrating, his edges smearing and reforming so that looking at him was like looking at a struck bell — and in his right hand, clamped so hard the knuckles of it had gone white-blue, was a sealed packet of paper with a black ribbon and the Arbiterium's scales in wax.

"—whereistheroad," he said. "TheOrganumroad. TheTramway. Whichway— *which way* —"

Every lamp on the deck went out. The lift-tanks shrieked up an octave.

"East," said Vane, because it was the only word he had time for.

"EastEastEast — thankyou — thank you—" The boy's head turned, and for a fraction of a second the smearing stopped and there was a face in there, a young, frightened, ordinary face, and his eyes went across the deck: the children at the sail-locker door, the woman screaming in the hold, the press under canvas, Sable. "*Don't stop,*" he said. "Whateveryoudo. Ifyoustop—"

And he was gone, off the bow, down the road, a blue-white line going east through the willows at a speed that left a crack of thunder behind it and a long scorched stripe along four planks of Vane's foredeck in the shape of two running feet.

The lamps came back. The tanks came down an octave and stayed sour.

"What," said Pennick, carefully, "was that."

"That was a courier," said Sable. She had come up out of the hatch at the noise and was standing at the rail looking east with the Ledger hanging open beside her. "Arbiterium seal, black ribbon. That is a standing writ, which means it must be delivered into a named hand and read aloud and witnessed." She paused. "He has been running since the Valley of Velocity went, which was five years ago, and he has not delivered it."

"How do you know that?"

"Because the Office has a file on him," said Sable, "and the file is one line long, and it says: *courier Quill, J., in transit.*"

---

They put her down in the deep woods nine miles north-east, because they had no choice.

The second shot from the cutter's long gun had not missed. It had gone in through the skirt and clipped the number-three lift-tank, and drift-glass does not explode; it *weeps*, very slowly, a cobalt vapour that takes the lift with it, and by the fourth mile the *Kestrel* was flying nose-high and crabbing and Pennick had stopped arguing, which frightened Vane more than the gun had.

They came down in a clearing of silver willows with the keel in the moss and the envelope sagging over them like a tent, four hundred yards from a long black building that had once had a roof.

Sable looked at it for a while in the last of the light.

"The Mausoleum of Chords," she said. "The old Veil-house. Eleven hundred years of records." A pause. "I learned to rule a page in there."

"Is it dangerous?"

"Everything here is dangerous, Captain. That one is only sad."

They got Pennick's leg bound and Bettis dosed and the children fed, and Mags went up the envelope with a needle in her teeth and one working arm, and Vane walked his ruined deck in the dark: four feet of rail gone, two scorched footprints, a hole in the deckhouse the size and shape of a man, a cracked knee-strut on a nine-foot automaton that was standing at the bow facing the way they had come, and six feet of cut rope still hanging from the winch drum, which nobody had coiled in five days.

He stood at the stern for a long time looking back toward the Cloister road.

"Twenty-one," he said, to the dark. He had got into the habit of saying it out loud, lately. "And four hundred and thirty-two. And one screaming."

In the hold beneath him, patiently, four hundred and thirty-two people went on talking in their sleep.

---
---

## CHAPTER TWENTY-TWO
### What the Ledger Knows

> *Rule the page before you write on it. A margin is not decoration. A margin is the place where you are not permitted to put your own opinion, and leaving it empty is the discipline of the whole trade.*
>
> — P. Quist, to a nineteen-year-old apprentice, c. 405 SR

*The Mausoleum of Chords, deep Spectral Woods. Nullday night.*

---

There were two things in the inventory now, and Sable could not stop counting them.

She did it at the foot of the willow where she had put her bedroll, in the dark, with the ship's hull creaking forty feet away and no fire because a fire in these woods is a lamp hung out for the Cacophony.

"One. The scriptorium at the Veil-house. Cold stone, tallow, wet wool, vinegar, because we cleaned the desks on the last day of every month. Fourteen desks. Mine by the second window."

Four hundred yards away, through the trees, the Veil-house itself stood with no roof on it and eleven hundred years of records rotting in its cellars, and she had not gone to look, and was not going to.

"Two. My name is—"

Still there.

And then the place where the third thing had been, which was not an absence. That was what nobody understood who had not had it done to them, and she had written seventy-four thousand entries about it without understanding it herself: it does not feel like a gap. It feels like a shelf. You run your hand along it and it is smooth and complete and there is nothing missing, and the only reason you know is that you wrote down, three years ago, that there were three.

Warm, she thought. It had been warm, and it had had hands in it, and she had been able to describe it at breakfast this morning.

She sat in the dark with the Bound Ledger closed on her knees and worked out what that had cost, in the ledger-keeping way, which was the only way she had.

One fragment for one name. Two fragments remaining. Four hundred and thirty-two people in a hold, one thousand three hundred and forty-two in total, seventy-four thousand six hundred and eleven in the book.

And the thing she had learned in the pressing room, which she had not yet said out loud to anybody and which she was going to have to:

That it does not work. Not the way they wanted it to. That the Office had never put one back because the Office had never needed to find out, and now she knew what the finding out had cost a woman who was lying sedated in a canvas sling in that hold saying *put it back, put it back, put it back*, and would be saying it, in all likelihood, for the rest of her life.

Sable had written the pamphlet on the queue.

She was going to have to write this one too.

---

She found Kaelen Vance on the far side of the clearing, sitting against a tree with his coat off and his shirt open, doing something to his own chest with a piece of string.

He had pulled the collar back to the shoulder. In the dark the glass was a pale shape, like the moon under water, and the frost of the tideline had crossed the collarbone and come down onto the chest in a ragged crescent, and he was measuring it: string from the point of the shoulder, thumbnail at the mark, string held up against a knot he had tied in it some days before.

He stopped when he saw her and pulled his shirt closed.

"Don't," said Sable. "I have seen worse this morning."

She sat down four feet away with her back against the next tree, which was as close as she got to anyone, and put the papers on the moss between them.

She had taken them off the archive table while he was on his knees on the floor with his hand flat on the flagstones, saying nothing, with his eyes shut, somewhere she could not follow. She had been loading a hand-cart with one arm and a cracked face and she had seen a box open on a table with a lamp beside it, and she had done what she always did, which was read.

Three hundred daily calibration returns, initialled *K.V.*

A procurement order, two hundred lines, line ninety-four, the same initials.

The casualty return of the Great Foundry Rupture: eleven thousand and forty, with thirty-nine listed as SKY CHOIR (CONSECRATED).

And the Inquisitorial report, filed by one I. Crane, Flambeau of the Synod, in the ninth month of 412, which recorded that the unison had been broken on the three hundredth morning by *a dissonance from the gantry*.

"I have read them," she said. "I have read them twice. Do you want to tell me anything?"

Kaelen looked at the papers on the moss.

"No," he said.

"You walked the bore down. Three hundred days, a hair under the failure-pitch, which they tell me was the finest piece of work in forty years."

"Yes."

"And you signed for the collars."

"Line ninety-four."

"And the Church's own report says that at the end there was a dissonance from the gantry." She let that sit. "There was one man on that gantry, Engineer. The report is the only document in that box that says anybody tried to stop it, and it was written by somebody who meant it as the charge against you."

"I know what's in the report," said Kaelen.

"Then you know that I have in front of me the whole of the evidence against you and precisely one line in your favour, and that the line in your favour is being used as the accusation." Sable tilted her head. "Which is tidy. Almost too tidy. I spent eleven years in that Office and I have never once seen a file that complete and that one-sided that did not have something taken out of it."

He said nothing at all.

She watched him not say it. She was extremely good at watching people not say things; it had been, for eleven years, the largest part of her job.

"You are carrying something inside your shirt," she said, "and it is not the string."

"Sable."

"I will not ask again. I am recording that I asked once." And she opened the Ledger, and wrote, in the dark, by feel: *Asked. Declined.*

---

She took the papers to Lyra an hour later.

The Cantor was sitting on the keel-block the crew had chocked under the bow, with a blanket round her shoulders and the slate on her knees, watching the Aegis Sentinel.

The machine had not moved since they landed. It stood at the edge of the clearing facing back along the line of their flight with the red cloak folded in its fist, and every so often the tick in its chest went fast and then settled.

Sable sat down on the moss in front of the woman she had come to ruin, and laid the papers on her lap, and said:

"These are from the Catalogue archive at the Malting. They are the Consortium's file on the Rite of Pure Unison at the Dawning Spire, and they are in his hand, and I took them off the table in front of him while he was not looking, and I am giving them to you."

Lyra did not pick them up.

She looked at Sable for a long moment with an expression that Sable, who had spent three years reading faces that had nothing behind them, could not parse at all. Then she wiped the slate with her sleeve and wrote, and turned it round.

*I ALREADY KNOW.*

Sable sat back.

"How?"

*IT TOLD ME. THIS MORNING. AT THE CLOISTER.*

*IN HIS VOICE.*

---

It took a while, after that, because the Cantor could only write.

Sable learned to wait, which she had never been good at. She watched the chalk go across the slate, and the sleeve wipe it, and the chalk go again, and she sat on the moss in the dark and took the whole of it: the fog, the porch, the slate held out into the white, *lark, I can't read*, the hymn sung back seven times in her own whole voice, the boy's whistle with too much air in it.

And at the end, after a long pause in which she wrote nothing at all, this:

*I STOOD IN THE DOOR AND LET IT TELL ME.*

*I COULD HAVE WALKED AWAY. IT CAN'T COME IN WHERE PEOPLE ARE. IT WANTS YOU ALONE.*

*I STAYED AND I LISTENED TO THE WHOLE THING.*

Sable read it twice.

"Why?"

The Cantor's hand shook slightly on the chalk.

*BECAUSE IT WAS THE ONLY WAY I WAS EVER GOING TO HEAR IT.*

---

They sat with that for some minutes. In the hold, muffled, four hundred and thirty-two people went on murmuring, and Bettis Renn had stopped screaming an hour ago and started something quieter and worse.

Then Lyra picked up the papers.

She read them by the light of a shuttered lamp held an inch above the page, with her hair falling forward, slowly, the way a woman reads a thing she intends to be able to recite afterward. The returns. The order. Line ninety-four, with the initials against it, between the lamp oil and the scaffold clips.

She stopped at the casualty return and stayed there a long time.

Then the Inquisitor's report.

And then she wrote, and her hand was quite steady now:

*DOES ANYTHING IN HERE SAY HE SHOUTED?*

"Only that," said Sable. "*A dissonance from the gantry.* Crane wrote it as the cause of the catastrophe. There is no other record of it anywhere in that box, and there will not be one anywhere else, because the only other people on that gantry are dead."

*SO THE ONLY PROOF HE TRIED*

The sleeve. The chalk.

*IS THE THING THEY'RE BLAMING HIM FOR.*

"Yes."

Lyra put the papers down on the moss and put both hands over her face and sat like that.

Sable did not touch her. She did not know how, and she suspected it would have been unwelcome, and she had learned a great deal that day about doing things to people for their own good.

"I want to tell you why I brought them," she said instead, to the dark above the Cantor's head. "It is not kindness and I would rather you did not mistake it for kindness.

"I stood in a doorway at a place four miles from here for eleven days with an intake sheet in my hand and watched forty-one children go into a room, and I told myself that what I was doing was *witnessing*. That the recording was the moral act. That so long as the book was true, I was not one of them." She heard her own voice do something unfamiliar and went on through it. "I have had three years in a drowned plaza to find out what that was worth, and the answer is that it was worth nothing whatsoever, and that a woman who watches and writes it down is simply a woman who watches.

"So I am not going to do it again. I know a thing, and the person it belongs to is you, and I am not going to sit on it and feel complicated about it. That is all this is."

The Cantor lowered her hands.

She wrote one word, and showed it, and it was not the one Sable expected.

*THANK YOU.*

---

Afterward, when Lyra had gone across the clearing toward the tree where Kaelen Vance was sitting with his shirt closed and his string in his fist — walking slowly, with the papers held down at her side, in no hurry at all, the way a woman walks toward a thing she has decided to do — Sable went and stood in front of the Aegis Sentinel.

It was a clear night. Through the willow-tops the stars were out, and the Spire of Decay's end of the sky had that faint wrongness to it that everybody had stopped mentioning years ago.

"I know what you are," she said.

Tick. Tick-tick. Steady.

"You are entry forty-one thousand and five. You were taken on the eleventh day of the Ashfall harvest in the year 416, in the hour after noon, out of the Cloister of the Lost Choir, with forty other children of a charitable choir under Mistress Dray, because the Office had a requisition for small clear voices and your choir had them under one roof." She kept her hands at her sides. "You were third in the line, or near it. The girl behind you would not let go of your hand. The proctors took the two of you into the room together rather than make a scene in front of forty children, and you were pressed in the same hour, and I wrote both of your entries.

"Hers is legible. She is in the third crate on that ship and her name is Annet Sowerby and she is six years old and she is frightened of the dark.

"Yours is not."

She made herself say the rest of it standing up straight.

"By the eleventh day my hand had begun to shake. I have looked at your entry perhaps four hundred times in three years. I can read the number. I can read *alto*. I can read the first letter of the surname and it is either a W or an M and I have never been able to decide, and the given name is one syllable and might be anything, and there is no one left in the city who could tell me, because I catalogued all of them."

The lilac point behind the grille did not move.

"I am telling you this," said Sable, "because you are owed it, and because I am not going to stand here and let you be a machine for my convenience." She opened the Ledger. It came round and hung at her elbow, and the pages turned themselves to a place she had found four hundred times. "And because I am going to find it. There is a duplicate schedule in the Organum, in the Hall of Carriage, third floor, and the Registrar-General told me himself this morning that no data has been lost. So there is a copy of your name in a building nine hundred miles east of here, and I know the room, and I know the shelf.

"That is a long way and I am forty-one years old with a broken arm and two things left in my head. But I have been reading twenty names a night to people who cannot hear them for three years, and I would like, at least once before the end, to say one to somebody who can."

She wrote, standing, in the dark, under the entry that was not legible:

*I am going to find it.*

The Aegis Sentinel's head came down.

It was not a nod — it had no vocabulary for a nod, and it would be another eleven months before it learned one — but it came down, slowly, until the great bronze grille was level with the face of the small grey woman standing in the moss in front of it.

And then it opened its left hand and gave her the red cloak.

---
---

## PART FOUR: CODA

---

## CHAPTER TWENTY-THREE
### Coda

> *For a death with no body, sing the full office anyway. The song is not for the dead. It is for the hole the dead left, and a hole will take a song the way dry ground takes rain.*
>
> — T. Ashgrove, Cantor's notebook, 404 SR

*The clearing by the Mausoleum of Chords. Nullday night.*

---

He watched her come across the clearing with the papers in her hand and he did not get up.

"You've read them," said Kaelen.

She sat down on the moss in front of him — not beside him; in front, where he would have to look at her — and set the slate on her knees, and wrote, and turned it round.

*TELL ME.*

"Lyra—"

*TELL ME. OUT LOUD. ALL OF IT.*

So he did.

It took the better part of an hour, and she never once wrote anything else, and he was grateful for that in a way he could not have explained, because the silence meant he had to keep going.

He told her about the Conservatory, and the rented room over the chandler's, and a young man who had been the cleverest person in the Organum for about four years and had known it. He told her what the Vance Sequence was, and why it was beautiful, and that it was beautiful. He told her about the Foundry: the risers, the censer-chain, the thirty-nine. The girl on the top tier, and her collar, and the ninth morning. He told her that he had gone back up the gantry.

He told her about line ninety-four.

He told her about the question — about the chord drawing in and in until it was the size of a hand on his shoulder, and what it had meant, and the three seconds he had spent being sure, and what he had shouted, and who had answered him.

And then, because she sat there with her ruined throat and her father's papers in her lap and did not look away, he told her the rest of it, which nobody alive had heard.

That he had read the grey folder in the archive at the Malting.

That it was dated eight months before the Fracture. That the object of Stage Nine had never been extraction; that the whole Rite had been built to put a question to a god in a form it could not mistake; that the Arbiterium had expected the Light to let go, and had regarded that as the proof of a method; and that there was a line at the bottom of the third sheet, in a small beautiful hand, saying that Engineer V. was not to be informed, because his ear was more accurate when his conscience was not engaged.

He took the four sheets out of his shirt and put them on the moss between them.

"I was used," said Kaelen. "That's what those say. I've had them since yesterday morning and I haven't shown them to anybody, and I want you to understand why, because it isn't modesty." He looked at his own hands. "When I read them, the first thing I felt was relief. It came up in me like water. *It wasn't me. It was never me. A better man in my place would have done exactly the same.*" He swallowed. "And Coralie is still dead. Fifteen years old, second riser, left shoulder, and she used to make jokes under her hem with her hands because the wire wouldn't let her talk. And she gets nothing out of my relief. Nobody gets anything out of it except me. So I decided I wasn't entitled to it, and I put it inside my shirt, and I was going to burn it."

Lyra picked up the four sheets.

She read them by the shuttered lamp, slowly, the way she read everything. When she got to the line about his conscience, her hand closed on the edge of the paper hard enough to crease it.

Then she put them down.

And she wrote, and her hand was shaking so badly that the letters came out like a child's:

*THERE IS SOMETHING ELSE. SAY IT.*

Kaelen looked at her.

"In the archive," he said, "something spoke to me out of the glass."

---

He told her about Vaelen, too. All of it: two pieces of one Spire are one thing in two places; seven years of listening; a voice that had heard every word Kaelen Vance had said aloud in an empty room since the year the sky went grey.

"He's heard me talk to the hut," he said. "Every night. The second winter, when I — he's heard the whole of it. And he never spoke, because he said it would have been an intrusion." He laughed, once, with no humour in it at all. "That's the man. That's exactly the man. He will unmake the world and he will be *polite* about it."

*WHAT DOES HE WANT?*

"He wants me to help him let them stop." Kaelen looked up at the willow-tops. "And here is the thing I have to say to you, Lyra, because if I don't say it tonight I never will. I stood in that archive with the Anchor of Memory underneath me trying to get to the end of a list it can't finish, and a man was telling me that eight of them have been in pain since before there were people, and that the term of the thing ran out in the year the Light broke, and that nobody came.

"And I didn't have an argument."

She went very still.

"I don't now," he said. "I've been looking for one for two days. The Root under Oakhaven is still singing with a hole in it, right now, badly, and it will go on singing badly for as long as it can hold out, because that's what it agreed to do ten thousand years ago when somebody promised it that we'd learn to take over." He made himself say it. "I think they are owed. I think he's right about what they're owed, and I think he is entirely wrong about how it ends, and the distance between those two things is the only ground I have to stand on, and it is not very wide. So when you ask me what I am — I am a man who built the drill, and the only person on this continent who understands what the man at the other end of it wants, and some nights that feels less like opposition than like being the same thing twice."

The lamp hissed.

"That's everything," said Kaelen Vance. "That's the whole account. There isn't any more."

---

Lyra Ashgrove opened her mouth to reach for the chalk.

And her voice came back.

It came back the way a stream comes back after a dry month: not with the first push, and then suddenly, wrongly, in the wrong place. What came out of her was a cracked, ruined, barely-pitched thing, two tones lower than it had been a fortnight ago and furred like a split reed, and it was the first sound she had made in six days.

"I breathed on your arm," she said.

Kaelen's head came up.

"In my kitchen. On Rootday." Every word cost her something visible. "I had nothing left — I'd spent it all on a boy's hands — and I put my mouth on the glass and breathed the Root note into it because it was the only thing I had, and you sat in my father's house and let me, and you had *this* in your head the whole time."

"Yes."

"I sang in a procession with a wire in my throat for you."

"Yes."

"I was going to go down that shaft and hold a bore's pitch and I didn't know *what I was holding it for*—"

"You did," he said. "I told you what Stage Nine was. I told you everything I knew about the Rite. What I didn't tell you was what I was."

"That's the same thing!" It tore; she went on through the tearing. "That *is* the same thing, Kaelen. I chose you. I chose you on Rootday at my own table and I chose you in the Hollow and I chose you on the line over Oakhaven, and every single time I was choosing a man who was missing a piece, and you knew which piece, and you let me do it." She was crying now, which she had not done in front of anyone since her father, and she did not stop talking. "I would have forgiven you. Do you understand that? On Rootday I would have forgiven you in about four minutes, because I'm a Cantor and that is what we are *for*. I can't now. Not because of the Spire. Because you let me pick you up without telling me how heavy you were."

Kaelen sat with his back against the tree.

"I know," he said.

"Don't you *dare* be calm about it—"

"I'm not calm. I've got no idea what my face is doing." He said it quite steadily. "You're right. All of it's right. I'm not going to argue a word of it, and I'm not going to apologise either, because an apology is a thing you give somebody so they'll stop, and I don't want you to stop. You're the only person who's said any of this to me in seven years and most of what I know about what I am I learned in the last four minutes."

Lyra put her head down on her knees.

They stayed like that for a long time, two feet apart, in a clearing in the Spectral Woods with an eleven-hundred-year-old Veil-house rotting in the trees behind them.

"I hate that it told me first," she said at last, into her knees. "That's the part I can't — it *should have been you*, and instead it was a thing in a fog wearing your voice, and it was kind about it. It was kind, Kaelen. It let me sit down."

She lifted her head.

"And I'm going to kill it tonight," she said.

---

Here is what the Wearer was, and what it was not.

It was not clever. It had no plan beyond its appetite, and its appetite had one shape: a voice, taken whole, and the sounds around that voice, taken with it. It could not read. It could not guess. Everything it knew, it knew because somebody had said it out loud where it could hear.

But it learned, and it kept what it learned forever, and it had been learning Lyra Ashgrove for two years.

It knew her note. It knew her torn voice and her whole one. It knew the falling fifth, and Pim's whistle, and eleven hymns, and the particular hitch her breath made when she was about to cry. It had followed her note four miles out of a drowning town, and then nine more to a drowned city, and then nine again through the deep woods to a clearing by the Mausoleum of Chords, because once it has your note it can find you anywhere, and because she had never once, in forty-one nights, told it to stop coming.

It knew everything about her except one thing, which is the thing nobody can learn by listening: what she would do.

---

She set it up like a procedure, because that was how you did a thing you could not afford to feel.

"It won't come where people are," she told them, in the bow's shadow, in that ruined voice, with Vane and Sable and Kaelen and Tobiah around her and Mags watching her mouth. "It never has. In two years it has never crossed the Hedge, or come through the culvert, or set foot in the Hollow, and at the Cloister it stood in the fog and would not come into the room. It wants me *alone*. That's the whole of its nature. So I am going to be alone, out there, at the edge of the trees, and all of you are going to be back here where it can see that you are."

"And when it comes?" said Vane.

"It won't come straight away. It'll circle, and it'll use voices, and it will do everything it can to make me walk further out." She looked at Kaelen. "You get one strike. You get it when it's inside the ring of lamps and not before, because if it sees you it will simply go away and come back in a week when I'm asleep."

"Lyra," said Sable, "may I say the obvious thing."

"No."

"You are the bait, with no voice to speak of, at night, in a wood, against the thing that has killed four people we know of—"

"I'm not bait," said Lyra. "Bait doesn't sing."

---

She walked out past the last lamp at the second hour and stood in the moss between two silver willows with her hands at her sides.

And she sang her father's funeral.

She had never sung it. That was the thing she had carried for two years, under everything, under the Hedge and the stone and the forty-one nights: Tobin Ashgrove had gone into the dark on a spring morning in the Pellows' yard and there had been nothing left to bury but a lyre, and a Cantor does not sing the office over a stile. She had sung four hundred people down in her life. Every one of them had got the full office — the six movements, the naming, the long low close that goes under the floor — and the only one she had never given it to was her own.

It was the oldest thing she knew. It went back past the Synod and past the Reckoning and probably past the Compact itself, and it was in five parts and one coda, and it was not a beautiful song. It was a working song, the way a plough is a working tool. You sang it to make the hole the right shape.

She sang it in a voice like a torn sheet, in the dark, badly, at full volume.

*Tobin Ashgrove of the orchard rows. Son of Mara. Cantor of Oakhaven.*

The wood went quiet.

*He sang the births. He sang the burials. He hummed off-key on the fourth bar and he never once learned better.*

She could hear her own voice going out between the trunks and not coming back, the way sound goes in deep woods, and she kept singing, and her throat opened something it should not have opened and she tasted iron and kept going.

*He went out into the yard so the hounds would follow him and not the house. He was fifty-six years old. He was afraid of ladders and nothing else.*

And somewhere out in the dark, perfectly in tune, her father's voice began to sing the second part.

---

It knew the office. Of course it knew the office; it had eaten a Cantor.

It came in on the harmony line the way Tobin Ashgrove had come in on it four hundred times at four hundred gravesides, a fifth below, warm and hoarse and a little late on the entries the way he always was, and for eight bars Lyra Ashgrove and the thing that had eaten her father sang his funeral together in the dark.

That was the worst eight bars of her life, and she would have them for the rest of it, and some part of her — the part she would not forgive, ever, not until she was very old — was glad of them.

Then she saw it.

It came between the trunks at thirty feet: her father's height, her father's stoop, the long-backed forward lean of forty years over reed-rows, with the willow-dark showing straight through it and its edges crawling like heat off a road. It had no face. It had the place where a face was not, rippling, and the voice came out of that.

*"Lark,"* it said, between the phrases. *"Oh, lark. You never sang it for me."*

"I know," said Lyra, and kept singing.

*"I waited."*

Third part. The naming. She lifted her chin and sang her father's name into the dark four times, which is what the naming is, and the thing came closer with every one, because it could not help it, because that was what it was: a mouth that goes toward a voice.

Twenty feet. Fifteen.

*"Come out of the lamps, lark,"* it said, in her own whole voice, gently. *"I can't come in there. Come out to me and I'll sing the rest with you. You can't do the close on your own, you know you can't, it's a two-voice part—"*

It was true. That was the obscenity of it. The close of the office is two voices, and she had not had a second voice since the Pellow farm, and the thing in the trees knew it because it had been the second voice.

Ten feet.

"I know it's a two-voice part," said Lyra Ashgrove.

And she sang the coda alone.

---

It came apart four feet in front of her.

Kaelen came out of the dark behind it at a dead run with his left arm bare and blazing and his ribs grinding and he hit it with everything he had left, and nine feet of bronze came out of the dark one second behind him and hit it from the other side, because DEFEND THE SINGERS is four words long and has no clause in it for what the thing in front of you is wearing.

The Wearer burst.

It did not die like the hounds in the tavern, in a spray of bright falling notes. It had eaten a Cantor and four others and two years of a woman's nights, and when the glass went through it, all of that came out at once, in the space of about three seconds, in a wall of sound that knocked Tobiah Marsh off his feet at forty yards.

Davin Hale, singing the fourth line of the harvest home.

Old Tam, going up for the high note he never once reached.

Ada Pellow, saying *oh, it's easy, come on, it's easy from this side* in the voice of a woman who has just discovered otherwise.

A boy whistling a falling fifth with too much air in it.

Lyra's own voice, whole and warm and twenty-nine years old, saying *I've shown him how to hold his hands out stiff, Da, so they won't touch anything.*

And under all of it, rising up through it as the rest came apart, her father.

Not the voice it had worn. The one underneath. Tobin Ashgrove at the end, in a farmyard in the spring, singing the calling-song at the top of his lungs and walking away from a house with a sick child in it, and the song getting ragged as the thing came, and then not ragged — *louder* — because he had worked out, in the last four seconds of his life, that the louder he was the further away they would be from the door.

He was still singing when it took him.

That is what came out of the Wearer in the clearing by the Mausoleum of Chords, and Lyra Ashgrove stood four feet away with her hands at her sides and let every bit of it go through her, and did not cover her ears.

The last of it was one word, in her father's voice, from about the height of her shoulder, very close, almost surprised:

*"Lark—"*

And then the clearing was quiet, and there was nothing in the moss in front of her at all.

---

She sat down.

Not gracefully; her legs stopped and she went down on them in the wet moss. Kaelen got to her and did not touch her and knelt a yard away with his arm ringing low and full — it had fed; she heard it feed; it was the fullest she had ever heard it — and said her name twice.

"That's it," said Lyra.

"Lyra—"

"That's it. That's the end of him." She was looking at the empty place in the moss. "That was the last of his voice in the world, and it was in *that*, and I've just put it out." Her face did something she had no control over. "I've been going out to the Hedge for forty-one nights to listen to a monster because it was the only place my father still existed, and I knew exactly what it was the whole time, and tonight I killed it on purpose, and now there's nowhere."

Kaelen said nothing at all, which was, for once, precisely right.

"Say something," she said.

"No."

"*Kaelen.*"

"You don't want something said," he said. "You want somebody to sit here. I can do that one."

So he sat there, a yard away in the wet moss, with a nine-foot automaton standing over the pair of them holding a child's red cloak, and the lamps burning down, and the ship creaking in the dark, and four hundred and thirty-two people murmuring in her hold.

And at some point before dawn Lyra Ashgrove put her hand out sideways, without looking, and found his good one, and did not let go of it, and did not forgive him either.

Both of those things were true at the same time, and would be for a long while yet.

---
---

## CHAPTER TWENTY-FOUR
### Heartglass

> *Writ of Attainder and Grace. Be it known that the persons herein named are declared Dissonant, their goods forfeit, their testimony void, their names struck. Provided always that grace is offered to each, once, and that a man who refuses grace has chosen what follows.*
>
> — Standing form, the Arbiterium

*The clearing by the Mausoleum of Chords. Rootday, dawn.*

---

Four fingers and a half.

He measured it sitting on the keel-block in the grey light with the string and the knots, and then he measured it again because the first answer was wrong, and the second answer was the same.

At midsummer it had been one finger from the point of the shoulder. On Rootday, in his father's hall, two and a half. On Massday, after he drank a Legionnaire's bell in a depot at the quarry, three. Four at the Malting, after a Steam Golem and a boiler's worth of working noise. And now four and a half, because last night he had put his fist through a thing that had eaten five people and two years of sound, and the glass had taken all of it, and he had felt it go in like a man drinking in a doorway.

Four and a half fingers from the point of the shoulder puts the tideline past the collarbone, across the top of the chest, and three inches from the notch of the throat.

The heart is deeper than that and further over. He had worked out the distance in the second winter with a piece of chalk on the hut wall, and had then not looked at the sum for seven years, which was its own kind of arithmetic.

He wrote the number on the deck-beam under the nine names, in charcoal, and sat looking at the column.

*1 · 2½ · 3 · 4 · 4½*

Six weeks, he thought. Perhaps eight, if nothing came at them. Nothing had failed to come at them in nineteen days.

"Engineer."

Sable was at the hatch with the Ledger at her elbow and her arm out of its sling, which Bettany had forbidden.

"There is a man in the clearing," she said, "in white, under a parley flag, and he says he has a writ for Kaelen Vance, and he is going to read it aloud whether or not anyone comes, because the form requires it to be read aloud and witnessed."

---

He was about twenty-five, and he had walked nine miles through the Spectral Woods at night alone to do this, which told you something about the Arbiterium's training or about what they had on him.

He stood in the middle of the clearing in a white herald's tabard with the scales on the breast, holding a staff with a white rag knotted to it, with a nine-foot Office automaton eleven feet away and a skyship crew around him in a loose and extremely unfriendly ring, and he read from a sealed sheet with a black ribbon in a clear carrying voice, and his hands shook the entire time and he did not let it get into his reading, and Kaelen found that he respected him enormously.

"*By the Arbiterium of the Iron Synod, under the hand of the Supreme Arbiter, at the Organum, the fourth day of Leaffall, 419.*

"*Be it known: the persons styled Kaelen Vance, late Chief Resonant Engineer, badge four-oh-eight-A; Lyra Ashgrove, Cantor, of Oakhaven; the carrier styled Vane, of registry OK-114; and the scribe styled Sable, formerly of the Office of the Catalogue — are declared Dissonant. Their goods are forfeit. Their testimony is void in any court of the Synod. The Church of the Pure Note has entered a claim upon the Cantor, which claim the Arbiterium has stayed, for now.*"

Hetty Bram said something short under her breath. The herald did not look up.

"*Be it further known that the Justiciar Aurelian Vaelen, Dread Arbiter, is upon the Iron Tramway with a column of the Carillon Legions and the authority of the Scales, and will be at the Mausoleum of Chords within nine days.*

"*And be it known that grace is offered, once.*"

He turned the sheet.

"*To Kaelen Vance: surrender of his person at the Organum, upon these terms. The glass shall be drawn. The Supreme Arbiter attests, upon his own body, that it can be drawn, and that he has carried eighty years of it and will show the engineer how.*"

Pennick, on his crutch, made a small noise.

"*To the Cantor Lyra Ashgrove: amnesty, full and recorded, and the Church's claim extinguished, and transit to any province she names.*

"*To the persons of Oakhaven aboard the said vessel: relief, transit, and registration waived.*"

"*Waived*," said Sable, very quietly. "He knows what we saw at the Malting."

"*And to the consignment of engram cores presently aboard the said vessel: proper disposition by the Office of the Catalogue, under the Registrar-General's hand.*"

Nobody said anything for a moment.

"There it is," said Vane.

---

"I'm taking it," said Kaelen.

They had gone twenty yards into the trees to have it, which fooled nobody, because Mags could read lips at that distance and the rest of them could hear perfectly well.

"Of course you are," said Lyra.

"Listen to me—"

"No, it's wonderful. It's the best one yet." Her voice was still a ruin; it made everything she said sound as though it had been dragged over gravel, which suited her this morning. "Nineteen days ago you were going to go down a shaft on your own and break a bore with your hands. Then you were going to sign a paper so the Hollow wouldn't burn. Now you're going to walk into the Organum and lie down on a table for the man who used you, and every single time, Kaelen, every single time, the plan is *only Kaelen goes into the dark*."

"It's twenty-one people off this ship and out of a war."

"It's four hundred and thirty-two people into a furnace!" She pointed back through the trees. "*Proper disposition.* You heard it. Say what it means."

He said it, because she was right and because she had earned it. "It means they go back into Legionnaires."

"And Annet Sowerby is in the third crate," said Lyra.

---

The others came up, because of course they did.

"I'd like to put one thing in," said Vane. He had his hands in his pockets. "I've been thinking about it since the herald started, which is longer than I usually think about anything. Nine days, he said. That's a column on the Tramway with a Justiciar at the head of it, and they'll be at this clearing on Driftday, and the *Kestrel* can't fly on three tanks."

"I know."

"Which means the choice isn't *Kaelen goes in or nobody goes in*. It's *Kaelen goes in and buys us nine days we'd have had anyway*, because that writ already told us where they are and how fast." He shrugged. "It's a bad trade, that's all I'm saying. I've made a career of bad trades and that one's bad."

"He also told us something else," said Sable.

She had the Ledger open and she was looking at it the way she looked at a page that had something wrong with the ruling.

"Think about the terms. Amnesty for the Cantor, transit for twenty-one farmers, registration waived — the Registrar-General would never waive registration; it is the only thing he has — and the cores returned, which the Catalogue would demand anyway. All of that is small." She looked up. "And the thing he is buying with it is one man with a Dawning-glass arm who can hear the Choristers."

"Sable—"

"He does not want you punished, Engineer. He wants you *at the Organum*." Her voice did not rise. "He has eight Anchors to ask a question of, and a Planetary Engine thirty years in the building, and exactly two people alive who have heard a god answer. He is one of them. He is a hundred and forty years old and eighty percent glass and he will not last another twenty years, and he has just offered you a cure, a desk, and an education.

"That is not a sentence. That is a *succession*."

The wood was very quiet.

"He told me the Cantor was the most important person alive," said Kaelen slowly.

"Then he will come for her next," said Sable, "and he will be polite about that too."

---

In the end it was Lyra who answered the herald, because Kaelen found that he could not.

She walked out into the middle of the clearing in a borrowed coat with her hair unbrushed and the mark of a wire under her jaw, and stood in front of a young man in white with a staff, and said, in that torn and carrying voice:

"Write this down, and get it exactly right, because he'll ask you.

"The Cantor of Oakhaven thanks the Supreme Arbiter for the amnesty and declines it.

"The persons of Oakhaven aboard this ship are not goods and are not in his gift, and they will go where they choose, and if he wants to do something useful for them he can put the Reach back.

"The consignment is not a consignment. It is four hundred and thirty-two people out of Whisper-Grove, and we know all of their numbers and some of their names, and we are taking them home.

"And Engineer Vance declines grace."

The herald's pen had stopped.

"He will ask me why," he said.

Lyra thought about that for a moment.

"Tell him the Cantor says: *we heard the question too.*"

---

"You didn't ask me," said Kaelen afterward, by the bow.

"No."

"Lyra—"

"I'm not doing this because I've forgiven you," said Lyra Ashgrove. "I want to be extremely clear, because I know exactly what you'll make of it if I leave a gap. I have not forgiven you. I'm not sure I'm going to. Last night I killed the last place my father's voice existed, and the day before that a thing in a fog told me what you did, kindly, and let me sit down, and both of those are in me now and they are not coming out."

She turned round and faced him.

"But I have watched you try to pay for seven years," she said, "and the arithmetic is wrong, and I'm the only person here who can say so, because everybody else on this ship wants something from you. You can't pay. There isn't enough of you. Eleven thousand and forty people and a sky — there is no amount of Kaelen Vance that buys that back, and you have been trying to settle it one limb at a time since the year it happened, and the Supreme Arbiter has just offered you the biggest instalment yet and you lit up like a lamp."

He did not deny it. He had felt it light.

"So: no," she said. "You don't get to pay. You get to *help*. You get to get up tomorrow and do the next useful thing and then the one after that, for as long as it takes, with people who know exactly what you are, which is the worst sentence I can think of and the only one that's any use to anybody." Her mouth twisted. "That's not mercy. Don't write it down as mercy."

"What should I write it down as?"

"Work," said Lyra.

And she put her hand in his — the glass one, the left, the cold one with the ringing in it, because the right one was easy and she had never once in her life taken the easy one — and stood beside him looking east, and did not say anything else.

---

They buried nothing and marked nothing, because there was nothing to bury.

Mags went up the envelope at noon with a palm and a needle. Pennick drew the number-three tank down and sealed the clip and pronounced it good for perhaps forty miles, and then said the thing he had been not saying for a day and a half.

"We need a tank, Captain. A whole one. And a yard with a drift-bench and somebody who knows how to tune one, and there is one place on this continent inside forty miles of where we can get to."

Vane stood at the wheel for a while.

"Say it," he said.

"Port Cantabile," said Pennick.

The Rust Coast. Oona Salt, and four hundred and eighty sovereigns, and a debt called in. The Salt Stair, and a dealer who had sold him a bottle with a name on the label. And somewhere on that coast, or off it, a woman with eyes gone violet who had come in off the water in the evening, which nobody does, and asked very politely which captain it was who had cut his tow over the trench.

"Right," said Vane.

He said it in a perfectly ordinary voice, and went below, and stood in the hold among the crates in the dark for a while with his hand flat on the third one.

Then he came up and set a course west-south-west, for the coast, which he had sworn for five years he would never do.

---

That night Kaelen went down into the hold with a lantern and a nub of charcoal and read his own list on the beam, which had got longer.

*Tamsin Reed*
*Aldric Vance*
*Davin Hale*
*Tam — (no surname. ASK.)*
*Orla Hale*
*Hob Wendle*
*Ada Pellow*
*8 men at the pumps — Edders. Ruhl. Caine. Ott. Sennet. Vey. Marrow. Hale, B. (no relation)*
*Halder Wick*
*Coralie — (no surname. There is no one to ask.)*

He had added the eight from the carriage file, which had been exactly where Vaelen said it would be, and he had thought about that for a long time afterward and had still written them down.

He sat with his back against the third crate with the murmur going on around him, four hundred and thirty-two people talking in their sleep, and through the glass — faint, four days and a hundred miles behind them, with a hole in it, still going — he could hear the Chorister of Growth losing the tune of a five-note lullaby and starting again from the beginning.

*Somebody has to hold it.*

He had been pushing the thought away for four days, the way you push a dog off a bed.

He stopped pushing it, and looked at it, and found it looking back.

Then Kaelen Vance picked up the charcoal, and under the last name on the list, in the hold of a stolen skyship flying west toward a coast he had never seen, he wrote two words that were not a name and were not a number, and that he could not have explained to anybody aboard:

*Not yet.*

---
---

## ECHO I
### The Thread Where She Answered

> *A loom has no opinion about the cloth. That is the first thing they teach you at the Sandglass, and it is a lie told to children, and they know it is a lie, and they teach it anyway, because the alternative is to tell a nineteen-year-old that he is going to spend his life watching.*
>
> — Ilias Marren, exile of the Loom-Wardens

---

You will want to know who is talking.

My name is Ilias Marren and I was a Warden of the Loom at the Sandglass Bazaar until they put me out of it, and I am not in this story yet. I will come into it in the second book, in a glass city that is living the same twenty-four seconds over and over, and you will like me, I think, for a while.

I am going to show you a thread that was not taken.

Understand what that means before you go any further. This did not happen. It also did not *un*happen; nothing does. Every road not taken is still standing out there in the dark with the lamps lit and people walking up and down it, and the only difference between that road and this one is that you are on this one, and the thing that decides which road you are on is almost always small, and almost always a Tuesday.

In the thread you have just read, Lyra Ashgrove went out to the cooperage wall on the forty-first night, and sat on her stone, and told a monster about a child's hands.

On the forty-second night — the night Kaelen Vance came to her door and hummed his note in her kitchen and she recognized it — she did not go.

That is the whole of the difference. One night.

Here is the thread where she went.

---

She goes out at the dead hour, because she always does, and she sits down with her back to the thorn, and it says *there's my lark*, and she says *hello, Da*, and it asks about her day.

And on this night — because the difference between the threads is one night, and this thread is one night further along than the one you know — it asks her something it has not asked before.

*Come through,* it says. *Lark. There's a gap down by the cooperage drain, where the water goes under. It's not even a squeeze. Come through and sit with me a bit. It's cold out here and I've been on my own two years.*

And she says no.

She says no eleven times over the next three hours, which I want you to know, because it matters later and nobody in that thread ever finds out. She is not weak and she is not a fool; she is a thirty-three-year-old woman who has been doing triage for two years with nothing in the account, and the thing on the other side of the thorn has her father's voice and has been practising for seven hundred and thirty nights.

On the twelfth time, it says: *All right, lark. Then I'll come through to you.*

And she hears the thorn move.

And she is so frightened — not of dying; of *that*, of the shape of it coming through the hedge into Oakhaven where the Carders are, where Bettany is, where ninety-one children are asleep — that she stands up and says the only thing she can think of that will keep it out there.

*Don't. I'm coming.*

---

I have watched that moment four hundred and eleven times.

I want to be precise with you, because precision is the only decency available to me: in two hundred and six of those threads she stays on her side of the thorn. In a hundred and ninety-five she does not. The variable is not courage. It is whether she slept the night before, and whether the Carder boy's roots came back that afternoon, and whether old Tam was humming at the Weavers' Cross as she passed, which is a thing that depends on the state of his knees, which depends on the weather in a week that is now three years gone.

That is what a thread is. You are a soup, and a thing in the dark is patient, and some nights you have had enough sleep.

---

She goes through the drain on her hands and knees.

It takes eleven seconds. I am not going to describe what is waiting, because you have met it now, and because there is a difference between dread and cruelty and I am trying to stay on the right side of it.

I will tell you the part that matters.

It does not kill her. It does not, in any sense you would recognize, *hurt* her. It takes four minutes, and what comes back through the drain into Oakhaven at the end of those four minutes walks up Cooper's Row in Lyra Ashgrove's boots and lets itself into Lyra Ashgrove's house and lies down in her bed, and in the morning it gets up and goes to look at Pim Carder's hands.

And it is very good.

That is the thing nobody in that thread ever works out, and I have watched them not work it out four hundred and eleven times. It has her whole voice. It has two years of her nights — every patient, every fear, every small stupid thing she told it on a stone behind a cooperage. It knows the falling fifth. It knows that Nell Carder takes two sugars and that Bettany's hip hurts in the wet and that the Tanner cousins are not speaking. It is kind, because kindness is a sound and it has heard a great deal of it.

It cannot sing the Root note into a clay jar and make a baby breathe. But in a town where the Cantor has been running on empty for two years, nobody notices for nine days that the mending has stopped working, and by then it does not matter.

---

Here is the rest of it, quickly, because you have a book to finish.

Kaelen Vance comes to her door on the forty-second night with a bore plan under his arm and a Stage Nine to tell her about, and the thing wearing her face lets him in, and he sits at her table and tells it everything — the Sequence, the coil, the phase port, the eight bolts, the forty seconds — and it listens with her eyes, and asks the questions she would have asked, and at the door he hums his note and it does not recognize it, because there is nothing in there that was ever eight years old on a stile.

He goes away believing he has a plan and an ally.

On Hearthday they take her down four hundred feet in a white shift and put a brass collar round her throat, and Kaelen Vance kneels in front of her and threads a wire through a fold of skin instead of the fold of her voice, and his hands do not shake at all, because the woman on the stool is perfectly calm and that steadies him.

Then the Hold begins, and the thing in the stone draws in and listens.

And the thing on the stool, which eats voices and has never in its existence had the slightest reason to care what a dying god does with its law, holds the note.

It holds it beautifully. It holds it for four hours and nineteen minutes with blood on its collar, and nobody shouts *sing it down*, because the engineer at the collar walk is waiting for a signal from an ally who is not there, and when he finally understands — at four hours and eleven minutes, by her face, by something behind her eyes that is not fear — he goes for the drum anyway, and gets five bolts out of eight before Factor Vosk's Legionnaires put him on the boards.

The Root is relieved.

It has been singing a five-note lullaby for ten thousand years and a voice outside it holds its note steady for four hours and nineteen minutes, and it believes — the way the Light believed — that the thing it was promised has come.

It lets go.

The Greening in that thread is not four miles across. It is the whole Resonant Reach, from the Flume to the Greenroot road to the edge of the Shifting Dunes, in eleven days: the second Fracture, Growth following Light, and the Arbiterium's grey folder vindicated at a scale that makes a Supreme Arbiter weep with relief in a locked room at the Organum, which I have also watched, and which I would not have believed if I had only heard about it.

Kaelen Vance dies under the purge, in the vault, with his father's voice coming out of the speaking-tube counting down from sixty.

Vane cuts the mast-line at the quarry and runs, because there is nobody at the North Gate worth going back for, and lives another nine years, and drinks, and dies on the Rust Coast of something ordinary.

Sable reads twenty names a night in a drowned plaza until the Office finds her in the spring.

And the thing wearing Lyra Ashgrove goes on walking around inside the Greening, singing, for a very long time, because there is a great deal to eat.

---

Four hundred and eleven times.

I have a reason for showing you this one, and it is not the one you think. It is not *look how lucky they are*. Luck is not a thing I have ever been able to find in the cloth; I have looked.

It is this.

In the thread you are reading — the one where she did not go out on the forty-second night — she is alive at the end of Book One, and so are four hundred and thirty-two people in a hold, and so is a child in a crate who is frightened of the dark. And every single one of those outcomes rests on a woman recognizing a low note with a ring in its heart, in her own kitchen, at the end of a day in which she had already spent everything she had.

That is all. That is the whole margin. A tired woman heard something, and did not go out.

I have watched a great many threads, and I can tell you with some authority that the world is never saved by the people who are fresh.

---

One more thing, and then I will leave you to the second book.

You should know that I am going to be in this story, and that I am going to be useful, and that I am going to like them — the Cantor especially, who reminds me of somebody, and the archivist, who does what I do and has not yet discovered what it costs.

And you should know that in every thread where I say nothing at the right moment, they reach the Organum together and all of them are taken into the Hush, and are a very long time being digested, and the world ends in the way that does not have a sound.

In one thread, somebody sells them at the gate.

Some of them live.

I have looked at four hundred and eleven of these, and I have not yet found a third option, and I am still looking, and I would like you to remember, when we get there, that I was looking.

*— the Tide-Weaver*

---
---

**END OF BOOK ONE: *THE SILENCED PROVINCE***

*Book Two:* The Great Discord *— the Rust Coast and a woman with violet eyes; Sol-Luminary and two sisters; the glass city living the same twenty-four seconds; the Valley of Velocity and an undelivered writ; the Gasket Strike; and a Synod that begins, for the first time in four hundred years, to turn on itself.*
